You want to understand the article "The Codebook Utilization Scam," but you find concepts popping up one after another—Embedding, gradient descent, Transformer, autoregressive, CTR prediction, VAE, codebook collapse…each seemingly requiring yet another article to explain.

This is that other article. And it is much longer than you expected.

This article covers the **complete knowledge chain** from the most basic machine learning all the way to vector quantization. It is not a brief list of concepts, but **truly teaches you every single concept**—from intuitive analogies to mathematical formulas to concrete numerical examples, and then to their specific applications in RQ-VAE / recommendation systems. Arranged in dependency order, it guarantees that after reading you can seamlessly transition to the target article.

## Part 1: Machine Learning Basics—Letting Machines Learn Patterns from Data

### 1.1 What Is Machine Learning

Traditional programming: humans write rules, computers execute.

Machine learning: humans provide data and objectives, computers find rules on their own.

```
Traditional programming: Input + Rules → Output
Machine learning:        Input + Output → Rules (Model)
```

This process of "finding rules" is called **Training**. The discovered rules are stored in a **Model**. Using the discovered rules to process new data is called **Inference**.

**A concrete example**:

Suppose you are an algorithm engineer at an advertising platform and want to predict "will User A click on Ad B?"

- Traditional approach: manually write rules—"if user age is 18–25 and ad category is gaming, then show it." The problem is that you can never write enough rules, and different users have different preferences.
- ML approach: collect historical data (out of 1 million impressions, which ones were clicked and which were not), and let the model discover the patterns of "what kind of user–ad combinations tend to get clicked."

### 1.2 Three Learning Paradigms

| Paradigm | Input | Signal | Typical Applications |
|------|------|------|---------|
| **Supervised Learning** | Features x + Labels y | Every data point has a correct answer | CTR prediction, image classification |
| **Unsupervised Learning** | Only features x | No labels, discover data structure | Clustering, dimensionality reduction, K-Means |
| **Self-Supervised Learning** | Data itself constructs supervisory signals | Labels generated from the data itself | Word2Vec, BERT, contrastive learning |

<Callout type="info" title="The Importance of Self-Supervised Learning">
Self-supervised learning is a prerequisite for understanding VQ-VAE and contrastive learning later on. Its core idea is: no manual annotation is needed; training signals are created by leveraging the data's own structure.

For example, Word2Vec: use "surrounding words" to predict the "center word." The labels are not human-annotated but come from the text's own structure.

Another example is VQ-VAE's reconstruction loss: encode the input and then decode it; the training signal is "the reconstructed output should be the same as the original input." No external labels are needed.
</Callout>

### 1.3 Supervised Learning in Detail

The core of supervised learning: show the machine a large number of "input–correct answer" pairs so it learns to predict the correct answer from the input.

| Task Type | Input | Output | Example |
|----------|------|------|------|
| **Classification** | Feature vector | Discrete category | Will this ad be clicked? (Yes/No) |
| **Regression** | Feature vector | Continuous value | How many units of this product will sell tomorrow? (156) |
| **Ranking** | Candidate set | Ordered list | In what order should these 10 ads be displayed? |

In recommendation/advertising systems, **CTR prediction** is a typical binary classification problem: given user features + ad features + context features, predict "will this user click this ad?" The output is a probability value between 0 and 1.

**What does the actual data look like?**

```python
# A single training sample
features = {
    "user_age": 25,
    "user_gender": "male",
    "user_history_categories": ["gaming", "electronics", "sports"],
    "ad_category": "gaming",
    "ad_price": 68.0,
    "hour_of_day": 22,
    "device": "iPhone",
    "city": "Beijing"
}
label = 1  # This user actually clicked this ad
```

### 1.4 Feature Engineering: Why It Used to Be the Most Important Thing

Before deep learning, model capacity was limited (only capable of linear or shallow nonlinear transformations), so a data scientist's core job was **manually constructing features**:

```python
# Raw features
user_age = 25
ad_price = 68.0

# Manually constructed cross features
age_x_price = user_age * ad_price  # 1700
is_young_and_cheap = (user_age < 30) and (ad_price < 100)  # True
hour_bucket = "night" if hour > 20 else "day"  # "night"
```

**Why did deep learning reduce the need for feature engineering?** 

Because neural networks can automatically learn feature interactions. After DeepFM, you only need to feed in raw features and the model learns which combinations are useful on its own. But **feature engineering has not completely disappeared**—industrial systems still manually add some high-value features (e.g., "the item's average CTR over the past 7 days").

### 1.5 Loss Functions: Measuring "How Well the Model Has Learned"

The model predicts a value ŷ, and the true value is y. The **Loss Function** measures the gap between the two.

#### Mean Squared Error (MSE) — Regression Tasks

```
MSE = (1/N) * sum((y_i - y_hat_i)^2)
```

Intuition: the farther the predicted value is from the true value, the heavier the penalty (because it's squared).

Numerical example:
```python
# Predicting sales for 3 products
y_true = [100, 200, 150]
y_pred = [110, 180, 160]

mse = ((100-110)^2 + (200-180)^2 + (150-160)^2) / 3
    = (100 + 400 + 100) / 3
    = 200
```

#### Cross-Entropy — Classification Tasks

Binary cross-entropy:

```
BCE = -(1/N) * sum(y_i * log(y_hat_i) + (1-y_i) * log(1-y_hat_i))
```

Intuition: if the true label is 1 (clicked), the closer the model's predicted probability is to 1, the smaller the loss. If the prediction is 0.01 (almost certain no click) but the user actually clicked, the penalty is −log(0.01) = 4.6, which is very large.

Numerical example:
```python
# Ground truth: clicked (y=1)
# Model predicted probability
p = 0.9   → loss = -log(0.9) = 0.105  (very small, model was right)
p = 0.5   → loss = -log(0.5) = 0.693  (uncertain)
p = 0.01  → loss = -log(0.01) = 4.605 (terribly wrong)
```

<Callout type="warning" title="Why CTR Prediction Uses Cross-Entropy Instead of MSE">
CTR prediction is a classification problem (click / no click), not a regression problem. Training a classification model with MSE leads to:
1. Gradients approaching zero when the probability is near 0 or 1 (sigmoid saturation region), making training very slow
2. Poor probability calibration—a model output of 0.3 cannot be interpreted as "a 30% probability of clicking"

Cross-entropy + sigmoid output layer is the standard configuration, ensuring the output is a calibrated probability.
</Callout>

#### Reconstruction Loss — Autoencoders / VQ-VAE

```
L_reconstruct = ||x - Decoder(Encoder(x))||^2
```

Intuition: compress the input and then reconstruct it; see how similar the reconstruction is to the original. In VQ-VAE, this loss determines whether the codebook has preserved enough information.

### 1.6 Gradient Descent: How to Make the Loss Smaller

<TechGraph src="/images/posts/rqvae-prerequisite-knowledge/05-gradient-descent.svg" title="Core Mechanism of Gradient Descent" caption="Take a step in the direction opposite to the gradient—the foundation of all deep learning training" />

Imagine you are standing on a mountain (your current loss value), blindfolded (you don't know the global terrain), and want to reach the lowest valley (minimum loss).

The **gradient** tells you "which direction is the steepest uphill from where you're standing right now." **Gradient descent** is simply "take a step in the opposite direction of the gradient"—always walk downhill.

```
param_new = param_old - learning_rate * gradient
```

#### Learning Rate

How big each step is. This is the most important hyperparameter in model training.

```python
lr = 0.1    # Too large: overshoots the minimum, oscillates back and forth, loss increases instead of decreasing
lr = 0.001  # Moderate: steady descent
lr = 0.00001 # Too small: descends extremely slowly, may get stuck in a local minimum
```

<Callout type="info" title="Learning Rate and Its Relationship to RQ-VAE">
Later you will see that during RQ-VAE training, "limiting the Tokenizer's learning rate" is used to prevent drift—this leverages exactly this principle: small learning rate = small parameter changes = stable SIDs.

If the Tokenizer's learning rate is as large as the downstream model's, the codebook changes drastically every step, and previously assigned SIDs become invalid.
</Callout>

#### Learning Rate Schedule

In practice, a fixed learning rate is not used; instead it is dynamically adjusted:

```python
# 1. Warmup: linearly increase lr from a very small value to the target lr (prevent overly large gradients at the start)
# First 1000 steps: lr = step/1000 * target_lr

# 2. Cosine Decay: after warmup, decay following a cosine curve
# lr = target_lr * 0.5 * (1 + cos(pi * step / total_steps))

# 3. Step Decay: multiply by 0.1 every N steps
# Every 30 epochs: lr = lr * 0.1
```

**Why is Warmup needed?** At the beginning of training, parameters are random and gradient directions are inaccurate. If you start with a large learning rate right away, you might push the parameters into a very bad region. Start with a small learning rate to "probe," and accelerate once gradient directions stabilize.

#### Optimizer Evolution

```python
# SGD: the most basic
theta = theta - lr * gradient

# SGD + Momentum: adds momentum, like a ball rolling downhill with inertia
v = 0.9 * v + gradient
theta = theta - lr * v

# Adam: adaptive learning rate, each parameter has a different effective learning rate
# Combines Momentum (first moment) and RMSProp (second moment)
m = beta1 * m + (1-beta1) * gradient          # First moment estimate (direction)
v = beta2 * v + (1-beta2) * gradient^2        # Second moment estimate (step size scaling)
theta = theta - lr * m / (sqrt(v) + epsilon)

# AdamW: Adam + weight decay (correct implementation of L2 regularization)
# The default choice for deep learning in industry
```

<Callout type="tip" title="Practical Advice">
- **Recommendation system CTR models**: typically use Adam or AdaGrad (sparse-feature friendly)
- **Transformer training**: AdamW + cosine schedule + warmup is the standard setup
- **VQ-VAE codebook**: does not use gradient updates, but rather EMA (Exponential Moving Average)—more on this later
</Callout>

### 1.7 Batch Training: Why Not Learn One Sample at a Time

If you update parameters after every single sample (pure SGD), the direction will be very unstable—a single noisy sample can push the parameters off course.

**Mini-Batch training**: look at a batch of samples at a time (e.g., 256), compute the average gradient over the batch, then update.

```python
# Mini-Batch training loop
for epoch in range(100):
    for batch in dataloader(train_data, batch_size=256):
        predictions = model(batch.features)       # Forward pass
        loss = cross_entropy(predictions, batch.labels)  # Compute loss
        gradients = backward(loss)                 # Backpropagation to compute gradients
        optimizer.step(gradients)                  # Update parameters
```

**Effects of Batch Size**:

| Batch Size | Gradient Stability | GPU Utilization | Generalization | Memory Usage |
|-----------|-----------|----------|---------|---------|
| 1 (pure SGD) | Extremely unstable | Extremely low | Good (noise = regularization) | Minimal |
| 32–256 | Moderate | Fairly high | Good | Moderate |
| 1024–4096 | Very stable | Very high | May deteriorate | Large |
| Full dataset | Most stable | Highest | Poor (no randomness) | May OOM |

<Callout type="info" title="The Relationship Between Batch Size and RQ-VAE">
One of RQOPQ's core advantages is that it "can be trained with batches"—traditional K-means requires loading all data at once for clustering (Full Batch), and when the data is large (hundreds of millions of items) it simply won't fit in memory. RQOPQ trains rotation matrices via gradient descent and can use Mini-Batch, making it scalable to industrial-scale data volumes.
</Callout>

### 1.8 Overfitting and Regularization

**Overfitting**: The model performs perfectly on the training set but poorly on new data. It's like a student who memorized all the exam answers but can't solve any new problems—it memorized the noise in the data rather than the underlying patterns.

**How to detect overfitting?**

```
Training loss: 0.01  (extremely low, nearly perfect fit)
Validation loss: 0.85 (very high, helpless on new data)
The larger the gap → the more severe the overfitting
```

**Bias-Variance Tradeoff**:

- **High Bias (Underfitting)**: The model is too simple and can't even fit the training set well. For example, using a linear model to fit curved data.
- **High Variance (Overfitting)**: The model is too complex, perfectly fitting the training set but sensitive to new data. For example, using a 100th-degree polynomial to fit 10 points.

**Regularization Strategies in Detail**:

#### Dropout

During training, randomly shut off some neurons (set their outputs to 0), forcing the model not to rely on any single pathway.

```python
# Dropout pseudocode
def dropout(x, rate=0.5, training=True):
    if not training:
        return x  # No dropout during inference
    mask = random_bernoulli(shape=x.shape, p=1-rate)
    return x * mask / (1-rate)  # Divide by (1-rate) to keep the expected value unchanged
```

Intuition: each training step uses only "part of the network," which is equivalent to training an ensemble of many sub-networks.

#### L2 Regularization (Weight Decay)

Penalizes large parameter values, encouraging the model to use "simple" rules.

```
Total_Loss = Original_Loss + lambda * sum(w_i^2)
```

When lambda = 0.01, if a weight w = 10, the additional penalty is 0.01 * 100 = 1.0. This forces the model to prefer many small weights rather than a few large ones, reducing model complexity.

#### L1 Regularization (Lasso)

```
Total_Loss = Original_Loss + lambda * sum(|w_i|)
```

The difference from L2: L1 tends to drive parameters to exactly 0 (sparsity), while L2 tends to make parameters small but not zero. L1 naturally performs feature selection—weights corresponding to unimportant features get "killed off."

#### Early Stopping

Stop training when validation set performance stops improving:

```python
best_val_loss = float('inf')
patience = 10
no_improve_count = 0

for epoch in range(1000):
    train(model)
    val_loss = evaluate(model, val_set)
    if val_loss < best_val_loss:
        best_val_loss = val_loss
        save(model)
        no_improve_count = 0
    else:
        no_improve_count += 1
        if no_improve_count >= patience:
            break  # Stop! Training further will only overfit
```

### 1.9 Embedding: Turning Discrete IDs into Continuous Vectors

This is the foundational concept of the entire recommendation system, and the direct prerequisite for understanding Semantic IDs.

**Problem**: Suppose you have 1 million users, each with an ID (1 to 1,000,000). You can't feed ID numbers directly to the model—"User 999,999" isn't 999,998 times greater than "User 1." ID numbers have no numerical meaning.

**The problem with One-Hot encoding**:

```python
# One-hot for 1 million users
user_1 = [1, 0, 0, 0, ..., 0]  # 1 million dimensions, only one 1
user_2 = [0, 1, 0, 0, ..., 0]  # 1 million dimensions, only one 1
# Problem 1: Dimension explosion (1 million dimensions!)
# Problem 2: The distance between any two users is always sqrt(2), no way to distinguish similarity
# Problem 3: No meaningful vector operations possible
```

**The Embedding solution**: Assign each ID a learnable low-dimensional dense vector.

```python
# Embedding Table: a 1 million × 64 matrix
embedding_table = nn.Embedding(num_embeddings=1000000, embedding_dim=64)

# Lookup
user_12345_vec = embedding_table[12345]  # → 64-dim vector [0.23, -0.45, 0.87, ..., 0.12]
user_67890_vec = embedding_table[67890]  # → 64-dim vector [-0.11, 0.56, 0.34, ..., -0.78]
```

**The Embedding training process**:

Initially, each ID's vector is random. During model training, through backpropagation, gradients flow into the looked-up row of the Embedding table and update that row's parameters. After extensive training:

- Similar users (with similar behavior patterns) have Embedding vectors that are close in space
- Dissimilar users have vectors that are far apart
- Each dimension of the vector implicitly encodes some kind of "semantics" (though humans may not understand the specific meaning)

```python
# Effect after training
cosine_similarity(user_A, user_B) = 0.92  # Both A and B love watching sports
cosine_similarity(user_A, user_C) = 0.15  # A loves sports, C loves beauty/cosmetics
```

<Callout type="warning" title="The Core Limitation of Embeddings — This Is Why Semantic IDs Exist">
Each ID's Embedding is an **independent parameter**. This means:

1. **Cold start**: A new item's Embedding is initialized as a random vector and must go through extensive impressions and clicks before it can be properly trained. Until then, it can't be effectively recommended.
2. **No generalization**: Two items with nearly identical content (e.g., the same shoe in different colors) have completely independent ID Embeddings that learn nothing from each other.
3. **Vocabulary explosion**: Million-level items × 64 dimensions = 256 million parameters, just for the single item ID feature. Billion-level items would require a 64-billion-parameter Embedding table.
4. **No Scaling Law**: Increasing model parameters cannot improve item ID representation quality—because each ID's parameters are already independent.

**Semantic IDs aim to solve exactly these problems**: instead of representing each item with independent parameters, they use combinations of a finite set of codewords—new items with similar content automatically get similar SIDs, enabling natural generalization.
</Callout>

### 1.10 Evaluation Metrics

After training a model, how do you evaluate whether it's good?

#### Classification Metrics

```python
# Confusion Matrix
#                Predicted Pos  Predicted Neg
# Actual Pos        TP              FN
# Actual Neg        FP              TN

Accuracy = (TP + TN) / (TP + TN + FP + FN)  # Overall accuracy
Precision = TP / (TP + FP)   # Of those predicted positive, how many are correct
Recall = TP / (TP + FN)      # Of those actually positive, how many were found
F1 = 2 * Precision * Recall / (Precision + Recall)  # Harmonic mean of P and R
```

#### AUC-ROC — The Core Evaluation Metric for CTR Models

AUC (Area Under ROC Curve): Among all positive-negative sample pairs, the probability that the model ranks the positive sample ahead of the negative sample.

```
AUC = 0.5  → Random guessing (model is useless)
AUC = 0.7  → Decent
AUC = 0.8  → Good
AUC = 1.0  → Perfect (all positive samples score higher than all negative samples)
```

**Why use AUC instead of Accuracy?** Because CTR data is extremely imbalanced (click-through rate is usually &lt; 5%). If the model always predicts "no click," Accuracy = 95%, which looks high but is completely useless. AUC is not affected by class imbalance.

#### GAUC — More Commonly Used in Industrial Recommendation Systems

```
GAUC = sum(AUC_user_i * impression_user_i) / sum(impression_user_i)
```

Compute AUC per user group and then take a weighted average. Because different users have different CTR baselines (heavy users have high CTR, light users have low CTR), GAUC eliminates this discrepancy.

### 1.11 Train/Val/Test Split

```python
# Time-based split (standard practice in recommendation systems)
train_data = data[data.date < "2026-06-01"]      # Train on historical data
val_data = data[data.date == "2026-06-01"]       # Validate on the next day
test_data = data[data.date == "2026-06-02"]      # Test on the day after that

# Why split by time instead of randomly?
# Because recommendation systems have temporal dependencies—you can't train today's model
# with tomorrow's data (information leakage).
```

### 1.12 Connection to the Target Article

| Concept You Just Learned | How It Manifests in RQ-VAE Training |
|--------------------------|------------------------------------|
| Loss function | VQ-VAE has three loss terms pulling against each other (reconstruction + VQ + commitment) |
| Learning rate | Limiting the Tokenizer LR to prevent SID drift |
| Batch training | One of RQOPQ's advantages is supporting Mini-Batch |
| Overfitting/regularization | Codebook collapse is in some sense "underfitting"—a large number of codewords go unused |
| Embedding | SIDs exist precisely to solve the four major limitations of Embedding tables |
| AUC/GAUC | Evaluating the gains that SIDs bring to CTR models |

## Part 2: Deep Learning — Letting the Model Automatically Discover Complex Patterns

### 2.1 From Linear Models to Neural Networks

Linear model: `y = w1*x1 + w2*x2 + ... + b`. Can only fit linear relationships.

But in the real world, "whether a user clicks an ad" depends on **complex nonlinear interactions** among features—nonlinear combinations of age × interest × time × context.

**Universal Approximation Theorem**: A single hidden-layer neural network of sufficient width can approximate any continuous function. In other words, neural networks can theoretically learn any pattern—given enough data and compute.

<TechGraph src="/images/posts/rqvae-prerequisite-knowledge/12-neural-network-layers.svg" title="Layered Structure of a Neural Network" caption="Forward propagation computes the output + backpropagation computes gradients — this is the training mechanism behind all deep learning" />

### 2.2 Basic Structure of a Neural Network

```python
# A 3-layer MLP (Multi-Layer Perceptron)
class MLP:
    def __init__(self):
        self.W1 = random_matrix(input_dim, 256)   # Layer 1 weights
        self.W2 = random_matrix(256, 128)          # Layer 2 weights
        self.W3 = random_matrix(128, 1)            # Output layer weights

    def forward(self, x):
        z1 = x @ self.W1 + self.b1        # Linear transformation
        a1 = relu(z1)                       # Nonlinear activation
        z2 = a1 @ self.W2 + self.b2        # Linear transformation
        a2 = relu(z2)                       # Nonlinear activation
        z3 = a2 @ self.W3 + self.b3        # Linear transformation
        y_hat = sigmoid(z3)                 # Output probability
        return y_hat
```

Each layer does two things:
1. **Linear transformation**: `z = W * x + b` (matrix multiplication = weighted sum + bias)
2. **Nonlinear activation**: `a = f(z)` (enables the model to fit curves, not just straight lines)

**Why is nonlinear activation needed?**

Without activation functions, the composition of multiple linear transformations is still a linear transformation:
```
z2 = W2 * (W1 * x + b1) + b2 = (W2*W1) * x + (W2*b1 + b2)
# This is equivalent to a single linear transformation! Multiple layers are pointless.
```

Activation functions break this linear superposition, enabling multi-layer networks to express nonlinear relationships.

### 2.3 Activation Functions in Detail

| Activation | Formula | Output Range | Pros | Cons |
|---------|------|---------|------|------|
| **Sigmoid** | 1/(1+e^-x) | (0, 1) | Output interpretable as probability | Vanishing gradients, non-zero-centered |
| **Tanh** | (e^x - e^-x)/(e^x + e^-x) | (-1, 1) | Zero-centered | Vanishing gradients |
| **ReLU** | max(0, x) | [0, +inf) | Fast computation, mitigates vanishing gradients | Dead neurons (gradient=0 when x&lt;0) |
| **LeakyReLU** | max(0.01x, x) | (-inf, +inf) | Solves dead neuron problem | One extra hyperparameter |
| **GELU** | x * Phi(x) | (-0.17, +inf) | Smooth, standard in NLP | Slightly slower to compute |
| **Swish** | x * sigmoid(x) | (-0.28, +inf) | Smooth, good performance | Slightly slower to compute |

```python
# ReLU: negatives become 0, positives stay unchanged
relu(-3) = 0
relu(0) = 0
relu(5) = 5

# GELU (standard in Transformers): like a smooth ReLU
# Intuition: "gates" the input probabilistically — larger values are more likely to be kept
gelu(x) = x * 0.5 * (1 + erf(x/sqrt(2)))
```

<Callout type="info" title="Choosing activation functions for different scenarios">
- **Hidden layers**: ReLU or GELU (GELU is the standard in Transformers)
- **Final layer (classification)**: Sigmoid (binary) or Softmax (multi-class)
- **Final layer (regression)**: No activation (or linear)
- **VQ-VAE Encoder output**: Typically no activation (to preserve the full continuous space)
</Callout>

### 2.4 Backpropagation: The Training Algorithm of Deep Learning

Given a loss function and a multi-layer network, how do we know how to adjust the parameters in each layer?

**Backpropagation**: Starting from the loss at the final layer, use the chain rule to compute each parameter's contribution to the loss (gradient) layer by layer going backward.

**Full derivation using a two-layer network as an example**:

```
Forward: x → z1=W1*x+b1 → a1=relu(z1) → z2=W2*a1+b2 → y_hat=sigmoid(z2) → Loss

Backward (chain rule):
dL/dW2 = dL/dy_hat * dy_hat/dz2 * dz2/dW2
       = (y_hat - y) * sigmoid'(z2) * a1

dL/dW1 = dL/dy_hat * dy_hat/dz2 * dz2/da1 * da1/dz1 * dz1/dW1
       = (y_hat - y) * sigmoid'(z2) * W2 * relu'(z1) * x
```

This is essentially the chain rule from high-school calculus `f(g(h(x)))' = f' * g' * h'` applied to multi-layer function composition.

**Numerical example**:

```python
# Simple two-layer network, input x=2, true label y=1
W1 = 0.5, b1 = 0, W2 = 0.8, b2 = 0

# Forward pass
z1 = 0.5 * 2 + 0 = 1.0
a1 = relu(1.0) = 1.0
z2 = 0.8 * 1.0 + 0 = 0.8
y_hat = sigmoid(0.8) = 0.69

# Loss (BCE)
loss = -(1*log(0.69) + 0) = 0.37

# Backpropagation
dL/dy_hat = -1/0.69 = -1.45
dy_hat/dz2 = sigmoid(0.8)*(1-sigmoid(0.8)) = 0.69*0.31 = 0.214
dL/dz2 = -1.45 * 0.214 = -0.31  (i.e., y_hat - y = 0.69 - 1 = -0.31)

dL/dW2 = dL/dz2 * dz2/dW2 = -0.31 * a1 = -0.31 * 1.0 = -0.31
dL/dW1 = dL/dz2 * dz2/da1 * da1/dz1 * dz1/dW1
       = -0.31 * W2 * relu'(1.0) * x = -0.31 * 0.8 * 1 * 2 = -0.496

# Update (lr=0.1)
W2_new = 0.8 - 0.1*(-0.31) = 0.831
W1_new = 0.5 - 0.1*(-0.496) = 0.5496
```

<Callout type="warning" title="Backpropagation breakage in VQ-VAE">
The core challenge in VQ-VAE — "argmin is not differentiable" — is fundamentally a backpropagation problem. The quantization operation is a discrete selection whose derivative is zero, so gradients cannot flow back.

Specifically: in `z_q = codebook[argmin_k ||z_e - e_k||]`, the derivative of argmin with respect to z_e does not exist. The chain rule breaks at this point.

The Straight-Through Estimator (STE) essentially "tricks" backpropagation into treating the quantization layer as an identity mapping z_q = z_e, allowing gradients to pass straight through.
</Callout>

### 2.5 Training Stability Techniques

#### Batch Normalization (BatchNorm)

Apply normalization to the output of each layer: make each feature have mean=0 and variance=1.

```python
def batch_norm(x, gamma, beta):
    mean = x.mean(dim=0)    # Mean along the batch dimension
    var = x.var(dim=0)      # Variance along the batch dimension
    x_norm = (x - mean) / sqrt(var + epsilon)  # Standardize
    return gamma * x_norm + beta  # Learnable scale and shift
```

**Why does it work?** It mitigates "internal covariate shift" — when parameters in earlier layers update, the input distribution to later layers changes, causing training instability. Normalization keeps the input distribution to each layer consistent.

#### Layer Normalization (LayerNorm)

Similar to BatchNorm, but normalizes along a different dimension:

```python
# BatchNorm: normalizes along the batch dimension (each feature across samples)
# LayerNorm: normalizes along the feature dimension (each sample across features)
def layer_norm(x, gamma, beta):
    mean = x.mean(dim=-1)   # Mean along the feature dimension
    var = x.var(dim=-1)     # Variance along the feature dimension
    x_norm = (x - mean) / sqrt(var + epsilon)
    return gamma * x_norm + beta
```

**Why does Transformer use LayerNorm instead of BatchNorm?**
- LayerNorm does not depend on batch size (works even with batch=1)
- BatchNorm is unsuitable when sequence lengths vary
- LayerNorm normalizes within each sample independently, so different samples are independent

#### Weight Initialization

```python
# Xavier initialization: suitable for sigmoid/tanh
W = random_normal(0, sqrt(2 / (fan_in + fan_out)))

# He initialization: suitable for ReLU
W = random_normal(0, sqrt(2 / fan_in))

# Why does it matter?
# Too small initialization → signal decays layer by layer during forward pass → later layers receive almost no input
# Too large initialization → signal explodes layer by layer → gradients also explode
```

#### Gradient Clipping

```python
# If the gradient norm exceeds the threshold, scale it down proportionally
if gradient_norm > max_norm:
    gradient = gradient * (max_norm / gradient_norm)
```

Prevents gradient explosion from causing excessively large parameter updates (common in RNNs and early Transformer training).

### 2.6 CNN: Spatial Feature Extraction

**Convolutional Neural Networks (CNN)**: Specifically designed for data with spatial structure (images, video frames).

The three core ideas:
1. **Local receptive field**: Only look at a small region of the image at a time (e.g., 3x3 pixels)
2. **Weight sharing**: The same filter (convolution kernel) scans the entire image — objects can be detected regardless of their position in the image
3. **Hierarchical structure**: Shallow layers detect edges/textures, middle layers detect parts, deep layers detect complete objects

```python
# Intuition behind the convolution operation
# Input image: 224x224x3 (height x width x channels)
# Convolution kernel: 3x3x3 (window size x input channels)
# Output: a single number = weighted sum of 27 pixels in the window with the kernel

# One 3x3 kernel scanning the entire image → produces one "feature map"
# 64 different kernels → 64 feature maps → output with 64 channels
```

**Applications in recommendation/ads systems**:
- Feature extraction from ad creatives (images/videos) uses pre-trained CNNs (e.g., ResNet-50)
- Extracts 2048-dimensional image embeddings
- These embeddings are fed into RQ-VAE for quantized encoding, becoming one of the input signals for Semantic IDs

### 2.7 RNN/LSTM/GRU: Sequence Modeling

**Recurrent Neural Networks (RNN)**: Handle sequential data (user behavior sequences, browsing history). Core idea: they have "memory" and can pass information from earlier steps to later ones.

```python
# Core formula of RNN
h_t = tanh(W_h * h_{t-1} + W_x * x_t + b)
# h_t: hidden state at the current timestep ("memory")
# h_{t-1}: hidden state at the previous timestep
# x_t: input at the current timestep
```

**Vanishing gradient problem**: In long sequences, gradients decay exponentially after repeated matrix multiplications, so early information contributes nearly zero gradient to later steps — the model "can't remember" things from long ago.

**LSTM (Long Short-Term Memory)**: Solves vanishing gradients through three gating mechanisms:

```python
# The three gates of LSTM
f_t = sigmoid(W_f * [h_{t-1}, x_t])   # Forget gate: what old information to discard
i_t = sigmoid(W_i * [h_{t-1}, x_t])   # Input gate: what new information to store
o_t = sigmoid(W_o * [h_{t-1}, x_t])   # Output gate: what information to output

# Cell state update
C_t = f_t * C_{t-1} + i_t * tanh(W_c * [h_{t-1}, x_t])
h_t = o_t * tanh(C_t)
```

**GRU (Gated Recurrent Unit)**: A simplified version of LSTM with only two gates (reset gate + update gate), fewer parameters, and comparable performance.

<Callout type="info" title="Why Transformer replaced LSTM">
LSTM's fatal flaw is that it **must process sequentially** — to process the 100th token, you must first process the preceding 99. This means GPU parallelism cannot be utilized.

In recommendation systems, a user's click history [item1, item2, item3, ...] is a sequence. Before 2018, LSTMs were used (e.g., DIEN); after 2018, Transformers essentially replaced LSTMs (e.g., SASRec, BST).

The generative recommendation in SID uses a Transformer (SASRec variant), not LSTM.
</Callout>

### 2.8 The Origin of the Attention Mechanism

Before Transformer, Attention already existed. It was originally used in machine translation (Bahdanau 2014).

Traditional sequence models (RNN) compress all historical information uniformly into a single fixed-length vector. But in practice, when translating "I love cats," the model should pay special attention to "cats" when translating the Chinese word for "cat", rather than "I."

**Attention** lets the model learn to "assign different weights to different positions":

```python
# Bahdanau Attention (additive attention)
# query: current decoder state
# keys/values: all encoder hidden states

scores = [score_function(query, key_i) for key_i in keys]
weights = softmax(scores)                    # Normalize to probabilities
context = sum(weight_i * value_i)            # Weighted aggregation
```

DIN (Deep Interest Network, Alibaba 2018) brought this idea into recommendation systems: use the "candidate ad" as the query and the "user's historical clicks" as keys/values — letting the model automatically find the items in the history most relevant to the current candidate.

### 2.9 Connections to the Target Article

| Concept You Just Learned | How It Appears in RQ-VAE Training |
|--------------|---------------------|
| Backpropagation chain rule | argmin breaks backpropagation → STE is needed |
| BatchNorm/LayerNorm | LayerNorm in Transformer stabilizes training |
| CNN | Ad creative images → CNN features → RQ-VAE input |
| RNN→Transformer | User behavior modeling migrated from LSTM to Self-Attention |
| Attention | Core architecture of the SID generative model |
| Vanishing gradients | Gradient decay across layers in RQ-VAE's multi-level quantization |

---

## Part 3: The Complete Evolution of Embedding Techniques

<TechGraph src="/images/posts/rqvae-prerequisite-knowledge/06-embedding-evolution.svg" title="Panorama of Embedding Technology Evolution" caption="From One-Hot to contrastive learning — four decades of representation technology evolution" />

### 3.1 Why Dedicate an Entire Part to Embedding

Embedding is the bridge connecting the "discrete world" to "continuous mathematics." In recommendation systems, almost all inputs are discrete (user IDs, item IDs, category IDs, device types…), yet all mathematical operations require continuous numerical values.

The essence of Semantic ID is **a special Embedding scheme** — representing items with combinations of discrete codewords. To understand why it's useful, you first need a deep understanding of the pros and cons of various Embedding methods.

### 3.2 Word2Vec (2013, Mikolov et al.)

**Core idea**: A word's semantics is determined by its "surrounding words" (the distributional hypothesis).

Two training modes:
- **CBOW**: Predict the center word from context. `["the", "cat", "on", "mat"] → "sat"`
- **Skip-gram**: Predict context from the center word. `"sat" → ["the", "cat", "on", "mat"]`

```python
# Core of Skip-gram training
# Given center word w_center, predict context word w_context
# Equivalent to: maximize the dot product of w_center and w_context vectors

# Positive sample: a real context pair
positive_score = dot(embedding[w_center], embedding[w_context])  # should be large

# Negative sampling: random pairing (not a real context)
negative_score = dot(embedding[w_center], embedding[w_random])   # should be small

# Loss: make positive sample score higher than negative samples
loss = -log(sigmoid(positive_score)) - sum(log(sigmoid(-negative_score)))
```

**Effect after training**:

```python
# Classic example: vector arithmetic can capture semantic relationships
king - man + woman ≈ queen
Paris - France + Japan ≈ Tokyo
```

**Word2Vec variants in recommendation systems**:

In 2016, Airbnb published "Real-time Personalization using Embeddings for Search Ranking," bringing the Word2Vec philosophy into recommendations:

- "Words" in text → "items" in user behavior sequences
- Word pairs within a "context window" → pairs of adjacently clicked items within the same session
- After training, items that are frequently clicked together will have embeddings close to each other

```python
# Airbnb-style Item2Vec
# User session: [item_A, item_B, item_C, item_D, item_E]
# Positive sample pairs with window size=2:
# (item_C, item_A), (item_C, item_B), (item_C, item_D), (item_C, item_E)

# This is essentially learning "collaborative filtering" signals — items frequently clicked together should be similar
```

<Callout type="info" title="Limitations of Word2Vec">
1. **Static Embedding**: Each word has only one vector. "Apple" has the same vector in "eat an apple" and "Apple phone."
2. **No context awareness**: Cannot dynamically adjust based on the sentence.
3. **Shallow model**: Only one linear transformation layer, limited expressiveness.
4. **Window limitation**: Can only learn local co-occurrence relationships, unable to capture long-range dependencies.

These limitations gave rise to the next generation: BERT.
</Callout>

### 3.3 GloVe (2014, Stanford)

**Core idea**: Use statistical information from word-word co-occurrence matrices to train word vectors.

Differences from Word2Vec:
- Word2Vec is "local window prediction" (one center word predicts surrounding words)
- GloVe is "global statistics fitting" (make the dot product of vectors equal the log of global co-occurrence frequency)

```python
# GloVe's objective function
# X_ij = co-occurrence count of word i and word j across the entire corpus
# Goal: learn vectors w_i, w_j such that
# dot(w_i, w_j) + bias_i + bias_j ≈ log(X_ij)
```

In practice, GloVe and Word2Vec perform comparably, but GloVe's training is more parallelizable. It is used less in recommendation systems because item interaction matrices are much larger than text co-occurrence matrices.

### 3.4 ELMo (2018, Allen AI)

**Breakthrough**: The first successful context-dependent Embedding.

Uses a bidirectional LSTM to encode the entire sentence, so the same word gets different vectors in different contexts.

```python
# The ELMo vector for "apple" in "The apple is sweet" ≠ the vector in "Apple released an iPhone"
# Because the LSTM encodes contextual information into the representation at each position
```

But ELMo is still based on LSTM — slow to train and limited on long texts. BERT swept everything by the end of the same year.

### 3.5 BERT (2018, Google)

**Revolutionary breakthrough**: Using the Transformer encoder + large-scale pre-training, it achieved SOTA on 11 NLP tasks in one shot.

**Pre-training tasks**:

```python
# Task 1: Masked Language Model (MLM)
# Randomly mask 15% of tokens, let the model predict the masked words
Input: "The [MASK] sat on the mat"
Target: predict [MASK] should be "cat"

# Task 2: Next Sentence Prediction (NSP)
# Given two sentences, determine if the second follows the first
Input: [CLS] "The cat sat" [SEP] "on the mat" [SEP]
Target: IsNext = True
```

**Core advantages of BERT**:

1. **Bidirectional context**: Every position can see all positions before and after it (via Self-Attention)
2. **Pre-train–fine-tune paradigm**: Pre-train on large-scale unlabeled text, then fine-tune on small datasets
3. **Transfer Learning**: One pre-trained model adapts to all downstream tasks

**BERT's applications in recommendations**:

```python
# Approach 1: Directly use BERT to encode item text descriptions
item_text = "Nike Air Max Running Shoes Men's Black Air Cushion Shock Absorption"
item_embedding = BERT(item_text)  # 768-dimensional vector

# Approach 2: BERT4Rec — treat behavior sequences as "sentences"
# Input: the sequence of items a user clicked
# Training: Masked Item Prediction (randomly mask items in the sequence and predict the masked ones)
# Exactly the same idea as BERT's MLM training
```

### 3.6 Sentence-BERT and Semantic Retrieval

When BERT encodes the similarity of two sentences, it needs to concatenate both sentences and pass them through the Transformer once, with O(n^2) complexity. This is impractical for retrieval scenarios that require comparing millions of pairs.

**Sentence-BERT (2019)**: Independently encode each sentence into a vector, then compare using cosine similarity:

```python
# Sentence-BERT
vec_A = SentenceBERT("These shoes are very comfortable")    # 768-dim
vec_B = SentenceBERT("Running shoes have high comfort")     # 768-dim
similarity = cosine(vec_A, vec_B)         # 0.87 (semantically close)

# This makes semantic retrieval over massive document collections possible:
# 1. Offline: encode all documents to get vectors
# 2. Online: encode the query to get a vector
# 3. ANN retrieval for the most similar document vectors
```

This is the direct predecessor of the "two-tower model" in recommendation systems — replacing text with user and item features.

### 3.7 Contrastive Learning

<TechGraph src="/images/posts/rqvae-prerequisite-knowledge/09-contrastive-learning.svg" title="Core Mechanism of Contrastive Learning" caption="Pull positives closer, push negatives apart — the core paradigm of self-supervised learning" />

Contrastive learning is the most important self-supervised learning paradigm since 2020. The core idea is extremely simple: **make similar things close and dissimilar things far apart**.

#### SimCLR (2020, Google)

```python
# SimCLR's approach on images
# 1. Apply two different data augmentations to the same image to get two "views"
view_1 = augment(image, [crop, color_jitter])  # random crop + color jitter
view_2 = augment(image, [crop, blur])          # random crop + blur

# 2. Both views pass through an encoder to get vectors
z_1 = encoder(view_1)  # positive sample
z_2 = encoder(view_2)  # positive sample

# 3. Two views of the same image are positives; other images in the batch are negatives
# InfoNCE Loss:
loss = -log(exp(sim(z_1, z_2)/tau) / sum(exp(sim(z_1, z_neg)/tau)))
# tau is the temperature parameter, controlling the sharpness of the distribution
```

#### MoCo (2020, Meta)

SimCLR requires large batches (4096–8192) to have enough negative samples. MoCo uses a "momentum queue" to maintain a large pool of negatives:

```python
# MoCo: maintain a negative sample queue with a momentum encoder
# query encoder: updated via normal gradients
# key encoder: momentum update (theta_k = 0.999 * theta_k + 0.001 * theta_q)
# queue: maintains the most recent 65536 keys as negative samples
```

#### CLIP (2021, OpenAI)

Cross-modal contrastive learning: align images and text into the same space.

```python
# CLIP: image-text contrastive learning
# Positive samples: real (image, caption) pairs
# Negative samples: incorrect image-text pairings within the batch

image_features = image_encoder(image)     # ViT or ResNet
text_features = text_encoder(text)        # Transformer

# Symmetric cross-entropy
logits = image_features @ text_features.T / temperature
labels = arange(batch_size)  # diagonal entries are positive samples
loss = (cross_entropy(logits, labels) + cross_entropy(logits.T, labels)) / 2
```

**Direct applications of CLIP in RQ-VAE/SID**:
- OneRec (Kuaishou 2024) uses CLIP-level multi-modal pre-training to obtain item embeddings
- These embeddings serve as input to the RQ-VAE encoder
- RQ-VAE quantizes the continuous CLIP embeddings into discrete SIDs

#### InfoNCE Loss in Detail

InfoNCE is the standard loss function for contrastive learning. Understanding it is important for later understanding VQ-VAE training:

```python
# InfoNCE Loss
# Given anchor (query), positive (match), negatives (non-matches)
def info_nce_loss(anchor, positive, negatives, temperature=0.07):
    # Positive sample similarity
    pos_sim = cosine_similarity(anchor, positive) / temperature
    # Negative sample similarities
    neg_sims = [cosine_similarity(anchor, neg) / temperature for neg in negatives]
    # Softmax normalization
    logits = [pos_sim] + neg_sims
    loss = -pos_sim + log(sum(exp(logit) for logit in logits))
    return loss

# Intuition: make the positive sample's score far higher than all negatives
# Low temperature (0.01) → sharp distribution, only tolerates the most similar → learns more compact representations
# High temperature (1.0) → flat distribution, tolerates more similarity variation → more lenient
```

<Callout type="warning" title="The Relationship Between Temperature and Softmax">
The concept of a temperature parameter in contrastive learning will appear in multiple places later:
- **Gumbel-Softmax in VQ-VAE**: temperature controls whether quantization is "hard" or "soft"
- **Attention in Transformers**: `sqrt(d_k)` in `scores / sqrt(d_k)` acts as temperature
- **Sinkhorn codebook balancing**: temperature in `exp(-distance / temperature)` controls how uniform the assignment is

The core math is the same: `softmax(x / tau)` — small temperature yields a sharp distribution, large temperature yields a flat distribution.
</Callout>

### 3.8 The Evolution of Embedding in Recommendation Systems

Embedding technology in recommendation systems has followed its own unique evolutionary path:

| Era | Method | Characteristics | Representative |
|------|------|------|------|
| 2014 | Matrix Factorization | User-item co-occurrence matrix decomposition | SVD++ |
| 2016 | Item2Vec | Skip-gram on sessions | Airbnb, YouTube |
| 2018 | Graph Embedding | Graph structure + random walks | DeepWalk, Node2Vec, PinSage |
| 2019 | Pre-trained Language Models | BERT-encoded content features | BERT4Rec |
| 2020 | Multi-modal Embedding | Graph + text + video fusion | MM-BERT, CLIP |
| 2022 | Contrastive Learning Embedding | Augmentation → contrastive → unified representation | CL4SRec, CoSeRec |
| 2023 | SID Embedding | Representing items via combinations of a finite set of codewords | TIGER, OneRec |

**The Essential Shift from ID Embedding to Semantic ID**：

```python
# Traditional: one independent vector per item (no parameter sharing)
item_embedding = EmbeddingTable[item_id]  # Direct lookup, each ID is independent

# SID: combinatorial representation from a finite set of codewords (codeword parameters are shared)
sid = [k1, k2, k3, k4]  # 4 codeword indices
item_repr = codebook1[k1] + codebook2[k2] + codebook3[k3] + codebook4[k4]
# Similar items share higher-level codewords (e.g., same k1 = same broad category)
```

### 3.9 Connection to the Target Article

| Concept You Just Learned | How It Manifests in RQ-VAE Training |
|--------------|---------------------|
| Word2Vec | Item2Vec was the dominant item representation method before SID |
| BERT Pre-training | The BERT4Rec architecture is directly inherited by SID generative models |
| Contrastive Learning | Multi-modal pre-training (CLIP) provides the input embeddings for RQ-VAE |
| InfoNCE Loss | The temperature parameter concept pervades quantization training (Gumbel-Softmax, Sinkhorn) |
| Embedding Limitations | The raison d'être of SID = solving the four major problems of ID Embedding |

---

## Part 4: Transformer Architecture — The Foundation of Modern Deep Learning

<TechGraph src="/images/posts/rqvae-prerequisite-knowledge/04-transformer-attention.svg" title="Transformer Self-Attention Mechanism" caption="QKV Attention + Autoregressive Generation — the core architecture behind all modern large models" />

### 4.1 Why Transformer Deserves an Entire Part

1. **The recommendation model for SID is a Transformer** (SASRec variant)
2. **All LLMs are Transformers** (GPT, LLaMA, Qwen...)
3. **Recommendation ranking models increasingly rely on Transformers** (BST, HSTU...)
4. **Multi-modal pre-trained models are Transformers** (CLIP, BERT...)

Without understanding Transformers, you cannot understand the SID generation process.

### 4.2 Self-Attention Mechanism

**Core question**: Given a sequence (say, 5 items a user clicked), how does each position "attend" to other positions to enrich its own representation?

**The Query-Key-Value Trio**:

```python
# Input: 5 item embeddings, each 64-dimensional
# X shape: [5, 64]

# Three linear transformations (learnable parameters)
Q = X @ W_Q  # Query: "What information am I looking for?" → [5, 64]
K = X @ W_K  # Key:   "What information can I provide?" → [5, 64]
V = X @ W_V  # Value: "What information do I actually provide?" → [5, 64]

# Attention scores
scores = Q @ K.T / sqrt(64)  # [5, 5], relevance of each position to every other position
weights = softmax(scores, dim=-1)  # Normalize to a probability distribution

# Weighted aggregation
output = weights @ V  # [5, 64]
# output[i] = sum_j(weight[i][j] * V[j])
# i.e., the output at position i is a weighted sum of all positions' Values
```

**A Concrete Numerical Example**:

```python
# User history: [running shoes, treadmill, protein powder, phone case, TV]
# Goal: predict what the user will click next

# The Self-Attention weight distribution might be:
# Running shoes attends to: running shoes(0.15) treadmill(0.40) protein powder(0.35) phone case(0.05) TV(0.05)
#   → The running shoes representation is enriched by "sports-related" neighbors
# Phone case attends to: running shoes(0.03) treadmill(0.02) protein powder(0.02) phone case(0.88) TV(0.05)
#   → Phone case has little relation to other items, mostly attends to itself
```

**Why is sqrt(d_k) Important?**

```python
# Without scaling:
scores = Q @ K.T  # Higher dimensions → larger dot product magnitudes (sum of d terms)
# Large values through softmax → vanishing gradients (softmax saturation region)

# With scaling:
scores = Q @ K.T / sqrt(d_k)  # Keeps variance in a reasonable range
# Reasonable softmax inputs → normal gradients → stable training
```

### 4.3 Multi-Head Attention

A single set of QKV can only learn one "attention pattern." Multi-head attention allows the model to learn multiple different attention patterns simultaneously:

```python
# 8-head attention, d_model=512
# Each head: d_k = d_v = 512/8 = 64

heads = []
for i in range(8):
    Q_i = X @ W_Q_i  # [seq_len, 64]
    K_i = X @ W_K_i  # [seq_len, 64]
    V_i = X @ W_V_i  # [seq_len, 64]
    head_i = attention(Q_i, K_i, V_i)  # [seq_len, 64]
    heads.append(head_i)

# Concatenate all heads, then apply one more linear transformation
multi_head = concat(heads, dim=-1)  # [seq_len, 512]
output = multi_head @ W_O            # [seq_len, 512]
```

**Examples of Different Heads Learning Different Patterns**:
- Head 1: Attends to positionally adjacent items (local pattern)
- Head 2: Attends to items in the same category (semantic pattern)
- Head 3: Attends to items in the same price range (attribute pattern)
- Head 4: Attends to recently clicked items (recency pattern)

### 4.4 Positional Encoding

Self-Attention is inherently "insensitive" to position — shuffling the input order produces the same output (permutation invariant). But in recommendations, click order matters!

**Sinusoidal Positional Encoding (Vaswani 2017)**:

```python
# Assign each position a unique "position vector"
PE(pos, 2i) = sin(pos / 10000^(2i/d_model))
PE(pos, 2i+1) = cos(pos / 10000^(2i/d_model))

# Encoding for position 0: [sin(0), cos(0), sin(0/100), cos(0/100), ...]
# Encoding for position 1: [sin(1), cos(1), sin(1/100), cos(1/100), ...]
# Encoding for position 2: [sin(2), cos(2), sin(2/100), cos(2/100), ...]
```

**Why Trigonometric Functions?**
- The encoding distance between any two positions depends only on their relative distance, not absolute positions
- Can generalize to sequence lengths unseen during training (because trigonometric functions are continuous)

**RoPE (Rotary Position Embedding, 2021)**：

Currently the more mainstream positional encoding in LLMs and recommendation Transformers. Core idea: encode positional information as "rotation angles" of vectors, so that the dot product of two positions naturally contains their relative position information.

```python
# Intuition behind RoPE:
# When the query at position m and the key at position n compute their dot product,
# the result depends only on m-n (relative position), not on absolute position.
# This is because the product of rotation matrices R(m)^T * R(n) = R(n-m)
```

### 4.5 Complete Structure of a Transformer Block

A standard Transformer Block (GPT-style, i.e., what the SID generative model uses):

```python
class TransformerBlock:
    def forward(self, x):
        # Sub-layer 1: Masked Multi-Head Self-Attention + Residual Connection + LayerNorm
        attn_out = multi_head_attention(x, mask=causal_mask)
        x = layer_norm(x + attn_out)  # Post-LN
        # Or: x = x + attn_out; x = layer_norm(x)  # Pre-LN (more common)

        # Sub-layer 2: Feed-Forward Network + Residual Connection + LayerNorm
        ffn_out = linear2(gelu(linear1(x)))  # First expand to 4*d_model then compress back
        x = layer_norm(x + ffn_out)

        return x
```

**Causal Mask**:

```python
# The causal mask ensures each position can only see positions before it (and itself)
# This is critical for autoregressive generation — when generating the 3rd token,
# the model must not peek at the 4th or 5th tokens

# Mask matrix (1=visible, 0=invisible):
# Position 0: [1, 0, 0, 0, 0]  → can only see itself
# Position 1: [1, 1, 0, 0, 0]  → can see 0 and 1
# Position 2: [1, 1, 1, 0, 0]  → can see 0, 1, 2
# Position 3: [1, 1, 1, 1, 0]  → can see 0, 1, 2, 3
# Position 4: [1, 1, 1, 1, 1]  → can see all

# Implementation: set attention scores of invisible positions to -infinity
scores = scores.masked_fill(mask == 0, float('-inf'))
# softmax(-inf) = 0, so these positions get zero weight
```

<Callout type="warning" title="Why SID Generation Must Use a Causal Mask">
SID generation is **autoregressive**: first predict the 1st-level codeword k1, then k2, then k3...

Without a causal mask, the model could see the true values of k2 and k3 when predicting k1 — that's "cheating" (information leakage). At inference time, the subsequent codewords haven't been generated yet, so the model can't see them.

Using a causal mask during training = simulating the "generate one by one" behavior at inference time, ensuring train-test consistency.
</Callout>

### 4.6 Residual Connection

```python
output = layer_norm(x + sublayer(x))
```

**Why Do We Need Residual Connections?**

In deep networks (12+ layers), without residual connections, gradients decay to near zero layer by layer during backpropagation — parameters in the deeper layers learn almost nothing.

Residual connections create an "information highway" — gradients can flow directly from the last layer to the first layer without having to pass through every layer's nonlinear transformations.

```python
# Without residual connections, the gradient chain:
# dL/dx = dL/df_12 * df_12/df_11 * ... * df_2/df_1 * df_1/dx
# 12 multiplied terms, if each < 1 → exponential decay

# With residual connections:
# output = x + f(x)
# doutput/dx = 1 + df/dx
# Even if df/dx is very small, the gradient is at least 1, so it won't vanish
```

**In recommendation models**: All successful deep CTR models (DCN, DeepFM, AutoInt...) use residual connections.

### 4.7 Feed-Forward Network (FFN)

```python
# Standard FFN: expand → activate → compress
ffn(x) = W_2 * GELU(W_1 * x + b_1) + b_2
# W_1: [d_model, 4*d_model]  → expand by 4x
# W_2: [4*d_model, d_model]  → compress back
```

**Role of the FFN**: The Attention layer is responsible for "information exchange across positions," while the FFN layer is responsible for "information transformation at each individual position." You can think of the FFN as a "knowledge store" — in large models, much factual knowledge is stored in FFN parameters.

**Why expand first and then compress?** Nonlinear transformations are easier to perform in high-dimensional space (linear separating hyperplanes are richer in higher dimensions). Analogy: project the data into a higher-dimensional space to "think," then compress the conclusions back to the original dimensionality.

### 4.8 Pre-LN vs Post-LN

```python
# Post-LN (original Transformer)
x = layer_norm(x + sublayer(x))

# Pre-LN (GPT-2 and subsequent mainstream approach)
x = x + sublayer(layer_norm(x))
```

**Advantages of Pre-LN**:
- More stable training with a more consistent gradient range
- No need for careful learning rate warmup
- Deep networks (24+ layers) almost always require Pre-LN

**The recommender Transformer in SID typically uses Pre-LN**.

### 4.9 Autoregressive Generation

This is one of the **most critical concepts** for understanding SID generation.

**What autoregressive means**: tokens are generated one at a time; each newly generated token is fed back as input for the next step.

```python
# The process by which GPT generates text (SID generation is entirely analogous)
def autoregressive_generate(prompt, max_length):
    tokens = tokenize(prompt)
    for step in range(max_length):
        # Use all existing tokens to predict the next one
        logits = transformer(tokens)     # [seq_len, vocab_size]
        next_logit = logits[-1]          # Only look at the prediction from the last position
        next_token = sample(softmax(next_logit))  # Sample or argmax
        tokens.append(next_token)
        if next_token == EOS:
            break
    return tokens
```

**The autoregressive process of SID generation**:

```python
# A SID is a codeword sequence, e.g. [k1, k2, k3, k4]
# The generation process is exactly the same as GPT generating text

def generate_sid(user_history_sids):
    # Input: the user's historical SID sequence
    # [SID_1, SID_2, ..., SID_N, <SEP>]
    
    # Step 1: predict k1 (first-layer codeword)
    # P(k1 | user_history)
    logits_layer1 = transformer([history..., <SEP>])[-1]  # vocab=1024
    k1 = beam_search_top(logits_layer1)  # Keep top-B candidates
    
    # Step 2: predict k2 (second-layer codeword)
    # P(k2 | user_history, k1)
    logits_layer2 = transformer([history..., <SEP>, k1])[-1]
    k2 = beam_search_top(logits_layer2)
    
    # Step 3: predict k3
    # P(k3 | user_history, k1, k2)
    ...
    
    return [k1, k2, k3, k4]
```

<Callout type="info" title="Autoregressive vs Non-autoregressive">
- **Autoregressive**: generates one token at a time; each step is conditioned on all previously generated results. High quality but slow.
- **Non-autoregressive**: generates all tokens in parallel at once. Fast but usually lower quality (because inter-token dependencies are ignored).

SID generation uses **autoregressive** decoding because there are strong dependencies between codewords (when k1="China", k2 should be a province of China, not a province of France). Non-autoregressive generation would break this hierarchical structure.
</Callout>

### 4.10 Beam Search

During autoregressive generation, each step has multiple candidates. Greedy selection (picking the best at each step) may miss the global optimum. Beam Search maintains B candidate paths:

```python
# Beam Search (beam_width=3)
# Step 1: P(k1|history) → keep top-3 k1 values
# candidates: [(k1=37, score=0.3), (k1=12, score=0.25), (k1=891, score=0.2)]

# Step 2: expand each k1 with top-3 k2 values → 3*3=9 candidates → keep top-3
# candidates:
#   (k1=37,k2=512, score=0.3*0.4=0.12)
#   (k1=37,k2=100, score=0.3*0.35=0.105)
#   (k1=12,k2=200, score=0.25*0.45=0.1125)

# Step 3: continue expanding... until a complete SID is generated

# Final result: top-B complete SIDs
# Each SID corresponds to one item (or a group of similar items)
```

**Choosing the Beam Width in recommender systems**:
- Too small (B=10): low recall, may miss good recommendations
- Too large (B=1000): inference is too slow, latency is unacceptable
- In practice B=50–200 is the common range

### 4.11 Transformer Applications in Recommender Systems

<TechGraph src="/images/posts/rqvae-prerequisite-knowledge/14-transformer-in-recsys.svg" title="Transformer Applications across Recommendation Stages" caption="From retrieval to ranking — Self-Attention has become the unified paradigm for sequence modeling" />

| Model | Year | Architecture | Training Method | Application |
|------|------|------|---------|------|
| **SASRec** | 2018 | Unidirectional Transformer | Next-item prediction | Sequential recommendation (→ SID backbone) |
| **BERT4Rec** | 2019 | Bidirectional Transformer | Masked item prediction | Sequential recommendation |
| **BST** | 2019 | Transformer embedded in CTR | CTR classification | Ranking (Taobao) |
| **HSTU** | 2024 | Transformer (Meta) | Multi-task | Unified model (Meta) |

### 4.12 Connection to the Target Article

| Concept You Just Learned | How It Manifests in RQ-VAE Training |
|--------------|---------------------|
| Self-Attention | Core of sequence modeling in the SID recommendation model |
| Causal Mask | SID autoregressive generation must be unidirectional |
| Positional Encoding | Temporal information of user historical behaviors |
| Residual Connection | Transformer depth can reach 12–24 layers |
| Autoregressive Generation | Layer-by-layer generation: k1→k2→k3→k4 |
| Beam Search | Generating top-K SID candidates during the retrieval stage |
