## Part 5: LLMs, VAEs, and Generative Models — From Autoregressive to Latent Space

<TechGraph src="/images/posts/rqvae-prerequisite-knowledge/13-vae-vs-vqvae.svg" title="VAE vs VQ-VAE vs RQ-VAE" caption="From continuous latent space to discrete codebooks — understanding the evolution across three generations of models" />

### 5.1 Core Mechanisms of Large Language Models (LLMs)

LLMs and SID generation models share a striking similarity. Understanding LLMs is essentially understanding how SID generation works.

**LLM Training Objective (Next Token Prediction)**:

```python
# Training data: "I love Beijing Tiananmen"
# tokenized: [I, love, Beijing, Tiananmen]

# The model predicts step by step:
# P(love | I)                     → Step 1
# P(Beijing | I, love)               → Step 2
# P(Tiananmen | I, love, Beijing)       → Step 3

# Loss: sum of cross-entropy at each step
loss = -log P(love|I) - log P(Beijing|I,love) - log P(Tiananmen|I,love,Beijing)
```

**Comparison between SID recommendation models and LLMs**:

| Concept | LLM | SID Recommendation Model |
|------|-----|-------------|
| Token | Text token (word or subword) | SID codeword index (0–1023) |
| Vocabulary | 32K–128K | Codebook size K (typically 1024–8192) |
| Training Objective | Predict the next token | Predict the next codeword |
| Input Sequence | All preceding tokens | SID sequence of user historical behaviors |
| Generation Method | Autoregressive + sampling/beam search | Autoregressive + beam search |
| Model Architecture | Transformer Decoder | Transformer Decoder (usually a SASRec variant) |

<Callout type="tip" title="If you understand GPT, you understand SID generation">
SID generation = "GPT continuation, but with a vocabulary of only 1024 codewords"

Input: "The SID codeword sequence corresponding to items the user recently clicked"
Output: "Predict the SID codewords of the item the user will click next, generated layer by layer"

The only difference: LLM tokens carry linguistic meaning (subwords), while SID tokens are learned semantic clusters.
</Callout>

### 5.2 Tokens and Tokenization

**Why do we need Tokenization?**

Models can only process discrete IDs from a fixed-size vocabulary. Mapping arbitrary inputs to a fixed vocabulary is Tokenization.

**Evolution of text Tokenization**:

```python
# 1. Character-level: vocab = 26 letters
"hello" → ['h', 'e', 'l', 'l', 'o']  # Sequences too long

# 2. Word-level: vocab = all English words (millions)
"hello world" → ['hello', 'world']  # Vocab too large

# 3. Subword-level (BPE): vocab = 32K–64K frequent subwords
"unhappiness" → ['un', 'happiness']
"unbelievable" → ['un', 'believ', 'able']
# Balances sequence length and vocabulary size
```

**Semantic ID is Tokenization for Items**:

```python
# Traditional recommendation: item_id → independent embedding (million-level vocab)
# SID recommendation: item → [k1, k2, k3, k4] (1024-level vocab per layer)

# Analogy:
# BPE decomposes text into subword combinations → "unhappiness" = "un" + "happiness"
# RQ-VAE decomposes items into semantic codeword combinations → Nike running shoes = "sports" + "footwear" + "mid-range" + "men's"
```

### 5.3 Scaling Law

**LLM Scaling Laws (Kaplan 2020, Chinchilla 2022)**:

```
Loss = a * N^(-alpha) + b * D^(-beta) + c
# N: number of model parameters
# D: amount of training data
# Loss: cross-entropy loss on the validation set
# alpha ≈ 0.076, beta ≈ 0.095 (Chinchilla)
```

Core finding: increasing both the number of parameters and the amount of data predictably reduces loss, with no ceiling (within the observed range).

**Why doesn't traditional recommendation have a Scaling Law?**

```python
# LLM: finite vocabulary (32K tokens); more parameters → richer representation per token
# Semantic compositionality: the model can generalize to unseen word combinations

# Traditional recommendation (Hash ID):
# Million-level item vocabulary, each item has its own independent row of parameters
# More parameters = larger embedding dimension = each item improves independently
# No cross-item generalization, because there is no semantic relationship between IDs

# SID recommendation:
# Finite codeword vocabulary (1024–8192)
# More parameters → richer representation per codeword → all items sharing those codewords benefit
# Cross-item generalization exists, because similar items share high-level codewords
# → In theory, a Scaling Law is possible
```

<Callout type="warning" title="The debate around Scaling Laws for recommender systems">
There is currently industry debate over whether "SID truly brings Scaling Laws to recommendation." Optimists argue that OneRec/HSTU have already shown similar trends. Pessimists contend that a large number of business features in recommender systems (bids, budgets, targeting) cannot be semanticized, and Scaling Laws only apply to the content understanding component.

The real answer is probably: pure content recommendation (short videos / music) has the best chance of achieving Scaling Laws, while ad systems (with heavy business constraints) can only partially benefit.
</Callout>

### 5.4 Autoencoder

The predecessor of all VAEs. Understanding autoencoders is the starting point for understanding VQ-VAE.

```python
# Structure of an Autoencoder
# Input x → Encoder → Latent variable z (low-dimensional) → Decoder → Reconstructed x_hat

class Autoencoder:
    def __init__(self, input_dim=784, hidden_dim=32):
        self.encoder = Linear(input_dim, hidden_dim)  # Compression
        self.decoder = Linear(hidden_dim, input_dim)  # Reconstruction

    def forward(self, x):
        z = relu(self.encoder(x))     # Encode to low dimension
        x_hat = sigmoid(self.decoder(z))  # Reconstruct from low dimension
        return x_hat

    def loss(self, x, x_hat):
        return mse(x, x_hat)  # Reconstruction error
```

**Core idea**: Force data through a "bottleneck" (low-dimensional latent space), compelling the model to learn to extract the most important information.

**Dimensionality reduction effect**:
```
Input: 784 dimensions (28x28 MNIST image)
Latent space: 32 dimensions (24.5x compression)
Output: 784 dimensions (reconstructed image)

If reconstruction quality is good → the 32-dimensional space has preserved the core information of the image
```

### 5.5 VAE (Variational Autoencoder)

The latent space of a vanilla autoencoder is "unstructured"—you don't know which region of the latent space corresponds to what kind of output. VAE adds **probabilistic structure** to the latent space.

**VAE's core improvement**:

```python
class VAE:
    def encode(self, x):
        # The encoder doesn't output a single point, but parameters of a distribution
        mu = encoder_mu(x)      # Mean
        log_var = encoder_var(x) # Log variance
        return mu, log_var

    def reparameterize(self, mu, log_var):
        # Reparameterization trick: make the sampling process differentiable
        std = exp(0.5 * log_var)
        epsilon = randn_like(std)   # Sample from standard normal distribution
        z = mu + std * epsilon      # z ~ N(mu, std^2)
        return z

    def decode(self, z):
        return decoder(z)

    def loss(self, x, x_hat, mu, log_var):
        # Loss = Reconstruction loss + KL divergence
        recon_loss = mse(x, x_hat)
        kl_loss = -0.5 * sum(1 + log_var - mu^2 - exp(log_var))
        return recon_loss + beta * kl_loss
```

**Reparameterization Trick**:

This is VAE's most critical innovation. The problem: the sampling operation `z ~ N(mu, sigma)` is not differentiable—gradients cannot propagate back through "sampling."

Solution: separate the randomness from the computation graph:
```
z = mu + sigma * epsilon  (epsilon ~ N(0,1) is external noise)
```

Now the gradient of z with respect to mu is 1, and the gradient with respect to sigma is epsilon—both are computable!

<Callout type="warning" title={'This "non-differentiable" idea is exactly the same as in VQ-VAE'}>
- **VAE's problem**: Sampling operation is non-differentiable → Solution: Reparameterization trick
- **VQ-VAE's problem**: argmin operation is non-differentiable → Solution: STE (Straight-Through Estimator)

Both follow the pattern of "a non-differentiable operation blocks the gradient" → "use a clever approximation to route the gradient around it."
</Callout>

**The role of KL divergence**:

```python
# KL(q(z|x) || p(z)) penalizes the encoding distribution for deviating from the standard normal
# Effect: The latent space is "regularized" to be close to a standard normal distribution
# Benefits:
# 1. You can sample z from a standard normal → generate new samples
# 2. The latent space is continuous and smooth → interpolation is possible
# 3. Prevents the latent space from degenerating into a set of discrete points
```

**Posterior Collapse**:

A classic problem with VAE: when the decoder is too powerful (e.g., using PixelCNN), it completely ignores the latent variable z and learns the data distribution on its own. In this case, the KL loss approaches 0 (because the encoder output = prior), but this is not a good thing—the latent space becomes useless.

### 5.6 VQ-VAE (Vector Quantized VAE)

VQ-VAE is the key intermediate step from VAE to RQ-VAE.

**Core change: Replace continuous distributions with a discrete codebook**

```python
class VQVAE:
    def __init__(self, num_codes=512, code_dim=64):
        # Codebook: 512 learnable 64-dimensional vectors
        self.codebook = nn.Embedding(512, 64)

    def encode(self, x):
        z_e = self.encoder(x)  # Continuous encoding [batch, 64]
        return z_e

    def quantize(self, z_e):
        # Find the nearest code word
        distances = cdist(z_e, self.codebook.weight)  # [batch, 512]
        indices = distances.argmin(dim=1)              # [batch]
        z_q = self.codebook(indices)                   # [batch, 64]
        return z_q, indices

    def decode(self, z_q):
        return self.decoder(z_q)
```

**VQ-VAE's three loss terms**:

```python
def vqvae_loss(x, x_hat, z_e, z_q):
    # 1. Reconstruction loss: decoding quality
    L_recon = mse(x, x_hat)

    # 2. VQ loss: move code words toward encoder outputs (update codebook)
    L_vq = mse(z_e.detach(), z_q)  # z_e as target, no gradient backprop

    # 3. Commitment loss: move encoder outputs toward code words (stabilize encoder)
    L_commit = mse(z_e, z_q.detach())  # z_q as target, no gradient backprop

    return L_recon + L_vq + beta * L_commit
    # beta is typically = 0.25
```

**Why are three loss terms needed?**

If there were only reconstruction loss:
- The codebook has no incentive to move (argmin is non-differentiable, so the codebook receives no gradient)
- Encoder outputs may be far from all code words

L_vq pulls code words toward encoder outputs (more commonly done via EMA updates).
L_commit pulls encoder outputs toward code words (prevents the encoder from "drifting too far").

**Straight-Through Estimator (STE)**：

```python
# Forward: use the quantized z_q
output = decoder(z_q)

# Backward: pretend quantization didn't happen, gradient is copied directly from z_q to z_e
# Implementation: z_q = z_e + (z_q - z_e).detach()
# Equivalent to: value = z_q during forward, gradient = gradient w.r.t. z_e during backward

def straight_through(z_e, z_q):
    return z_e + (z_q - z_e).detach()
    # .detach() truncates gradient: no gradient is computed for the (z_q - z_e) part
    # Backward: d/dz_e [z_e + constant] = 1
    # So the decoder's gradient w.r.t. z_q directly becomes the gradient w.r.t. z_e
```

<Callout type="warning" title="The cost of STE">
STE is a biased estimator—it pretends the quantization layer doesn't exist, but the quantization layer does lose information. This bias grows larger when:
1. z_e is far from the nearest code word (large quantization error)
2. Code words are unevenly distributed (parts of the space have no code word coverage)
3. Multiple RQ layers are used (bias accumulates layer by layer)

This is why codebook collapse (a large number of code words going unused) doesn't just waste capacity—it directly degrades gradient quality.
</Callout>

### 5.7 EMA (Exponential Moving Average) Codebook Updates

In practice, the VQ-VAE codebook is usually not updated via gradient descent (because argmin is non-differentiable), but via EMA:

```python
# EMA codebook update
# For each code word e_k:

# Collect all encoder outputs assigned to e_k in the current batch
assigned_vectors = z_e[assignments == k]  # All z_e assigned to code word k
n_k = len(assigned_vectors)              # Assignment count

# EMA update
N_k = decay * N_k + (1-decay) * n_k     # Update count
m_k = decay * m_k + (1-decay) * sum(assigned_vectors)  # Update accumulator
e_k = m_k / N_k                          # Code word = mean

# decay is typically = 0.99
# Intuition: code words slowly move toward the centroid of vectors assigned to them
```

**EMA vs Gradient Update**:
- EMA: More stable, faster convergence, no need to tune the codebook learning rate
- Gradient: End-to-end consistent, but requires STE, can be unstable

### 5.8 Generative Models Overview

| Model | Generation Method | Latent Space | Representative |
|------|---------|--------|------|
| **GAN** | Adversarial training | Continuous (random noise) | StyleGAN |
| **VAE** | Encode-sample-decode | Continuous (Gaussian) | DALL-E (1st gen) |
| **VQ-VAE** | Encode-quantize-decode | Discrete (codebook) | DALL-E, AudioLM |
| **Diffusion** | Iterative denoising | Continuous (diffusion process) | Stable Diffusion |
| **Autoregressive** | Token-by-token generation | Discrete (vocabulary) | GPT, SID Recommendation |
| **Flow** | Invertible transforms | Continuous (invertible mapping) | Glow |

**Why does SID choose the "VQ-VAE + Autoregressive" combination?**

```python
# SID's two-stage approach:
# Stage 1: VQ-VAE/RQ-VAE converts continuous embeddings → discrete code word sequences
# Stage 2: Autoregressive Transformer learns the generative distribution over code word sequences

# Why not use Diffusion directly?
# 1. Diffusion generates continuous vectors, not suitable for "discrete IDs"
# 2. Autoregressive generation of discrete tokens is highly unified with language modeling,
#    allowing reuse of the mature LLM tech stack
# 3. Beam Search is efficient in discrete space but very hard to do in continuous space

# Why not use VAE directly (continuous latent space)?
# 1. Continuous vectors can't directly serve as a "vocabulary"—can't train with softmax + cross-entropy
# 2. Discrete tokens can be trained directly with LLM's next-token prediction
# 3. Discrete IDs are better suited for indexing (hash table lookup in O(1))
```

### 5.9 Connection to the Target Article

| Concept You Just Learned | How It Manifests in RQ-VAE Training |
|--------------|---------------------|
| LLM Next Token Prediction | The SID generative model's training objective is exactly the same |
| Tokenization | RQ-VAE is the Tokenizer for items |
| Scaling Law | SID makes it possible for recommendation systems to exhibit Scaling Laws |
| VAE Reparameterization | Solves the same class of problem as VQ-VAE's STE |
| VQ-VAE Three Loss Terms | Source of the "loss tug-of-war" failure mode |
| EMA Codebook Update | The mainstream codebook update method in practice |
| STE | The core mechanism for passing gradients through the quantization layer |
| Posterior Collapse | A degeneration phenomenon similar to codebook collapse |

---

## Part 6: Search, Ads & Recommendation Systems — The Complete Architecture of Industrial-Scale Recommendation and Advertising

<TechGraph src="/images/posts/rqvae-prerequisite-knowledge/02-recsys-evolution.svg" title="Recommendation System Evolution and Architecture" caption="From one-size-fits-all to personalized — the design logic behind multi-stage pipelines" />

### 6.1 Why a Multi-Stage Pipeline Is Needed

Suppose you have **100 million candidate items**, and the user opens the App—you have **50ms** to decide which 10 to display.

```
100M items × complex deep model × 50ms time limit = impossible
```

Solution: **Funnel-shaped multi-stage filtering**, where each stage uses a simpler/faster model to process a larger candidate set.

```
Full item pool (10^8)
    ↓ Retrieval (Recall)     → Simple models, 5-10ms, select 5,000-10,000
    ↓ Pre-ranking (Pre-Rank) → Lightweight models, 5-10ms, select 500-1,000
    ↓ Ranking (Rank)         → Complex deep models, 10-20ms, score & sort each
    ↓ Re-ranking (Re-Rank)   → Diversification / business rules, 5ms
    ↓ Auction                → Ad scenario: eCPM = CTR × Bid
Final display (10-30 items)
```

### 6.2 Retrieval (Recall)

Quickly find a "possibly relevant" subset from the full item pool. Requirements:

- **Speed**: Must be extremely fast (cannot run complex computation on every item)
- **Recall rate**: Avoid missing good items as much as possible
- **Precision can be low**: The ranking stage downstream will correct errors

**Main retrieval strategies**:

| Strategy | Method | Latency | Recall Rate |
|------|------|------|--------|
| Collaborative filtering | ItemCF / UserCF / Matrix factorization | &lt;1ms | Medium |
| **Two-tower** | User/Item encoding → ANN search | 2-5ms | High |
| Graph retrieval | User-item heterogeneous graph walk | 5-10ms | High |
| **SID generative** | Transformer autoregressive generation | 5-20ms | High (no index limitation) |
| Rules / tags | Popular / new / category | &lt;1ms | Supplementary |

<Callout type="info" title="The revolutionary nature of SID generative retrieval">
Traditional retrieval relies on an index (two-tower → ANN index). Indexes have two fundamental problems:
1. **Representation and retrieval are decoupled**: The model learns embeddings while a separate system builds the index—both are optimized independently.
2. **ANN approximation error**: Exact nearest neighbors are sacrificed for speed.

SID generative retrieval has **no index**—the model directly generates the item's ID. Representation and retrieval live in the same model. In theory, it can achieve "exact most relevant" results.
</Callout>

### 6.3 Two-Tower Model and ANN Retrieval

<TechGraph src="/images/posts/rqvae-prerequisite-knowledge/08-two-tower-ann.svg" title="Two-Tower Architecture and ANN Retrieval" caption="Separate encoding + approximate nearest neighbor — the dominant paradigm for industrial retrieval" />

**Core idea of two-tower**: The user and item are each encoded into vectors by an independent "tower" (network), and their match is measured via inner product / cosine similarity.

```python
# Two-tower model
class TwoTower:
    def __init__(self):
        self.user_tower = MLP(user_features_dim, embedding_dim=128)
        self.item_tower = MLP(item_features_dim, embedding_dim=128)

    def forward(self, user_features, item_features):
        user_vec = self.user_tower(user_features)   # [batch, 128]
        item_vec = self.item_tower(item_features)   # [batch, 128]
        score = dot(user_vec, item_vec)              # [batch]
        return score

# Why must the two towers be independent (no cross-interaction)?
# Because at online inference time we need:
# 1. Offline: encode all items once and store them in an ANN index
# 2. Online: encode the current user once and query the ANN index
# If the two towers have cross-interactions, we'd have to run the model
# for every user-item pair → too slow
```

**ANN (Approximate Nearest Neighbor) Index**:

After building the item embedding index, we find the most similar items given a user embedding. Exact nearest neighbor is O(N) — too slow — so we use approximate algorithms at O(log N) or O(1).

#### HNSW (Hierarchical Navigable Small World)

```python
# HNSW: Hierarchical graph structure
# Like a highway network:
# Top layer: a few key nodes, long-distance jumps (interstate highways)
# Middle layers: more nodes, medium distance (regional roads)
# Bottom layer: all nodes, local connections (streets)

# Search process: coarse positioning at the top layer, refine layer by layer
# Time: O(log N)
# Space: O(N * M)  M = number of connections
# Precision: 95%+ recall@100
```

#### IVF-PQ (Inverted File + Product Quantization)

```python
# Step 1: IVF - partition the space into N_list Voronoi cells
#   Each item is assigned to the nearest cell centroid
# Step 2: PQ - split the high-dimensional vector into M sub-vectors,
#   each independently quantized to 256 centroids
#   Storage: only M bytes needed (instead of d*4 bytes)

# Search: only search the nearest N_probe cells, use PQ distance for fast filtering
# Time: O(N_probe * N/N_list)
# Space: O(N * M) bytes (extremely low!)
# Best for: billion-scale item retrieval
```

#### ScaNN (Google, 2020)

Google's production-grade ANN. Key innovation: Anisotropic Quantization — adjusting quantization error weights based on the query direction.

**Core trade-offs of ANN indexes**:

| Metric | HNSW | IVF-PQ | ScaNN |
|------|------|--------|-------|
| Precision | Highest | Medium | High |
| Speed | Fast | Extremely fast | Very fast |
| Memory | Large | Extremely small | Medium |
| Applicable scale | Tens of millions | Billions | Hundreds of millions |
| Dynamic updates | Difficult | Easy | Medium |

### 6.4 Evolution of CTR Prediction Models

<TechGraph src="/images/posts/rqvae-prerequisite-knowledge/07-ctr-model-evolution.svg" title="Six Generations of CTR Prediction Model Evolution" caption="From hand-crafted features to Foundation Models — 20 years of industrial innovation" />

CTR prediction is the core task in the ranking stage: given (user, item, context), predict the click probability.

#### First Generation: Linear Models (2007-2013)

```python
# LR (Logistic Regression)
# The starting point of industrial CTR prediction
score = sigmoid(w · x + b)
# x is a manually constructed feature vector (including many cross features)
# Advantage: simple, interpretable, fast to train
# Disadvantage: cannot automatically learn feature crosses
```

```python
# GBDT + LR (Facebook 2014)
# Use GBDT for automatic feature crossing, output fed as LR input
tree_features = gbdt.predict(raw_features)  # leaf node one-hot
score = lr(tree_features)
```

#### Second Generation: FM Family (2010-2016)

```python
# FM (Factorization Machines)
# Automatically learn second-order feature crosses
score = w0 + sum(w_i * x_i) + sum_pairs(dot(v_i, v_j) * x_i * x_j)
# v_i: latent vector (embedding) for each feature
# Number of crosses: O(n^2) but computational complexity O(kn) (via mathematical expansion)
```

```python
# FFM (Field-aware FM)
# Each feature has a different embedding for different "fields"
score = ... + sum_pairs(dot(v_i_fj, v_j_fi) * x_i * x_j)
# More fine-grained but more parameters
```

#### Third Generation: Deep + Cross (2016-2019)

```python
# Wide&Deep (Google 2016)
# Wide part: manually crossed features → LR (memorization)
# Deep part: embedding → MLP (generalization)
score = sigmoid(wide(x) + deep(x))
```

```python
# DeepFM (Huawei 2017)
# FM replaces the manual crosses of the Wide part
# Deep handles high-order crosses
score = sigmoid(fm(x) + mlp(x))
```

```python
# DCN (Deep & Cross Network, Google 2017)
# Cross Network explicitly models high-order crosses
# Layer l+1: x_{l+1} = x_0 * x_l^T * w_l + b_l + x_l
# Each layer introduces one additional order of crossing
score = sigmoid(cross_net(x) + deep_net(x))
```

#### Fourth Generation: Attention-Driven (2018-2020)

```python
# DIN (Deep Interest Network, Alibaba 2018)
# Core idea: different candidate ads should attend to different items
# in the user's history
# Use Attention to weight user history items

def din_attention(candidate, user_history):
    # candidate: embedding of the current candidate ad
    # user_history: list of item embeddings from user's click history
    weights = [mlp([h, candidate, h*candidate, h-candidate]) for h in user_history]
    weights = softmax(weights)
    user_interest = sum(w * h for w, h in zip(weights, user_history))
    return user_interest
```

```python
# DIEN (Deep Interest Evolution Network, Alibaba 2019)
# Builds on DIN by adding interest evolution modeling (GRU + Attention)
# Core idea: user interests evolve over time (from running shoes → running watches)
```

```python
# AutoInt (2019)
# Uses Multi-Head Self-Attention to automatically learn feature crosses
# Each attention layer is equivalent to learning one layer of feature crossing
# Stacking multiple layers = automatically learning high-order crosses
```

#### Fifth Generation: Multi-Task Learning (2019-2022)

```python
# MMOE (Multi-gate Mixture-of-Experts, Google 2018)
# Simultaneously optimize multiple objectives (CTR + CVR + watch time + ...)
# Shared expert networks + each task has its own independent gate

experts = [expert_1(x), expert_2(x), ..., expert_8(x)]
gate_ctr = softmax(gate_net_ctr(x))   # CTR task selects which experts
gate_cvr = softmax(gate_net_cvr(x))   # CVR task selects which experts

ctr_input = sum(g * e for g, e in zip(gate_ctr, experts))
cvr_input = sum(g * e for g, e in zip(gate_cvr, experts))

ctr = sigmoid(tower_ctr(ctr_input))
cvr = sigmoid(tower_cvr(cvr_input))
```

```python
# ESMM (Entire Space Multi-task Model, Alibaba 2018)
# Solves the sample selection bias problem in CVR estimation
# pCVR = pCTCVR / pCTR
# Trains on the entire impression space rather than only clicked samples
```

#### Sixth Generation: Foundation Model (2023-2025)

```python
# HSTU (Hierarchical Sequential Transduction Unit, Meta 2024)
# Transformer as a unified representation for user behavior sequences
# 1.5 trillion parameters, processing 200+ real-time features
# Already deployed across all Meta products

# OneRec (Kuaishou 2024)
# End-to-end unified recommendation
# Multimodal large model → Tokenizer → Transformer → Generative recommendation
# Claims to have achieved a Scaling Law for recommendation
```

### 6.5 Ad Auction System

The biggest difference between recommendation systems and ad systems: ads involve **money flow**.

```python
# Core formula for ad ranking
eCPM = pCTR × pCVR × Bid × Quality_Factor

# eCPM: expected Cost Per Mille (estimated revenue per thousand impressions)
# pCTR: predicted click-through rate (model prediction)
# pCVR: predicted conversion rate (model prediction)
# Bid: advertiser's bid (manually set)
# Quality_Factor: ad quality factor (creative quality, etc.)
```

**Why does the ad scenario demand even higher SID stability?**

```python
# Recommendation scenario: SID changes → user might see one fewer video → experience slightly degraded
# Ads scenario: SID changes → pCTR prediction inaccurate → eCPM ranking wrong →
#           revenue either drops (platform loss) → or advertiser spends money they shouldn't (advertiser loss)
#           → financial loss is directly quantifiable

# This is why the ads system "never dared to fully roll out end-to-end SID"
```

### 6.6 Offline Evaluation vs Online Evaluation

| Evaluation Method | Metrics | Reliability | Cost |
|---------|------|--------|------|
| **Offline Evaluation** | AUC, GAUC, NDCG | Medium (biased) | Low |
| **Online AB Testing** | CTR, CVR, GMV, Retention | High (real feedback) | High |

The core bias of offline evaluation: **it can only evaluate on "items that were shown in the past"**. If a new model would recommend items that were never shown before (e.g., new long-tail items), offline evaluation cannot measure the quality of these recommendations.

This is also why it's hard to verify SID's value for long-tail items offline—long-tail items inherently have few impressions, and historical data lacks feedback signals for them.

### 6.7 Cold Start Problem

| Cold Start Type | Problem | Traditional Solution | SID Solution |
|-----------|------|---------|---------|
| New item | No behavioral data → embedding cannot be trained | Content feature retrieval, exploration traffic | Content → RQ-VAE → automatically obtains SID |
| New user | No historical behavior → cannot personalize | Demographics + popular items | Not addressed (SID is an item-side technique) |
| New scenario | No training data | Cross-domain transfer | Same SID reused across scenarios |

<Callout type="tip" title="Intuition Behind SID Solving Cold Start">
Traditional approach: New shoe listed → ID = 12345 → embedding[12345] = randomly initialized → needs lots of clicks to learn well

SID approach: New shoe listed → image/text/attributes → encoder → RQ-VAE → SID = [3, 7, 2, 5] →
This SID shares the first two digits with other sneakers → **naturally inherits all behavioral knowledge of the sneaker category**

Reasonable recommendations without any click data—this is SID's greatest practical value.
</Callout>

### 6.8 Connection to the Target Article

| Concept You Just Learned | How It Manifests in RQ-VAE Training |
|--------------|---------------------|
| Multi-stage Pipeline | SID is used in the retrieval stage (replacing dual-tower + ANN) |
| Dual-tower + ANN | SID's direct competitor, solving the same problem |
| CTR Model | SID as feature input to CTR model / SID model replacing CTR |
| Ad Auction | eCPM's high requirement for SID stability |
| Cold Start | SID's core value scenario |
| Online AB | The only reliable way to verify SID's online effectiveness |

## Part 7: Vector Quantization—The Core Technique from Continuous to Discrete

<TechGraph src="/images/posts/rqvae-prerequisite-knowledge/03-vector-quantization.svg" title="Vector Quantization Mechanism" caption="Continuous vector → discrete codeword → Semantic ID: quantization is the critical middle layer of the entire tech stack" />

### 7.1 What Is Vector Quantization

**One-sentence definition**: Map a vector in continuous space to the nearest representative point in a finite set.

```python
# Input: a continuous vector z (e.g., 64 dimensions)
# Codebook: K preset representative vectors {e_1, e_2, ..., e_K}
# Quantization: find the nearest representative vector

z = [0.23, -0.45, 0.87, ...]  # Continuous, infinite possibilities
z_q = codebook[argmin_k ||z - e_k||]  # Discrete, only K possibilities

# Final output: the index of the codeword (an integer)
index = argmin_k ||z - e_k||  # e.g., index = 37
```

**Everyday life analogies**:
- GPS coordinates (39.9042° N, 116.4074° E) → quantized to "Chaoyang District, Beijing"
- Height 175.3cm → quantized to "medium build"
- RGB (123, 87, 201) → quantized to "purple"

### 7.2 K-Means: The Most Basic Vector Quantization

K-Means is the simplest vector quantization: partition N data points into K clusters, where each cluster center is a codeword.

```python
# K-Means algorithm
def kmeans(data, K, max_iter=100):
    # 1. Randomly initialize K centers
    centers = random_sample(data, K)

    for iteration in range(max_iter):
        # 2. Assign: each point belongs to the nearest center
        assignments = [argmin_k ||x - centers[k]|| for x in data]

        # 3. Update: each center = mean of all points in that cluster
        for k in range(K):
            cluster_points = data[assignments == k]
            centers[k] = mean(cluster_points)

    return centers, assignments
```

**Limitations of K-Means**:

```python
# 1. Must load all data at once (Full Batch)
# 1 billion items × 128 dims × 4 bytes = 512 GB memory → impractical

# 2. Can only learn "spherical" clusters (because it uses Euclidean distance)
# If data distribution is not spherical → poor results

# 3. Limited capacity
# K=1024 K-Means → can distinguish at most 1024 types of items → far from enough

# 4. Initialization sensitive
# Different random initializations may converge to very different results
```

### 7.3 Product Quantization (PQ)

**Core idea**: Split a high-dimensional vector into M segments and run K-Means independently on each segment.

```python
# Original vector: 128 dimensions
# Split into M=8 segments, each 16 dimensions
# Run K=256 clustering independently on each segment

segments = split(z, M=8)  # 8 sub-vectors of 16 dimensions
codes = []
for i, seg in enumerate(segments):
    code = find_nearest(seg, codebook_i)  # Find nearest in the i-th codebook (256 entries of 16 dims)
    codes.append(code)

# PQ encoding: [c1, c2, c3, c4, c5, c6, c7, c8]
# Each c_i is an integer from 0-255 → 8 bytes total
# Capacity: 256^8 = 1.8 × 10^19 combinations (far exceeding 1 billion items)
# Storage: 8 bytes (vs original 128×4=512 bytes, 64x compression)
```

**PQ's problem**: Each segment is quantized independently, ignoring inter-segment correlations. If data is highly correlated in certain directions (e.g., "height" and "arm span"), independent segmentation loses information.

### 7.4 OPQ (Optimized Product Quantization)

**Core idea**: First rotate the data space to minimize correlations between segments, then apply PQ.

```python
# OPQ: Learn a rotation matrix R
z_rotated = R @ z  # Rotated vector
pq_code = PQ(z_rotated)  # Apply PQ in the rotated space

# Objective for rotation matrix R:
# min ||z - R^T * PQ_reconstruct(R * z)||^2
# i.e., minimize the reconstruction error of PQ in the rotated space

# Optimization methods:
# Traditional: Alternating optimization (Iterative Quantization) — requires full data
# New method (RQOPQ): Optimize R via gradient descent — supports Mini-Batch!
```

<Callout type="info" title="RQOPQ = RQ + OPQ">
Kuaishou's OneSearch (2025) assigns Semantic IDs using RQOPQ:
1. Learn a rotation matrix R (via gradient descent, supports batching)
2. Perform residual quantization (RQ) in the rotated space
3. End-to-end gradient backpropagation to both R and the codebooks

Compared to RQ-VAE, this adds a rotation step that makes the quantization directions of each codebook layer orthogonal, reducing redundancy.
</Callout>

### 7.5 Residual Quantization (RQ)

<TechGraph src="/images/posts/rqvae-prerequisite-knowledge/11-rqvae-residual-layers.svg" title="Layer-by-Layer Approximation in RQ-VAE Residual Quantization" caption={'Each layer encodes the "remaining error" from the previous layer—the more layers, the more precise the approximation'} />

**Core idea**: Instead of quantizing the original vector all at once, **quantize the residual layer by layer**.

```python
# Residual quantization process
def residual_quantize(z, codebooks, L=4):
    """
    z: original vector (64 dims)
    codebooks: L codebooks, each with K=1024 codewords of 64 dims
    L: number of quantization layers
    """
    residual = z  # Initial residual = original vector
    codes = []
    quantized_sum = zeros_like(z)

    for layer in range(L):
        # Find the nearest codeword in the current layer's codebook
        k = argmin_i ||residual - codebooks[layer][i]||
        codes.append(k)

        # Accumulate quantized value
        quantized_sum += codebooks[layer][k]

        # Compute new residual = original - quantized so far
        residual = z - quantized_sum
        # Equivalent to: residual = residual - codebooks[layer][k]

    return codes  # [k1, k2, k3, k4] — this is the Semantic ID!

# Numerical example:
# Original z = [0.23, -0.45, 0.87, 0.12]

# Layer 1: nearest codeword codebook1[37] = [0.20, -0.40, 0.90, 0.10]
#   residual r1 = [0.03, -0.05, -0.03, 0.02]  (much smaller than original!)
#   k1 = 37

# Layer 2: quantize r1, nearest codebook2[512] = [0.03, -0.04, -0.02, 0.02]
#   residual r2 = [0.00, -0.01, -0.01, 0.00]  (even smaller!)
#   k2 = 512

# Layer 3: quantize r2, nearest codebook3[891] = [0.00, -0.01, -0.01, 0.00]
#   residual r3 ≈ [0, 0, 0, 0]  (nearly zero → high-precision approximation!)
#   k3 = 891

# Semantic ID = [37, 512, 891]
# Reconstruction: codebook1[37] + codebook2[512] + codebook3[891] ≈ z
```

**RQ vs PQ comparison**:

| Dimension | PQ (Product Quantization) | RQ (Residual Quantization) |
|------|--------------|--------------|
| Split method | Spatial split (segments independent) | Residual split (layer-by-layer approximation) |
| Codeword dimension | d/M (low-dimensional) | d (full-dimensional) |
| Capacity | K^M | K^L |
| Hierarchical structure | None (segments are equal) | Yes (coarse → fine) |
| Semantic interpretability | Hard (each segment is a local feature) | Easy (layer 1 = broad category, layer 2 = subcategory...) |
| SID suitability | Usable but inferior to RQ | Best choice |

**Why does SID choose RQ over PQ?**

RQ naturally possesses a **hierarchical semantic structure**:
- Layer 1 codeword = broad category (sports / beauty / electronics)
- Layer 2 codeword = subcategory (running shoes / basketball shoes / slippers)
- Layer 3 codeword = finer subdivision (Nike running shoes / Adidas running shoes)
- Layer 4 codeword = specific model

This hierarchical structure makes **autoregressive generation natural**—first decide the broad category, then the subcategory, consistent with how humans make decisions.

### 7.6 Codebook Collapse

**Problem definition**: Most codewords are never used. For example, a codebook with K=1024 may have only 50–100 codewords actually assigned to data.

**Why does it happen?**

```python
# Vicious cycle:
# 1. At initialization, some codewords happen to be closer to the data distribution
# 2. These codewords get assigned more data → EMA updates more frequently → positions become more reasonable
# 3. Codewords far from data get no assignments → never updated → never move closer to data
# 4. Result: a few codewords "winner-takes-all", most codewords "starve to death"

# Like a restaurant:
# Good business → good reputation → more customers → better food → virtuous cycle
# Bad business → no one comes → can't improve → no one ever comes → vicious cycle
```

**Methods to solve codebook collapse**:

| Method | Principle | Effect | Scenario |
|--------|-----------|--------|----------|
| **Re-initialization** | Detect dead codewords and replace them with perturbations of active ones | Simple and effective | All scenarios |
| **Sinkhorn balancing** | Use optimal transport to enforce uniform assignment | Works well | When K is not too large |
| **Reduce codeword dimension** | Larger distance differences in low-dimensional space | Treats symptoms, not root cause | Requires trading off collision rate |
| **Temperature annealing** | Use Gumbel-Softmax soft assignment early in training | Effective but complex | End-to-end training |
| **Increase Commitment Loss** | Force encoder outputs to stay close to existing codewords | Simple | Mild collapse |
| **FSQ** | Completely eliminate the codebook | Radical cure | When very high precision is not needed |

### 7.7 Gumbel-Softmax

Use "soft" quantization instead of "hard" quantization during training so that gradients can flow through directly:

```python
# Hard quantization (argmin): non-differentiable
index = argmin_k ||z - e_k||

# Gumbel-Softmax: differentiable approximation
# Turn "pick the nearest" into "softmax with noise"
def gumbel_softmax(distances, temperature=1.0):
    # Add Gumbel noise
    gumbels = -log(-log(uniform(0, 1, shape=distances.shape)))
    logits = -distances / temperature + gumbels
    probs = softmax(logits)  # Soft assignment (every codeword gets a probability)
    return probs

# Temperature control:
# temperature = 10.0 → nearly uniform assignment (very "soft")
# temperature = 1.0  → moderate
# temperature = 0.01 → almost the same as argmin (very "hard")

# Training strategy: temperature annealing
# Start with high temperature (soft, all codewords participate) → ensures gradients flow to all codewords
# Gradually lower temperature (hard, approaching discrete selection) → approaches inference-time behavior
```

### 7.8 FSQ (Finite Scalar Quantization)

**A fundamentally different approach**: don't learn a codebook; discretize each dimension independently.

```python
# FSQ: round each dimension to a finite set of integers
def fsq(z, levels=[8, 8, 8, 8, 8, 8]):
    """
    z: continuous vector [batch, d]  d=6 in this example
    levels: number of quantization levels per dimension
    """
    z_bounded = tanh(z)  # Bound to [-1, 1]
    codes = []
    for i, L in enumerate(levels):
        # Map [-1, 1] to {0, 1, ..., L-1}
        code_i = round((z_bounded[:, i] + 1) / 2 * (L - 1))
        codes.append(code_i)
    return codes

# Capacity = product(levels) = 8^6 = 262,144
# If levels=[32, 32, 32, 32] → 32^4 = 1,048,576 (million-level)
```

**FSQ vs VQ-VAE**：

| Dimension | VQ-VAE | FSQ |
|-----------|--------|-----|
| Codebook | K learnable vectors | None (fixed levels per dimension) |
| Codebook collapse | Serious problem | Does not exist |
| Utilization | Requires Sinkhorn etc. to guarantee | Naturally 100% |
| Expressiveness | High (codewords can be arbitrary vectors) | Limited (just a coordinate grid) |
| Training complexity | Requires STE/EMA/multiple losses | Only round + STE |
| Practical performance | High ceiling | Google evaluations show comparable to VQ-VAE in some scenarios |

<Callout type="warning" title="Limitations of FSQ in Recommendation SID">
In practice, some teams have tried using FSQ for SID: "After hacking on it for half a year, we felt the ceiling was too low."

Possible reasons:
1. Item semantic distributions in recommendation scenarios are highly non-uniform (not suited for a uniform grid)
2. The required capacity is extremely large (billion-level items), requiring very high FSQ dimensionality
3. No learnable codebook → cannot adapt to data distribution → large quantization error

FSQ is better suited for scenarios with "uniform distribution and moderate capacity requirements" (e.g., image tokenization).
</Callout>

### 7.9 Codebook Utilization vs. Collision Rate — The Core Paradox

This is the most critical engineering dilemma in RQ-VAE training:

```python
# Codebook utilization = number of used codewords / total codewords
utilization = len(used_codes) / K

# Collision rate = number of different items mapped to the same SID / total items
# (or: proportion of SIDs with collisions out of total SIDs)
collision_rate = count(items_sharing_same_sid > 1) / total_items
```

**Why does low dimension → high utilization → high collision?**

```
Dimension d=8, K=1024:
- Distance differences between codewords are large in low-dimensional space → hard for one to dominate → utilization 95%
- But low-dimensional space can express few distinct directions → many different items map to the same codeword → collision 30%

Dimension d=128, K=1024:
- Distance degeneracy in high-dimensional space → a few codewords monopolize → utilization 20%
- But once a codeword is used, high-dimensional space has strong discriminative power → collision 1%
```

**Empirical sweet spot values**:

```python
# Classic configuration (OneRec level):
# Layers L=4, K=1024 per layer, dimension d=32
# Capacity: 1024^4 ≈ 10^12 (far exceeds billions of items)
# Utilization target: 70-90%
# Collision rate target: < 5%

# The monitoring dashboard must display all of the following simultaneously:
# 1. Utilization (too low → wasting capacity; too high → dimension may be too small)
# 2. Collision rate (must not exceed 5%)
# 3. Reconstruction error (must keep decreasing)
# 4. Per-layer residual norms (must decrease layer by layer)
```

### 7.10 Connection to the Target Article

| Concept you just learned | How it manifests in RQ-VAE training |
|--------------------------|-------------------------------------|
| K-Means | Each layer of RQ is essentially a K-Means |
| PQ vs RQ | SID chooses RQ because it has hierarchical structure |
| OPQ | RQOPQ improves quantization quality via rotation matrices |
| Codebook collapse | The #1 enemy of RQ-VAE training |
| Sinkhorn | The most commonly used anti-collapse strategy |
| FSQ | Codebook-free approach; eliminates collapse but has a limited ceiling |
| Gumbel-Softmax | Differentiable quantization substitute for end-to-end training |
| Utilization vs. collision rate | Core engineering trade-off; must be monitored simultaneously |

---

## Part 8: RQ-VAE and Semantic ID — The Core Engine of Generative Recommendation

<TechGraph src="/images/posts/rqvae-prerequisite-knowledge/10-generative-recsys-pipeline.svg" title="Complete Generative Recommendation Pipeline" caption="SID assignment → autoregressive training → Beam Search inference — a three-stage end-to-end system" />

### 8.1 The Complete Architecture of RQ-VAE

Combining all the concepts from before:

```python
class RQVAE:
    def __init__(self, input_dim, code_dim=32, num_codes=1024, num_layers=4):
        self.encoder = MLP(input_dim, code_dim)  # Input features → continuous vector
        self.decoder = MLP(code_dim, input_dim)  # Quantized vector → reconstruction
        self.codebooks = [Codebook(num_codes, code_dim) for _ in range(num_layers)]

    def encode(self, x):
        """x: item multimodal features"""
        z_e = self.encoder(x)  # → [batch, code_dim=32]
        return z_e

    def quantize(self, z_e):
        """Residual quantization: layer-by-layer quantization"""
        residual = z_e
        codes = []
        z_q_sum = torch.zeros_like(z_e)

        for layer, codebook in enumerate(self.codebooks):
            # Find the nearest codeword for the current residual
            distances = torch.cdist(residual, codebook.weight)
            code = distances.argmin(dim=1)  # [batch]
            z_q = codebook(code)            # [batch, code_dim]

            codes.append(code)
            z_q_sum = z_q_sum + z_q
            residual = z_e - z_q_sum  # Update residual

        return codes, z_q_sum  # codes = SID, z_q_sum = quantized vector

    def decode(self, z_q_sum):
        """Reconstruct original features"""
        return self.decoder(z_q_sum)

    def forward(self, x):
        z_e = self.encode(x)
        codes, z_q_sum = self.quantize(z_e)
        x_hat = self.decode(z_q_sum)
        return x_hat, z_e, z_q_sum, codes
```

### 8.2 Training Losses of RQ-VAE

```python
def rqvae_loss(x, x_hat, z_e, z_q_sum, codebooks, codes, beta=0.25):
    # 1. Reconstruction loss: decoding quality
    L_recon = F.mse_loss(x_hat, x)

    # 2. VQ loss: move codewords toward encoder output (used with EMA)
    L_vq = 0
    residual = z_e.detach()
    z_q_acc = torch.zeros_like(z_e)
    for layer, (codebook, code) in enumerate(zip(codebooks, codes)):
        z_q = codebook(code)
        L_vq += F.mse_loss(residual, z_q)
        z_q_acc = z_q_acc + z_q
        residual = z_e.detach() - z_q_acc

    # 3. Commitment loss: pull encoder output toward codewords
    L_commit = F.mse_loss(z_e, z_q_sum.detach())

    # 4. (Optional) Diversity loss: encourage codebook utilization
    # L_div = -entropy(code_distribution)

    return L_recon + L_vq + beta * L_commit
```

**The tug-of-war among the three losses**:

```
L_recon ↓ → needs more precise quantization → codebook must be large / dimension must be high → may collapse
L_vq ↓ → codewords move toward z_e → but if z_e is changing → codewords are always chasing
L_commit ↓ → z_e moves toward codewords → but this may hurt reconstruction quality

All three cannot be optimal simultaneously → need to tune beta and training strategy to balance
```

### 8.3 The Generative Recommendation Pipeline with Semantic IDs

The complete end-to-end workflow:

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Stage 1: SID Assignment (Offline, periodically retrained)       │
│                                                                   │
│ Item multimodal features → Encoder → z_e → RQ-VAE quantize → SID [k1,k2,k3,k4]│
│ (image+text+attributes)   (MLP)   (32-dim) (4 layers×1024) (4 codeword indices)│
│                                                                   │
│ Output: SID Table {item_id → [k1, k2, k3, k4]}                   │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                              ↓
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Stage 2: Autoregressive Model Training (Offline)                  │
│                                                                   │
│ Training data: User behavior sequences (converted to SIDs)        │
│ [SID_A, SID_B, SID_C, ...] → predict → SID_next                 │
│                                                                   │
│ Model: Transformer Decoder (SASRec variant)                       │
│ Input: [k1_A, k2_A, k3_A, k4_A, k1_B, k2_B, ..., SEP]         │
│ Output: P(k1_next), P(k2_next|k1), P(k3_next|k1,k2), P(k4|...) │
│                                                                   │
│ Loss: Per-layer cross-entropy (exactly like GPT's next token prediction)│
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                              ↓
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ Stage 3: Online Inference (Beam Search Generation)                │
│                                                                   │
│ 1. Input the SID sequence of the user's recent behaviors          │
│ 2. Transformer autoregressive generation → Beam Search keeps top-B paths│
│    Layer by layer: k1 → k2 → k3 → k4                            │
│ 3. Generated SIDs → reverse index → corresponding items           │
│ 4. Candidate items → sent to ranking model for scoring            │
│                                                                   │
│ Latency: ~5-20ms (depends on beam width and sequence length)      │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

### 8.4 Technical Evolution of Generative Recommendation

| Year | Paper | Contribution | Key Innovation |
|------|-------|--------------|----------------|
| 2022 | **DSI** (Google) | Pioneered document ID generation | Use Transformer to directly generate doc IDs |
| 2023 | **TIGER** (NeurIPS) | RQ-VAE + Transformer recommendation | First systematic formulation of SID |
| 2024 | **OneRec** (Kuaishou) | Industrial full-pipeline deployment | Multimodal pretraining + SID + end-to-end |
| 2025 | **OneSearch** (Kuaishou) | Unified search + recommendation | RQOPQ + unified generative approach |
| 2025 | **Spotify SID** | Music recommendation | RQ-VAE applied to the audio domain |

#### DSI (Differentiable Search Index, Google 2022)

**Pioneering idea**: Skip the index entirely and let the model directly "memorize" every document's ID, using generation for retrieval.

```python
# DSI training:
# Input: user query
# Output: ID of the relevant document (a sequence of digits)

# Problem: Document IDs are arbitrarily assigned random numbers with no semantics
# Solution: Use hierarchical clustering to assign similar documents to nearby IDs → semantic IDs

# Limitation: Clustering quality is critical but hard to guarantee; updating IDs requires retraining the model
```

#### TIGER (2023, NeurIPS)

**Core contribution**: Learn Semantic IDs with RQ-VAE + autoregressive generation with Transformer.

```python
# TIGER's key improvements:
# 1. SIDs are not manually designed but learned from data via RQ-VAE
# 2. SIDs naturally have hierarchical structure (due to RQ's residual property)
# 3. New items only need a single RQ-VAE forward pass to get a SID (vs DSI requiring retraining)
```

#### OneRec (Kuaishou 2024)

**Industrial-grade full-pipeline system**:

```python
# OneRec's complete tech stack:
# 1. Multimodal large model (video + cover + title + tags) → item embedding
# 2. RQ-VAE → Semantic ID (4 layers, K=8192 per layer)
# 3. Transformer (SASRec variant, 12 layers, d=512) → autoregressive generation
# 4. Beam Search (beam=200) → top-200 SIDs
# 5. SID → item → ranking model

# Key engineering decisions:
# - Codebook size K=8192 (much larger than TIGER's 256) → reduces collisions
# - 4 layers → capacity 8192^4 ≈ 4.5 × 10^15
# - Multimodal input (not just behavioral collaborative signals)
# - Sinkhorn to balance codebook utilization
```

### 8.5 Core Value of Semantic IDs

To summarize once more, the core problems SID solves:

| Problem | Traditional ID Embedding Approach | Semantic ID Approach |
|---------|----------------------------------|---------------------|
| Cold start | New item gets random embedding | Pass through RQ-VAE to get a meaningful SID immediately |
| Generalization | Each item is independent, no sharing | Similar items share higher-level codewords |
| Vocabulary explosion | Millions of independent parameters | K×L codeword parameters (drastically fewer) |
| Scaling Law | Does not exist | Theoretically possible |
| Cross-modal | Separate ID per modality | Unified semantic space |
| Interpretability | Vectors are uninterpretable | Hierarchical structure carries semantics |

### 8.6 Current Open Challenges

```
1. Pareto optimum between codebook utilization vs. collision rate
   → Currently found by tuning hyperparameters; lacking theoretical guidance

2. Stability of end-to-end training vs. freeze
   → Ads scenarios don't dare use end-to-end; recommendation scenarios are experimenting

3. SID quality for long-tail items
   → For items with sparse behavioral data, are SIDs reliable?

4. SID timeliness
   → If item features change (e.g., price drop promotion), does the SID need updating?

5. Multi-task / multi-scenario unification
   → Can a single SID serve both recommendation and search? (OneSearch is exploring this)

6. Tokenization of ads business features
   → Can non-semantic features like bid, budget, and targeting also be SID-ified?
```

### 8.7 Key Hyperparameters in Training Practice

```python
# RQ-VAE training hyperparameters (reference: OneRec/TIGER scale)
config = {
    "encoder": {
        "input_dim": 768,     # CLIP/BERT embedding dimension
        "hidden_dims": [512, 256],
        "output_dim": 32,     # Codeword dimension (sweet spot usually 32-64)
    },
    "quantizer": {
        "num_layers": 4,      # Number of RQ layers
        "num_codes": 1024,    # Codebook size per layer (industrial: 4096-8192)
        "ema_decay": 0.99,    # EMA decay rate
        "commitment_beta": 0.25,
    },
    "training": {
        "batch_size": 4096,
        "learning_rate": 1e-4,
        "warmup_steps": 1000,
        "total_steps": 100000,
        "sinkhorn_iters": 3,  # Number of Sinkhorn balancing iterations
        "sinkhorn_temperature": 0.1,
        "dead_code_threshold": 10,  # Re-initialize if unused for N consecutive batches
    }
}
```

    You should now be able to understand every concept in it without difficulty

## Part 9: Information Theory Fundamentals — Understanding the Mathematical Foundations of Quantization

### 9.1 Why We Need Information Theory

In Part 7 you encountered concepts like "codebook capacity," "information bottleneck," and "rate-distortion." These all come from information theory. Without understanding information theory, you cannot answer fundamental questions like "how large should the codebook actually be" or "how high should the dimensionality actually be."

### 9.2 Information Content and Entropy

**Information Content**:

The information content of an event is proportional to how "surprising" it is.

```python
# Information content = -log2(P(event))
# The more unlikely an event that occurs → the greater the information content

# Examples:
I("sun rises tomorrow") = -log2(0.9999) ≈ 0.0001 bits  # almost no information
I("earth explodes tomorrow") = -log2(0.0001) ≈ 13.3 bits     # huge information content
I("coin lands heads")   = -log2(0.5)    = 1 bit          # 1 bit of information
```

**Entropy**: The "average information content" of a random variable—a measure of uncertainty.

```python
# Entropy H(X) = -sum(P(x) * log2(P(x)))

# Example: rolling a die
H(fair die) = -6 * (1/6 * log2(1/6)) = log2(6) ≈ 2.58 bits
H(loaded die, P=[0.9,0.02,0.02,0.02,0.02,0.02]) ≈ 0.65 bits

# Intuition: the more uniform the distribution → higher entropy → harder to predict → more bits needed to encode
# The more concentrated the distribution → lower entropy → easy to predict → fewer bits needed
```

### 9.3 The Relationship Between Entropy and Codebooks

```python
# Codebook size K=1024 → need log2(1024) = 10 bits to represent one codeword index
# 4 layers of RQ → 4 * 10 = 40 bits total information budget

# But if codebook utilization is only 20% → actually only 200 codewords are used
# Actual information = log2(200) ≈ 7.6 bits (far less than the theoretical upper bound of 10 bits!)

# Codebook utilization directly determines actual information capacity:
# Utilization 100%: effective bits = log2(K) = 10 bits/layer
# Utilization 50%:  effective bits = log2(K/2) = 9 bits/layer
# Utilization 10%:  effective bits = log2(K/10) ≈ 6.7 bits/layer

# A more precise measure: entropy of the codeword assignment
H_codebook = -sum(P(k) * log2(P(k)))  # P(k) = proportion of assignments to codeword k
# Uniform assignment: H = log2(K) = maximum value (optimal!)
# Extremely skewed: H << log2(K) (information wasted)
```

### 9.4 Rate-Distortion Theory

Shannon's rate-distortion theory gives the theoretical limit for "encoding continuous data with a finite number of bits."

```python
# Rate-Distortion Function R(D):
# Given a maximum allowable distortion D, what is the minimum number of bits needed?

# R(D) gives an impossible-to-break lower bound:
# If you only use R bits, the distortion is at least D
# The (Rate, Distortion) of any real system must lie on or above the R(D) curve

# Implications for RQ-VAE:
# Rate = total bits of the SID = L * log2(K) (e.g., 4*10 = 40 bits)
# Distortion = reconstruction error ||x - x_hat||^2

# Increasing layers L or codebook size K → Rate increases → allows lower Distortion
# But constrained by: codebook collapse makes effective Rate far lower than theoretical
```

### 9.5 Mutual Information and Representation Learning

**Mutual Information I(X; Y)**: How much does knowing Y reduce the uncertainty of X.

```python
# I(X; Y) = H(X) - H(X|Y)
# Equivalently: I(X; Y) = H(X) + H(Y) - H(X,Y)

# In RQ-VAE:
# X = original item features
# Y = Semantic ID [k1, k2, k3, k4]
# I(X; Y) = how much information about the original features the SID retains

# Good SID: I(X; Y) close to H(Y) (every bit of the SID carries useful information)
# Bad SID: I(X; Y) << H(Y) (the SID contains a lot of redundancy/noise)
```

**Information Bottleneck**:

```python
# Goal: find a representation Z such that:
# max I(Z; Y)  (Z retains as much information about the target as possible)
# s.t. I(Z; X) <= R  (Z uses only a limited number of bits to encode X)

# VQ-VAE's information bottleneck:
# X = original item features (high-dimensional continuous)
# Z = quantized SID (low-dimensional discrete)
# Y = whether the user clicks (downstream target)

# Ideal SID: uses the fewest bits to retain the most predictive information about "will the user click"
# Reality: we usually only optimize reconstruction (retaining information about X), not directly optimizing prediction of Y
# This is the motivation for "end-to-end training" — directly optimizing I(Z; Y) rather than I(Z; X)
```

### 9.6 KL Divergence and Distribution Distance

```python
# KL(P || Q) = sum(P(x) * log(P(x) / Q(x)))
# Measures the information loss of "using Q to approximate P"

# Properties:
# KL >= 0 (always non-negative)
# KL = 0 if and only if P = Q
# KL is asymmetric: KL(P||Q) != KL(Q||P)

# In VAE:
# KL(q(z|x) || p(z)) → how much the encoding distribution q deviates from the prior p
# Minimizing this → makes the encoding distribution close to standard normal

# In Sinkhorn codebook balancing:
# KL(actual codeword assignment || uniform assignment) → degree of deviation from uniform
# Minimizing this → codewords are used uniformly
```

### 9.7 The Information-Theoretic Interpretation of Cross-Entropy

```python
# Cross-entropy H(P, Q) = -sum(P(x) * log(Q(x)))
# = H(P) + KL(P || Q)

# When P is the true distribution (labels) and Q is the model prediction:
# Minimizing cross-entropy ⟺ Minimizing KL(P||Q) ⟺ Making model predictions close to ground truth

# Cross-entropy for CTR models:
# P = {0, 1} (ground truth labels: clicked / not clicked)
# Q = sigmoid(model_output) (model's predicted probability)
# H(P, Q) = -(y*log(q) + (1-y)*log(1-q))

# Cross-entropy for SID autoregressive models:
# P = one_hot(true_code_index) (ground truth codeword)
# Q = softmax(transformer_output) (model's predicted probability distribution)
# H(P, Q) = -log(Q[true_code_index])
```

### 9.8 Connecting to the Target Article

| Concept You Just Learned | How It Manifests in RQ-VAE Training |
|--------------------------|-------------------------------------|
| Entropy | Entropy of codebook assignment = effective information capacity |
| Rate-Distortion | Layers × log2(K) determines the theoretical capacity upper bound |
| Mutual Information | A good SID = maximizing mutual information with the downstream target |
| Information Bottleneck | RQ-VAE is an information bottleneck system |
| KL Divergence | The essence of Sinkhorn optimization is minimizing KL with the uniform distribution |
| Cross-Entropy | The training objective of the SID generative model |

## Part 10: Deep Dive into CTR Models — From Mathematical Formulas to Industrial Implementation

### 10.1 Full Derivation of Logistic Regression

LR is the starting point for understanding all CTR models. Although simple, many core concepts (sigmoid, cross-entropy, feature crossing) originate here.

```python
# Model definition
# P(click=1 | x) = sigmoid(w^T x + b) = 1 / (1 + exp(-(w^T x + b)))

# Why use sigmoid?
# 1. Output is in (0, 1) → can be interpreted as a probability
# 2. When combined with cross-entropy loss, the gradient has a clean form: dL/dw = (y_hat - y) * x
# 3. Log-odds are linear: log(P/(1-P)) = w^T x + b

# LR in industrial practice:
# Number of features: millions to billions (massive one-hot + cross features)
# Training: FTRL (Follow-The-Regularized-Leader) online learning
# Advantages: fast training, interpretable, supports real-time updates
# Disadvantages: linear model, requires extensive manual feature crossing
```

**FTRL Online Learning** (standard in ads systems):

```python
# FTRL: the standard training algorithm for LR in industry
# Updates with every new sample, no need to accumulate batches

# Core formula:
# w_{t+1} = argmin_w (g_{1:t} · w + 1/2 * sum(sigma_s * ||w - w_s||^2) + lambda1*|w| + lambda2*||w||^2)

# Key properties:
# 1. Produces sparse solutions (L1 regularization drives unimportant feature weights to 0)
# 2. Real-time updates (one update per sample)
# 3. Memory-friendly (only needs to store non-zero weights)
# 4. Suited for CTR prediction with billions of sparse features
```

### 10.2 Mathematical Derivation of FM

The core innovation of FM: using vector dot products to approximate the weights of feature interactions.

```python
# Second-order FM formula:
# y = w0 + sum_i(w_i * x_i) + sum_i sum_{j>i} (v_i · v_j) * x_i * x_j

# Key insight:
# Traditional approach: w_ij * x_i * x_j → requires n^2 parameters, and sparse feature combinations are hard to train
# FM approach: (v_i · v_j) * x_i * x_j → only needs n*k parameters (k=embedding dim)
# v_i: the k-dimensional embedding of feature i

# Why can FM handle sparsity?
# Interaction weight w_ij = v_i · v_j
# Even if features i and j have never co-occurred, as long as v_i and v_j have each been trained individually
# Their dot product gives a reasonable estimate of the interaction weight

# Computational complexity optimization (from O(kn^2) to O(kn)):
# sum_i sum_{j>i} (v_i · v_j) * x_i * x_j
# = 0.5 * (||sum_i(v_i*x_i)||^2 - sum_i(||v_i||^2 * x_i^2))
# i.e., first compute the square of the weighted vector sum, then subtract the sum of individual squares
```

**Numerical Example**:

```python
# 3 features, embedding dimension k=2
# x = [1, 0, 1] (features 0 and 2 are activated)
# v_0 = [0.3, 0.5], v_1 = [0.1, 0.8], v_2 = [0.4, 0.6]

# Cross term (only looking at activated features 0 and 2):
# (v_0 · v_2) * x_0 * x_2 = (0.3*0.4 + 0.5*0.6) * 1 * 1 = 0.42

# Using the optimized formula:
# sum_v = v_0*1 + v_2*1 = [0.7, 1.1]
# ||sum_v||^2 = 0.49 + 1.21 = 1.7
# sum_sq = ||v_0||^2*1 + ||v_2||^2*1 = 0.34 + 0.52 = 0.86
# Cross term = 0.5 * (1.7 - 0.86) = 0.42  ✓
```

### 10.3 Architecture Details of DeepFM

```python
class DeepFM:
    def __init__(self, field_dims, embed_dim=16, mlp_dims=[256, 128, 64]):
        # FM part
        self.embedding = FeaturesEmbedding(field_dims, embed_dim)
        self.linear = FeaturesLinear(field_dims)  # First-order terms
        self.fm = FM_Interaction()  # Second-order interactions

        # Deep part
        self.mlp = MLP(num_fields * embed_dim, mlp_dims)

    def forward(self, x):
        # x: [batch, num_fields] IDs for each feature field
        embed = self.embedding(x)  # [batch, num_fields, embed_dim]

        # FM part
        linear_out = self.linear(x)     # [batch, 1] first-order
        fm_out = self.fm(embed)          # [batch, 1] second-order interactions

        # Deep part
        deep_input = embed.flatten(1)    # [batch, num_fields * embed_dim]
        deep_out = self.mlp(deep_input)  # [batch, 1] higher-order

        # Combine
        logit = linear_out + fm_out + deep_out  # [batch, 1]
        return sigmoid(logit)
```

**Why is DeepFM better than a plain DNN?**

In theory a DNN can learn any interaction, but in practice:
- For low-order interactions (e.g., "gender × ad category"), a DNN needs many layers to learn them
- FM explicitly models low-order interactions, providing a "shortcut"
- Deep handles more complex higher-order patterns that FM cannot express
- The two are complementary: FM provides precise low-order signals, Deep provides generalized higher-order signals

### 10.4 DCN-V2 (Deep & Cross Network)

The core of DCN: the Cross Network explicitly models high-order feature interactions.

```python
class CrossNetwork:
    """Each layer introduces one additional order of feature interaction"""
    def __init__(self, input_dim, num_layers=3):
        self.W = [Linear(input_dim, input_dim) for _ in range(num_layers)]
        self.b = [zeros(input_dim) for _ in range(num_layers)]

    def forward(self, x0):
        x_l = x0
        for layer in range(self.num_layers):
            # DCN-V2: x_{l+1} = x_0 ⊙ (W_l x_l + b_l) + x_l
            x_l = x0 * (self.W[layer](x_l) + self.b[layer]) + x_l
        return x_l

# Mathematical analysis:
# Layer 0: x_0 (original input)
# Layer 1: x_0 * (W_1 x_0 + b_1) + x_0 → contains 2nd-order interactions
# Layer 2: x_0 * (W_2 x_1 + b_2) + x_1 → contains 3rd-order interactions
# Layer L: → contains (L+1)-order interactions

# Key insight: An L-layer Cross Network explicitly models all interactions up to order (L+1)
# While an L-layer DNN can only theoretically approximate them, with no guarantee of actually learning them
```

### 10.5 Detailed Look at DIN's Attention Mechanism

DIN's core insight: among a user's historical behaviors, different items have different relevance to the current candidate.

```python
class DIN_Attention:
    """Target-Aware Attention"""
    def __init__(self, embed_dim):
        # Attention MLP
        self.attn_mlp = MLP(
            input_dim=embed_dim * 4,  # [behavior, candidate, behavior*candidate, behavior-candidate]
            hidden_dims=[64, 32],
            output_dim=1
        )

    def forward(self, candidate_embed, behavior_embeds, behavior_mask):
        """
        candidate_embed: [batch, embed_dim] current candidate ad
        behavior_embeds: [batch, max_seq_len, embed_dim] user history
        behavior_mask: [batch, max_seq_len] which positions are valid
        """
        # Expand candidate ad to the same length as history
        candidate_expand = candidate_embed.unsqueeze(1).expand_as(behavior_embeds)

        # Construct attention input: [behavior, candidate, element-wise product, element-wise difference]
        attn_input = torch.cat([
            behavior_embeds,
            candidate_expand,
            behavior_embeds * candidate_expand,  # Interaction term
            behavior_embeds - candidate_expand,  # Difference term
        ], dim=-1)  # [batch, seq_len, embed_dim * 4]

        # Compute attention weights
        attn_scores = self.attn_mlp(attn_input).squeeze(-1)  # [batch, seq_len]
        attn_scores = attn_scores.masked_fill(~behavior_mask, -1e9)
        attn_weights = softmax(attn_scores, dim=-1)  # [batch, seq_len]

        # Weighted sum
        user_interest = (attn_weights.unsqueeze(-1) * behavior_embeds).sum(dim=1)
        return user_interest  # [batch, embed_dim]
```

**Relationship between DIN and Transformer Attention**:

| Dimension | DIN Attention | Transformer Self-Attention |
|------|-------------|--------------------------|
| Query | Candidate ad (single) | Every position is a query |
| Key/Value | User historical behaviors | The entire sequence |
| Similarity function | MLP(concat) | Dot product / sqrt(d) |
| Output | A single user interest vector | New representation for each position |
| Complexity | O(seq_len) | O(seq_len^2) |

### 10.6 Multi-Task Learning (MTL) in Advertising

An ad system doesn't just predict CTR — it needs to simultaneously predict multiple objectives:

```python
# Multi-task objectives in ad systems:
# pCTR: Click probability
# pCVR: Conversion probability (download/purchase)
# pLTV: User lifetime value
# Watch duration (video ads)
# Negative feedback probability (close/report)

# eCPM ranking:
# eCPM = pCTR × pCVR × Bid × quality_factor
# Or more complex: eCPM = pCTR × f(pCVR, pLTV, watch_duration, ...)
```

**MMOE (Multi-gate Mixture of Experts)**：

```python
class MMOE:
    def __init__(self, input_dim, num_experts=8, num_tasks=3, expert_dim=128):
        # Shared expert networks
        self.experts = [MLP(input_dim, expert_dim) for _ in range(num_experts)]
        # Each task has its own gate
        self.gates = [Linear(input_dim, num_experts) for _ in range(num_tasks)]
        # Each task has its own tower
        self.towers = [MLP(expert_dim, 1) for _ in range(num_tasks)]

    def forward(self, x):
        # All experts process the input
        expert_outs = [expert(x) for expert in self.experts]  # 8 × [batch, 128]
        expert_outs = torch.stack(expert_outs, dim=1)  # [batch, 8, 128]

        outputs = []
        for task_id in range(self.num_tasks):
            # This task's gate decides which experts to use
            gate_weights = softmax(self.gates[task_id](x), dim=-1)  # [batch, 8]
            # Weighted mixture of expert outputs
            task_input = (gate_weights.unsqueeze(-1) * expert_outs).sum(dim=1)
            # Task tower outputs prediction
            outputs.append(sigmoid(self.towers[task_id](task_input)))

        return outputs  # [pCTR, pCVR, pLTV]
```

**Why is multi-task better than single-task?**
- Shared low-level feature representations → higher data efficiency (even sparse CVR samples can leverage the large volume of CTR data)
- Regularization effect → tasks constrain each other, preventing overfitting on any single task
- But task conflicts need to be handled (some users click a lot on ads but never purchase)

### 10.7 ESMM: Solving Sample Selection Bias

```python
# Problems with traditional CVR models:
# CVR is trained only on "clicked samples" → Sample Selection Bias
# Because clicked samples have been "filtered" by the CTR model → distribution doesn't represent the full population

# ESMM's solution:
# Definition: pCTCVR = pCTR × pCVR (over all impression samples)
# Train pCTR and pCTCVR on the full set of impression samples
# pCVR = pCTCVR / pCTR (implicitly learned)

class ESMM:
    def forward(self, x):
        pCTR = self.ctr_tower(self.shared_embedding(x))
        pCVR = self.cvr_tower(self.shared_embedding(x))
        pCTCVR = pCTR * pCVR  # Multiplicative relationship

        return pCTR, pCVR, pCTCVR

    def loss(self, pCTR, pCTCVR, click_label, convert_label):
        # CTR is trained on all impressions
        loss_ctr = bce(pCTR, click_label)
        # CTCVR is also trained on all impressions (conversion labels are defined over the full set)
        loss_ctcvr = bce(pCTCVR, convert_label)
        return loss_ctr + loss_ctcvr
        # Note: pCVR has no direct loss; it is learned indirectly through pCTCVR
```

### 10.8 Feature Engineering Best Practices

Although deep models reduce the need for manual features, feature engineering remains extremely important in industrial CTR systems:

```python
# Typical feature taxonomy in a production ads system:

user_features = {
    # Basic attributes
    "age_bucket": "25-30",
    "gender": "male",
    "city_level": "tier1",

    # Statistical features (the most important!)
    "7d_ctr": 0.045,           # Overall CTR over the past 7 days
    "7d_category_ctrs": {...},  # CTR per category
    "session_depth": 12,        # Number of items already viewed in the current session
    "last_click_time_gap": 300, # Seconds since last click

    # Sequence features
    "recent_click_items": [...],    # Last 50 clicks
    "recent_click_categories": [...],
}

item_features = {
    # Basic attributes
    "category": "sports",
    "price": 299.0,
    "brand": "nike",

    # Statistical features
    "7d_ctr": 0.032,           # This item's CTR over the past 7 days
    "7d_cvr": 0.008,           # This item's CVR over the past 7 days
    "total_clicks": 15234,      # Total historical clicks
    "freshness": 3,             # Days since listing

    # Content features (input sources for SID)
    "image_embedding": [...],   # Image features extracted by CNN
    "title_embedding": [...],   # Title features extracted by BERT
}

context_features = {
    "hour": 22,
    "weekday": 5,
    "device": "iphone",
    "network": "wifi",
    "position": 3,             # Display position (very important!)
}
```

### 10.9 Position Bias and Debiasing

Display position has a huge impact on click-through rate—the CTR at position 1 can be 5× that of position 10, not because the content is better, but because users look at position 1 first.

```python
# Handling Position Bias:

# Method 1: Position as a feature (simplest)
# Training: include position as a feature field
# Inference: fix position=1 or remove the position feature
# Problem: doesn't truly eliminate the bias

# Method 2: PAL (Position-Aware Learning)
# pCTR = pRelevance × pExamine(position)
# pExamine: probability the user sees this position (depends only on position)
# pRelevance: the item's true relevance (depends on content)
# Train both models jointly; use only pRelevance at inference time

# Method 3: IPW (Inverse Propensity Weighting)
# Weight samples by 1/P(examine|position)
# Samples in good positions get lower weight (they'd be seen regardless of content)
# Samples in poor positions get higher weight (a click implies genuine relevance)
```
