## Part 11: Advanced Transformer Topics — From Fundamentals to the Frontier

### 11.1 KV Cache

The core optimization for autoregressive generation. Without KV Cache, generating each new token requires recomputing K and V for all positions — wasting a huge amount of compute.

```python
# Without KV Cache:
# Generating the 5th token: need to compute Q,K,V for positions 1,2,3,4,5
# Generating the 6th token: need to compute Q,K,V for positions 1,2,3,4,5,6
# K,V for positions 1-5 are redundantly recomputed!

# With KV Cache:
# Generating the 5th token: only compute Q,K,V for position 5; read K,V for positions 1-4 from cache
# Generating the 6th token: only compute Q,K,V for position 6; read K,V for positions 1-5 from cache

class KVCacheAttention:
    def __init__(self):
        self.key_cache = []    # Store historical K
        self.value_cache = []  # Store historical V

    def forward(self, x_new, use_cache=True):
        # Only compute Q, K, V for the new position
        q_new = x_new @ W_Q
        k_new = x_new @ W_K
        v_new = x_new @ W_V

        if use_cache:
            # Append new K,V to the cache
            self.key_cache.append(k_new)
            self.value_cache.append(v_new)
            # Use the full K,V sequence for attention
            K = concat(self.key_cache)
            V = concat(self.value_cache)
        else:
            K, V = k_new, v_new

        # Q is only the new position; K/V spans the entire history
        scores = q_new @ K.T / sqrt(d)
        output = softmax(scores) @ V
        return output
```

**KV Cache in SID Generation**:

```python
# SID generation [k1, k2, k3, k4]:
# Generating k1: only user history KV (cache established)
# Generating k2: user history + k1 KV (incremental update)
# Generating k3: user history + k1 + k2 KV (incremental update)
# Generating k4: user history + k1 + k2 + k3 KV (incremental update)

# Speedup: the longer the sequence, the more significant the acceleration
# 100 token history + 4-layer SID → ~25x speedup
```

### 11.2 GQA (Grouped Query Attention)

The K and V in Multi-Head Attention consume a large amount of memory (especially with long sequences). GQA lets multiple Query Heads share the same set of K and V:

```python
# Standard MHA: 32 Q heads, 32 K heads, 32 V heads
# GQA:          32 Q heads, 8 K heads, 8 V heads
# MQA:          32 Q heads, 1 K head, 1 V head

# GQA: every 4 Q heads share 1 set of KV
# KV Cache size reduced by 4x, inference speed improved, accuracy nearly unaffected
```

### 11.3 FlashAttention

Standard Attention requires an O(N^2) intermediate matrix (attention scores). When sequence length N > 4096, memory becomes a severe bottleneck.

```python
# Standard implementation (requires O(N^2) memory):
scores = Q @ K.T          # [N, N] matrix
weights = softmax(scores)  # [N, N] matrix
output = weights @ V       # [N, d]

# FlashAttention (IO-aware tiled computation):
# Does not actually store the N×N attention matrix
# Completes computation in SRAM via tiling and online softmax
# Memory: O(N) instead of O(N^2)
# Speed: 2-4x faster (fewer HBM reads/writes)
```

### 11.4 Mixture of Experts (MoE)

Uses multiple "experts" in the Transformer's FFN layer, activating only a few of them for each token:

```python
class MoE_FFN:
    def __init__(self, d_model, num_experts=8, top_k=2):
        self.experts = [FFN(d_model) for _ in range(num_experts)]
        self.gate = Linear(d_model, num_experts)
        self.top_k = top_k

    def forward(self, x):
        # Gate decides which experts to activate
        gate_logits = self.gate(x)  # [batch, seq_len, num_experts]
        top_k_values, top_k_indices = topk(gate_logits, self.top_k)
        gate_weights = softmax(top_k_values, dim=-1)

        # Only compute the selected experts
        output = zeros_like(x)
        for i, expert in enumerate(self.experts):
            mask = (top_k_indices == i).any(dim=-1)
            if mask.any():
                output[mask] += gate_weights[..., i:i+1][mask] * expert(x[mask])

        return output

# Effect: 8 experts but only 2 used each time
# Parameter count: 8x FFN (large model capacity)
# Compute cost: ~2x FFN (high efficiency)
# Representatives: Mixtral 8x7B, Switch Transformer, GPT-4 (rumored)
```

**MoE in Recommendation Systems**:

Different user segments (high-activity / low-activity, male / female, young / older) may need different "experts" to understand them. MMOE is essentially the MoE philosophy applied to recommendation scenarios.

### 11.5 Long Sequence Modeling

User behavior sequences in recommendation systems can be extremely long (thousands to tens of thousands of interactions). The standard Transformer's O(N^2) complexity is infeasible.

```python
# Solutions:

# 1. Truncation: only use the most recent N behaviors (typically N=50-200)
# Simple and effective, but loses long-term interests

# 2. Hierarchical: fine-grained modeling of recent behaviors + coarse-grained representation of long-term interests
# SIM (Search-based Interest Model, Alibaba 2020):
# First filter relevant history by item category → only run attention on the filtered short sequence

# 3. Linformer / Performer: Linear Attention (O(N) complexity)
# Replace the N×N attention matrix with a low-rank approximation

# 4. Sparse Attention: BigBird, Longformer
# Only compute attention over local windows + global tokens (not fully connected)

# 5. Retrieval Augmented:
# Retrieve relevant segments from long-term memory and concatenate them into the short context
```

### 11.6 Transformer Training Tips

```python
# 1. Learning Rate Warmup + Cosine Decay (standard practice)
# warmup_steps = 5-10% of total steps
lr_schedule = WarmupCosineDecay(
    peak_lr=3e-4,
    warmup_steps=4000,
    total_steps=100000,
    min_lr=1e-5
)

# 2. Gradient Accumulation (simulate large batch on small GPUs)
# actual batch=32, accumulate 8 steps → effective batch=256
for step in range(total_steps):
    loss = model(batch) / accumulation_steps
    loss.backward()
    if (step + 1) % accumulation_steps == 0:
        optimizer.step()
        optimizer.zero_grad()

# 3. Mixed Precision Training (FP16/BF16)
# Forward and backward in FP16 (fast, saves memory)
# Parameter updates in FP32 (precise)
# BF16 is more stable than FP16 (more exponent bits, less likely to overflow)

# 4. Gradient Checkpointing (save memory)
# Don't save intermediate layer activations
# Recompute during backward pass → trade time for space
# Memory reduced ~5x, training ~30% slower
```

## Part 12: Industrial Recommendation System Engineering Practice — From Paper to Production

### 12.1 Feature System Architecture

Features in industrial recommendation systems aren't simply queried from a database — it's a complete real-time data system.

```python
# Three-layer architecture for feature storage:

# Layer 1: Real-time features (latency < 100ms)
# Storage: Redis / Tair / in-memory KV
# Updates: stream processing (Flink/Spark Streaming)
# Examples: item IDs from user's last 10 clicks, current session duration
realtime_features = redis.get(f"user:{uid}:realtime")

# Layer 2: Near-real-time features (latency < 10min)
# Storage: HBase / Cassandra
# Updates: micro-batch processing (every 5-10 minutes)
# Examples: user's CTR over the past hour, item's impression count over the past hour
nearline_features = hbase.get(f"user:{uid}:hourly_stats")

# Layer 3: Offline features (latency T+1)
# Storage: Hive/HDFS → Redis/HBase
# Updates: daily offline computation
# Examples: user's 7-day average watch duration, item's historical CTR
offline_features = hbase.get(f"user:{uid}:daily_profile")
```

### 12.2 Model Serving Architecture

```python
# Typical online serving architecture for a recommendation system:

# 1. Retrieval layer (execute multiple retrieval channels in parallel)
recall_results = parallel_execute([
    collaborative_recall(user),      # Collaborative filtering retrieval
    two_tower_recall(user),          # Two-tower + ANN retrieval
    # sid_generative_recall(user),   # SID generative retrieval (new!)
    hot_recall(),                    # Hot/trending retrieval
    tag_recall(user),                # Tag-based retrieval
])
candidates = merge_and_dedup(recall_results)  # ~5000 candidates

# 2. Pre-ranking (lightweight model for quick scoring)
pre_rank_scores = lightweight_model.batch_predict(candidates)
top_candidates = topk(candidates, pre_rank_scores, k=500)

# 3. Ranking (complex deep model)
rank_features = feature_service.batch_get(user, top_candidates)
rank_scores = deep_model.batch_predict(rank_features)  # DeepFM / DCN / BST

# 4. Re-ranking (business rules + diversity)
final_list = reranker.apply([
    diversity_filter,         # Category dispersion
    frequency_cap,           # Frequency capping
    position_bias_adjust,    # Position bias correction
    business_rules,          # Business rules (e.g., boosting new products)
])

# Total latency budget: 50-100ms
# Retrieval: 5-10ms (ANN queries)
# Pre-ranking: 5-10ms (simple model)
# Ranking: 10-20ms (complex model, key bottleneck)
# Re-ranking: 5ms (rules)
# Features: 10-20ms (parallel queries)
```

### 12.3 Model Training Pipeline

```python
# Industrial training pipeline (using ad CTR model as an example):

# Step 1: Sample generation (offline)
# Data sources: impression logs + click logs + conversion logs
# Join: impression ID → whether clicked → whether converted
# Sampling: negative sampling (can't use all negatives when CTR is very low)
# Output: training samples (features + labels)

# Step 2: Feature engineering (offline)
# Statistical features: 7d_ctr, 30d_cvr, freshness, etc.
# Embedding features: item_id, user_id, category_id
# Cross features: user_age_x_category, hour_x_position

# Step 3: Model training
# Incremental training: fine-tune daily with new data (don't train from scratch)
# Full training: retrain weekly/monthly (prevent distribution drift)
# Training framework: TensorFlow / PyTorch + Parameter Server (tens of billions of parameters)

# Step 4: Model validation
# Offline: AUC, GAUC, calibration
# Small traffic: 1% traffic A/B test
# Full rollout: gradually increase 1% → 5% → 20% → 100%

# Step 5: Deployment
# Model Serving: TF Serving / Triton / in-house solution
# Hot update: swap models without downtime
# Rollback: automatic/manual rollback when online metrics are abnormal
```

### 12.4 A/B Testing System

```python
# Core design of A/B testing:

# Traffic bucketing:
# hash(user_id) % 1000 → bucket_id
# Treatment group: bucket 0-9 (1%)
# Control group: bucket 10-19 (1%)
# Main pool: bucket 20-999 (98%)

# Key metrics:
# Primary metrics: CTR, CVR, GMV, retention rate
# Guardrail metrics: negative feedback rate, advertiser satisfaction, DAU
# Statistical test: t-test, p-value < 0.05

# A/B duration: typically 7-14 days (covering both weekdays and weekends)
# Minimum sample size: determined by MDE (minimum detectable effect) and power analysis

# Special considerations for SID experiments:
# 1. SID updates require reassigning all items → full offline computation
# 2. Treatment uses new SID, control uses old approach → ensure fairness
# 3. Need to wait for SID model to "warm up" (new codebook needs a few days to stabilize)
# 4. Cold-start performance needs separate tracking (segment new items and track metrics independently)
```

### 12.5 Model Calibration

The "probability" output by a CTR model isn't necessarily the true probability. Calibration ensures that when the model outputs 0.05, the actual click rate is also 5%.

```python
# Why is calibration needed?
# Because ad eCPM = pCTR × Bid
# If pCTR is systematically too high → advertisers overpay → dissatisfied
# If pCTR is systematically too low → platform earns less → auction inefficiency

# Calibration method 1: Platt Scaling
# Learn a sigmoid: calibrated = sigmoid(a * raw_score + b)
# Fit a, b on the validation set

# Calibration method 2: Isotonic Regression
# Non-parametric method: learn a piecewise monotonic function
# More flexible but prone to overfitting

# Calibration method 3: Binned Calibration
# Divide model outputs into 100 bins
# For each bin: calibrated = actual positive ratio in bin / average predicted value in bin

# Checking calibration: Reliability Diagram
# X-axis: predicted probability (binned)
# Y-axis: actual positive ratio
# Perfect calibration → points lie on the diagonal
```

### 12.6 Sample Delay and Attribution

A special problem in advertising scenarios: a click may not convert until 24 hours later, but model training can't wait that long.

```python
# Conversion delay problem:
# User clicks an ad at 10:00
# User downloads the App at 22:00 (conversion!)
# If this sample is labeled as "no conversion" at 12:00 → label error!

# Solution 1: Waiting window
# Wait T time before finalizing the label (e.g., wait 7 days)
# Problem: data delayed by 7 days, model updates too slowly

# Solution 2: Delayed correction
# Train with short-window labels first, while maintaining a "label correction" module
# When conversions arrive later, update corresponding sample labels, incremental training

# Solution 3: Survival analysis
# Don't predict "whether converted", but predict "the waiting time distribution for conversion"
# P(conversion | already waited t time) = hazard function
```

### 12.7 Real-time Recommendation vs Offline Recommendation

| Dimension | Real-time Recommendation | Offline Recommendation |
|------|---------|---------|
| Feature freshness | Second-level (user real-time behavior) | Day-level (T+1 statistics) |
| Model updates | Online learning / streaming updates | Daily full retraining |
| New item response | Minute-level | Hour-to-day-level |
| Computation method | Triggered per request | Batch pre-computation |
| Applicable scenarios | News feed, search | Push notifications, email recommendations |

**Freshness challenges for SID**:

```python
# SID is assigned offline → new items need to wait for reassignment
# Problem: breaking news/new products need to be recommended immediately

# Solutions:
# 1. High-frequency retraining: update SID every hour/every 4 hours (requires extremely fast RQ-VAE inference)
# 2. Incremental updates: only assign SID to new items, don't change old items (maintain stability)
# 3. Fallback strategy: new items go through non-SID retrieval channels, switch to SID once assigned
```

### 12.8 The Data Flywheel of Recommendation Systems

```
         ┌─────────────────────┐
         │ Better models/SID    │
         └─────────┬───────────┘
                   ↓
         ┌─────────────────────┐
         │ More precise recs    │
         └─────────┬───────────┘
                   ↓
         ┌─────────────────────┐
         │ More user interaction│
         └─────────┬───────────┘
                   ↓
         ┌─────────────────────┐
         │ More training data   │
         └─────────┬───────────┘
                   ↓
         ┌─────────────────────┐
         │ Better models/SID    │ ← Back to the start
         └─────────────────────┘
```

SID accelerates this flywheel:
- New items get good recommendations without needing massive impressions → users discover great content more easily
- User interactions increase → more training data → SID quality improves further

## Part 13: Math Supplement — Core Linear Algebra and Probability Theory

### 13.1 Vector Space Fundamentals

To understand embeddings and vector quantization, you need intuition about vector spaces.

```python
# Basic vector operations
a = [1, 2, 3]
b = [4, 5, 6]

# Addition (add corresponding components)
a + b = [5, 7, 9]

# Scalar multiplication (multiply each component by the scalar)
2 * a = [2, 4, 6]

# Inner product / dot product (multiply corresponding components and sum)
dot(a, b) = 1*4 + 2*5 + 3*6 = 32

# Norm (the "length" of a vector)
||a|| = sqrt(1^2 + 2^2 + 3^2) = sqrt(14) ≈ 3.74

# Cosine similarity (directional similarity, ignoring magnitude)
cos(a, b) = dot(a, b) / (||a|| * ||b||) = 32 / (3.74 * 8.77) ≈ 0.97
```

### 13.2 Distance Metrics

The core operation of vector quantization is "finding the nearest codeword" — and "nearest" requires a distance metric.

```python
# Euclidean distance (L2 distance)
d_euclidean(a, b) = sqrt(sum((a_i - b_i)^2))
# Most commonly used; VQ-VAE uses this by default

# Cosine distance
d_cosine(a, b) = 1 - cos(a, b) = 1 - dot(a,b)/(||a||*||b||)
# Ignores vector magnitude, only compares direction
# More commonly used in NLP embeddings and two-tower models

# Inner product (negative means farther apart)
d_inner(a, b) = -dot(a, b)
# Two-tower model + ANN retrieval typically uses inner product
# Note: inner product is not a true "distance" (does not satisfy the triangle inequality)

# Mahalanobis distance (accounts for feature correlation)
d_mahalanobis(a, b) = sqrt((a-b)^T * Sigma^{-1} * (a-b))
# The rotation matrix in OPQ essentially degenerates Mahalanobis distance into Euclidean distance
```

### 13.3 Matrix Decomposition and Dimensionality Reduction

```python
# SVD (Singular Value Decomposition): the foundation of all matrix decompositions
# A = U * Sigma * V^T
# U: left singular vectors (orthogonal)
# Sigma: singular values (diagonal, sorted from largest to smallest)
# V: right singular vectors (orthogonal)

# Dimensionality reduction: keep only the top k largest singular values
# A_k = U[:,:k] * Sigma[:k,:k] * V[:,:k]^T
# This is the optimal rank-k approximation in the Frobenius norm sense

# Application in recommendations:
# User-item interaction matrix R (millions of users × tens of millions of items)
# SVD: R ≈ U * Sigma * V^T
# U*Sigma^0.5 = user embeddings (k-dimensional)
# V*Sigma^0.5 = item embeddings (k-dimensional)
# → This is the earliest collaborative filtering embedding!
```

### 13.4 Orthogonal Matrices and Rotation

```python
# Orthogonal matrix Q: Q^T * Q = I (transpose equals inverse)
# Properties: preserves inner products → preserves distances and angles → pure rotation/reflection

# In OPQ:
# R is an orthogonal matrix (rotation matrix)
# z_rotated = R * z
# PQ after rotation → equivalent to quantizing in the rotated coordinate system
# Rotation preserves distance relationships between data points → quantization quality unchanged
# But rotation makes the variance across sub-spaces (segments) more uniform → lower PQ error

# Intuition: if data is mainly distributed along the diagonal direction
# Without rotation: split into x-direction and y-direction → both segments contain the diagonal trend → redundant
# With rotation: turn the diagonal direction into the x-direction → first segment captures the main variation, second segment captures residuals → efficient
```

### 13.5 Mathematical Properties of Softmax

Softmax is the most commonly used normalization function in deep learning. Understanding its properties is essential for understanding Attention, Gumbel-Softmax, and the temperature parameter.

```python
# softmax(x_i) = exp(x_i) / sum_j(exp(x_j))

# Properties:
# 1. Output is in (0, 1) and sums to 1 → probability distribution
# 2. Preserves order: x_i > x_j → softmax(x_i) > softmax(x_j)
# 3. Translation invariant: softmax(x + c) = softmax(x)
# 4. Temperature scaling: softmax(x / tau)
#    tau → 0: approaches one-hot (max value probability → 1, rest → 0)
#    tau → inf: approaches uniform distribution (all probabilities → 1/K)

# Numerical stability:
# Computing exp(x) directly can overflow (when x=1000, exp(1000) = inf)
# Trick: softmax(x) = softmax(x - max(x))  (using translation invariance)

# Temperature parameters in various scenarios:
# Attention: tau = sqrt(d_k) → prevents dot products from growing too large in high dimensions
# Contrastive learning: tau = 0.07 → sharp distribution, emphasizes hard negatives
# Sinkhorn: tau = 0.1 → controls how concentrated/uniform the assignment is
# Gumbel: tau annealed from high to low → training transitions from soft to hard
```

### 13.6 Optimal Transport and Sinkhorn

The mathematical foundation of Sinkhorn codebook balancing is optimal transport theory.

```python
# Optimal transport problem:
# There are N factories (encoder outputs), each with production a_i (usually uniform: a_i = 1/N)
# There are K warehouses (codewords), each with capacity b_j (usually uniform: b_j = 1/K)
# Transport cost: C_ij = ||z_i - e_j||^2 (distance from encoder output to codeword)
# Objective: find transport plan T that minimizes total cost

# min sum(T_ij * C_ij)
# s.t. T >= 0
#      sum_j(T_ij) = a_i  (every output is shipped out)
#      sum_i(T_ij) = b_j  (every codeword receives an equal amount)

# Sinkhorn algorithm: iterative row/column normalization to approximately solve
def sinkhorn(C, a, b, epsilon=0.1, n_iter=3):
    """
    C: [N, K] cost matrix
    a: [N] source distribution (usually 1/N)
    b: [K] target distribution (usually 1/K → uniform)
    epsilon: regularization strength (smaller = closer to exact solution, but less stable)
    """
    K_matrix = exp(-C / epsilon)  # [N, K]
    u = ones(N)
    for _ in range(n_iter):
        v = b / (K_matrix.T @ u)  # column normalization
        u = a / (K_matrix @ v)    # row normalization
    T = diag(u) @ K_matrix @ diag(v)  # optimal transport plan
    return T.argmax(dim=1)  # which codeword each output is assigned to
```

<Callout type="info" title="Intuitive Understanding of Sinkhorn">
Plain argmin (nearest-neighbor assignment): each output freely picks the nearest codeword → everyone may pick the same one → collapse.

Sinkhorn balancing: equivalent to "capacity-limited" — each codeword accepts at most N/K outputs. If too many outputs pick a particular codeword, the rest must go to their next-nearest → forced dispersion.

Analogy: university admissions where each department has an enrollment cap. Even if everyone's first choice is Computer Science, students must be redistributed to other departments based on scores.
</Callout>

### 13.7 The Mathematics of Exponential Moving Average (EMA)

EMA is the core mechanism for VQ-VAE codebook updates, and is also widely used in BatchNorm statistics, model weight averaging, and other scenarios.

```python
# EMA: x_t = alpha * x_{t-1} + (1-alpha) * observation_t
# alpha (decay rate): typically 0.99 or 0.999

# Expanded:
# x_t = (1-alpha) * obs_t
#      + alpha*(1-alpha) * obs_{t-1}
#      + alpha^2*(1-alpha) * obs_{t-2}
#      + ...

# Weights decay exponentially over time:
# Most recent observations have the largest weight → codewords are mainly determined by recently assigned vectors
# alpha=0.99 → effective window ≈ 100 steps (observations from 100 steps ago have weight < 0.01)
# alpha=0.999 → effective window ≈ 1000 steps

# In VQ-VAE codebook updates:
# m_k = alpha * m_k + (1-alpha) * sum(z_e_assigned_to_k)  # numerator
# n_k = alpha * n_k + (1-alpha) * count(assigned_to_k)    # denominator
# e_k = m_k / n_k  # codeword = weighted average

# alpha too small (0.9): codewords change fast → unstable → SID drift
# alpha too large (0.9999): codewords barely move → slow adaptation → can't keep up when data distribution shifts
# Typically use 0.99 as a starting point
```

---

## Part 14: Advanced Quantization Techniques and Frontier Directions

### 14.1 Multi-Codebook VQ

Besides RQ (residual quantization), there is another multi-codebook scheme: simultaneously selecting one codeword from each of multiple codebooks and summing them to obtain the quantized result.

```python
# Multi-Codebook VQ (also called Group VQ):
def multi_codebook_vq(z, codebooks, M=4):
    """
    z: [batch, d] original vectors
    codebooks: M codebooks, each K×d
    M: number of codebooks
    """
    codes = []
    z_q = zeros_like(z)

    # Split z into M segments (each d/M dimensions)
    z_splits = z.split(d // M, dim=-1)

    for i, (z_part, codebook) in enumerate(zip(z_splits, codebooks)):
        distances = cdist(z_part, codebook)
        code = distances.argmin(dim=1)
        z_q_part = codebook[code]
        codes.append(code)
        z_q[:, i*d//M:(i+1)*d//M] = z_q_part

    return codes, z_q

# Comparison:
# RQ: full-dimension layer-by-layer quantization (coarse → fine)
# Multi-Codebook: segment-wise parallel quantization (each segment independent)
# In practice, RQ is more commonly used in SID systems because the hierarchical structure is more friendly to autoregressive generation
```

### 14.2 Look-up Free Quantization (LFQ)

A new direction in 2024: quantization methods that don't require codebook lookup.

```python
# LFQ (Look-up Free Quantization):
# Core idea: per-dimension independent sign quantization

def lfq(z, codebook_size):
    """
    z: [batch, d] continuous vectors
    Quantize each dimension to {-1, +1} → binary codes
    When d = log2(codebook_size), you get an equivalent codebook size
    """
    # Per-dimension sign
    codes = sign(z)  # {-1, +1}^d

    # The codebook is implicitly defined by all combinations of {-1, +1}^d
    # Codebook size = 2^d
    # d=10 → codebook size = 1024
    # d=20 → codebook size = 1,048,576

    return codes

# Advantages:
# 1. No codebook storage (implicitly defined)
# 2. No codebook collapse (all codewords naturally exist)
# 3. Extremely fast quantization (only sign operation needed)
# 4. Gradients pass through via STE

# Disadvantages:
# 1. Codewords uniformly distributed on hypercube vertices → doesn't adapt to data distribution
# 2. Requires auxiliary losses to push encoder outputs close to {-1, +1}
```

### 14.3 Variable-Length SID

Latest trend: different items use different-length SIDs.

```python
# Traditional SID: all items have a fixed 4 layers
# Problem: popular items don't need that much information (they have abundant behavioral data)
#          long-tail items may need more layers (finer-grained distinction)

# Variable-length SID approaches:
# Method 1: adaptive layer count based on reconstruction error
def variable_length_rq(z, codebooks, max_layers=8, threshold=0.01):
    residual = z
    codes = []
    for layer in range(max_layers):
        code, z_q = quantize(residual, codebooks[layer])
        codes.append(code)
        residual = residual - z_q
        if norm(residual) < threshold:  # stop when residual is small enough
            break
    return codes  # length may vary from 2 to 8

# Method 2: importance-adaptive
# Head items: SID = [k1, k2] (2 layers, short → let ID embedding supplement details)
# Long-tail items: SID = [k1, k2, k3, k4, k5, k6] (6 layers, detailed → independently carries representation)

# Challenge: variable-length sequences make both training and inference more complex for autoregressive models
# Requires special padding/masking strategies
```

### 14.4 Distribution-level SID

Instead of treating a SID as a deterministic codeword sequence, treat it as a **probability distribution over codewords**.

```python
# Traditional: item_A → SID = [37, 512, 891] (deterministic)
# Distribution-level: item_A → P(SID) = {
#   layer_1: {37: 0.7, 45: 0.2, 12: 0.1},
#   layer_2: {512: 0.6, 100: 0.3, 200: 0.1},
#   layer_3: {891: 0.8, 500: 0.2}
# }

# Advantages:
# 1. Richer information (not just "nearest", but also "second nearest")
# 2. Collision mitigation (two items may have the same nearest codeword but different distributions)
# 3. Smoother training objective (using KL divergence instead of cross-entropy)

# Challenges:
# 1. Increased storage (each item needs to store a distribution instead of a single ID)
# 2. How to use efficiently at inference time
# 3. How to combine with autoregressive generation
```

### 14.5 Codebook Initialization Strategies

Codebook initialization is crucial for training stability.

```python
# Method 1: K-Means++ Initialization (most common)
# Run K-Means on training data, initialize codebook with cluster centers
# Advantage: Codewords are spread across the data space from the start → high utilization
# Disadvantage: Requires a pass over the data (may use sampling for billion-scale data)

def kmeans_plus_plus_init(data_sample, K):
    # Select the 1st center: random
    centers = [data_sample[random_int(len(data_sample))]]
    for k in range(1, K):
        # Select the k-th center: farther from existing centers → higher selection probability
        distances = min_distance_to_centers(data_sample, centers)
        probs = distances ** 2 / sum(distances ** 2)
        new_center = data_sample[sample_with_probs(probs)]
        centers.append(new_center)
    return centers

# Method 2: Uniform Initialization
# Sample points uniformly within the data range
# Simple but not as good as K-Means++

# Method 3: Training Set Sampling
# Directly sample K random points from training data as initial codewords
# Ensures codewords are within the data distribution, but may not be sufficiently uniform

# Method 4: Orthogonal Initialization (multi-layer RQ)
# Initialize each layer's codebook orthogonally to reduce inter-layer redundancy
# Used in conjunction with OPQ's rotation matrix
```

### 14.6 Codebook Temperature Annealing

Gradually transition from soft quantization to hard quantization during training:

```python
# Phase 1 (first 20% of steps): High temperature → soft assignment
# Each input is "spread" across multiple codewords, all codewords receive gradients
# tau = 10.0

# Phase 2 (middle 60% of steps): Gradually decrease temperature
# Assignment gradually concentrates on fewer codewords
# tau: 10.0 → 1.0 (linear or exponential decay)

# Phase 3 (last 20% of steps): Low temperature → approximately hard assignment
# Approaches argmin behavior at inference time
# tau = 0.1

def get_temperature(step, total_steps, start_temp=10.0, end_temp=0.1):
    progress = step / total_steps
    if progress < 0.2:
        return start_temp
    elif progress < 0.8:
        # Linear/exponential decay during the middle 60% phase
        t = (progress - 0.2) / 0.6
        return start_temp * (end_temp / start_temp) ** t
    else:
        return end_temp
```

### 14.7 Dead Code Replacement

```python
def replace_dead_codes(codebook, usage_count, z_e_batch, threshold=5):
    """
    Detect codewords unused for multiple consecutive batches,
    replace them with perturbed versions of active codewords
    """
    dead_mask = usage_count < threshold
    num_dead = dead_mask.sum()

    if num_dead == 0:
        return codebook

    # Strategy 1: Replace with vectors from the current batch that deviate
    # most from existing codewords
    # (these vectors most need new codewords to represent them)
    distances = cdist(z_e_batch, codebook.weight)
    min_distances = distances.min(dim=1).values
    hardest_samples = z_e_batch[min_distances.topk(num_dead).indices]
    codebook.weight[dead_mask] = hardest_samples + randn_like(hardest_samples) * 0.01

    # Strategy 2: Replace with the most frequently used codewords + small perturbation
    # (there may be uncovered data near active codewords)
    # top_used = usage_count.topk(num_dead).indices
    # codebook[dead] = codebook[top_used] + noise

    # Reset usage counts
    usage_count[dead_mask] = 0

    return codebook
```

### 14.8 Multi-Modal Fusion Input

The input to RQ-VAE is not just IDs—industrial systems fuse features from multiple modalities:

```python
# Multi-Modal Encoder Architecture:

class MultiModalEncoder:
    def __init__(self):
        # Independent encoders for each modality
        self.image_encoder = ResNet50(pretrained=True)    # Image → 2048-dim
        self.text_encoder = BERT(pretrained=True)         # Text → 768-dim
        self.audio_encoder = Wav2Vec(pretrained=True)     # Audio → 512-dim (if available)
        self.attr_encoder = MLP(attr_dim, 128)            # Attributes → 128-dim

        # Fusion layer
        self.fusion = MLP(2048 + 768 + 512 + 128, hidden=[512, 256], out=32)

    def forward(self, image, title, audio, attributes):
        img_feat = self.image_encoder(image)        # [batch, 2048]
        txt_feat = self.text_encoder(title)         # [batch, 768]
        aud_feat = self.audio_encoder(audio)        # [batch, 512]
        attr_feat = self.attr_encoder(attributes)   # [batch, 128]

        # Concatenate → fuse → 32-dim (fed to RQ-VAE)
        concat = torch.cat([img_feat, txt_feat, aud_feat, attr_feat], dim=1)
        z_e = self.fusion(concat)  # [batch, 32]
        return z_e

# Frozen vs. Fine-tuned:
# Typical: Freeze pretrained encoders, only train fusion layer and RQ-VAE
# End-to-end: Fine-tune all encoders together (computationally expensive, but potentially better)
```

### 14.9 Collaborative Signal Injection

The limitation of purely semantic SIDs: two items with similar content (a black and a red version of the same Nike running shoe) may have completely identical semantic SIDs—but user behavior can be very different (the red one is more popular among women).

```python
# Methods for injecting collaborative signals:

# Method 1: Behavioral Embedding Concatenation
# item_repr = [semantic_embedding; collaborative_embedding]
# semantic: obtained from content features (image/text/attributes)
# collaborative: obtained from behavior matrix factorization / Item2Vec

# Method 2: Contrastive Learning Alignment
# Positive pairs: two items consecutively clicked by the same user
# Negative pairs: random items
# Make "items frequently clicked together" closer in SID space

# Method 3: End-to-End Joint Training (most aggressive)
# RQ-VAE loss = reconstruction loss + downstream CTR loss
# CTR gradients backpropagate to Encoder → codebook automatically captures collaborative signals
# Problem: drift risk (discussed in detail in Part 8)

# Method 4: Weighted Fusion (most robust)
# Train two sets of embeddings:
#   - Semantic Embedding: obtained from content features, stable
#   - Collaborative Embedding: obtained from behavioral data, dynamic
# RQ-VAE input = alpha * semantic + (1-alpha) * collaborative
# alpha can be fixed (0.7), or a learnable item-level gate
```

---

## Part 15: Deep Dive into Contrastive Learning—From Theory to Recommendation System Practice

### 15.1 Theoretical Foundations of Contrastive Learning

Why does contrastive learning work? What is its relationship with mutual information?

```python
# Information-theoretic interpretation of InfoNCE Loss:
# L_InfoNCE = -log(exp(sim(z_i, z_j)/tau) / sum_k(exp(sim(z_i, z_k)/tau)))

# Theorem (Oord et al. 2018):
# InfoNCE is a lower bound on I(z_i; z_j) (mutual information)
# Specifically: I(z_i; z_j) >= log(N) - L_InfoNCE
# N = number of negative samples

# This means: Minimizing InfoNCE Loss ≈ Maximizing mutual information between positive pairs
# The greater the mutual information between positive pairs → the more information they share → the better the representations
```

**Why do we need a large number of negative samples?**

```python
# The tightness of the lower bound depends on N:
# I >= log(N) - L
# Larger N → larger log(N) → tighter bound → more accurate estimate

# In practice:
# SimCLR: batch_size=4096 → 8190 negative samples → good results
# MoCo: queue maintains 65536 negative samples → even better results
# Recommendation: item pool has millions of items → naturally abundant negative samples

# But too many negatives also has problems:
# "Easy negatives" (completely unrelated to the query) provide very weak signal
# "Hard negatives" (somewhat similar to the query but not positive) are needed to drive learning
```

### 15.2 Hard Negative Mining

```python
# Method 1: In-batch Negatives (most common)
# Other samples' positives within the same batch serve as negatives for the current sample
# Advantage: No extra computation
# Disadvantage: May contain "false negatives" within the batch (actually similar but treated as negatives)

# Method 2: Semi-hard Negatives
# Select negatives that are slightly farther than the positive, but not too far
# d(anchor, positive) < d(anchor, negative) < d(anchor, positive) + margin

# Method 3: ANN-retrieved Hard Negatives
# Use the current model to retrieve top-K most similar items
# These items that "the model considers similar but actually aren't" are the best negatives
# Requires periodically updating the ANN index (costly)

# Method 4: Hybrid Strategy (industrial practice)
# 50% in-batch negatives (easy)
# 30% ANN hard negatives (hard)
# 20% random negatives (diversity)
```

### 15.3 Data Augmentation Strategies

The quality of contrastive learning depends on how "positive pairs" are constructed—i.e., the data augmentation strategy.

```python
# Image augmentations (SimCLR):
augmentations = [
    RandomResizedCrop(scale=(0.2, 1.0)),  # Random crop
    ColorJitter(0.8, 0.8, 0.8, 0.2),     # Color jitter
    RandomGrayscale(p=0.2),               # Random grayscale
    GaussianBlur(kernel_size=23),         # Gaussian blur
    RandomHorizontalFlip(),               # Horizontal flip
]

# Text augmentations:
text_augmentations = [
    "random_token_deletion",   # Random word deletion
    "random_token_swap",       # Random word order swap
    "synonym_replacement",     # Synonym replacement
    "back_translation",        # Back-translation (zh→en→zh)
]

# Recommendation sequence augmentations (CL4SRec):
sequence_augmentations = [
    "item_crop",        # Crop subsequence
    "item_mask",        # Randomly mask some items
    "item_reorder",     # Randomly swap adjacent items
    "item_substitute",  # Replace with similar items
]
```

### 15.4 Complete Application of Contrastive Learning in Recommendations

```python
# CL4SRec (Contrastive Learning for Sequential Recommendation, 2022)
class CL4SRec:
    def forward(self, user_sequence):
        # Original sequence representation
        h = self.transformer(user_sequence)  # [batch, d]

        # Two augmentations
        seq_aug1 = self.augment(user_sequence, method="crop")
        seq_aug2 = self.augment(user_sequence, method="mask")
        h_aug1 = self.transformer(seq_aug1)
        h_aug2 = self.transformer(seq_aug2)

        # Contrastive learning loss
        cl_loss = info_nce(h_aug1, h_aug2, temperature=0.1)

        # Main task loss (next-item prediction)
        main_loss = cross_entropy(h, next_item_label)

        # Joint training
        return main_loss + 0.1 * cl_loss

# Effect: CL regularization makes sequence representations more robust
# Even if the user behavior sequence is noisy (accidental clicks), stable representations can still be learned
```

### 15.5 Relationship Between Contrastive Learning and SID

```python
# Three uses of contrastive learning in SID systems:

# Use 1: Multimodal pretraining (providing input for RQ-VAE)
# CLIP-style training: image-text alignment → high-quality item embeddings → RQ-VAE input

# Use 2: Regularization of the SID space
# Make "items frequently clicked by the same user" close in SID space
# Positive samples: item pairs clicked adjacently in the same session
# Negative samples: random item pairs
# Goal: SID captures not only content semantics but also behavioral similarity

# Use 3: SID quality evaluation
# Use contrastive learning's alignment/uniformity metrics to evaluate SID space quality:
# - Alignment: average distance of positive pairs (lower is better)
# - Uniformity: distribution uniformity of all SIDs in the space (more uniform is better)
```

### 15.6 Deep Understanding of the Temperature Parameter

Temperature is the most important hyperparameter in contrastive learning and also runs through VQ-VAE training.

```python
# Effect of temperature on loss gradients:

# Low temperature (tau=0.01):
# Softmax output is extremely concentrated → only the most similar negatives have non-zero gradients
# Effect: model focuses on distinguishing the "hardest" samples
# Risk: training instability, easy to collapse

# High temperature (tau=1.0):
# Softmax output is close to uniform → all negatives have gradients
# Effect: model learns from all negative samples
# Risk: learns too "flat", insufficient discrimination

# Empirical values:
# Image contrastive learning: tau=0.07-0.2 (SimCLR)
# Text contrastive learning: tau=0.05 (SimCSE)
# Recommendation: tau=0.1-0.5 (depends on item space density)
# Sinkhorn codebook balancing: tau=0.05-0.2

# Adaptive temperature (learnable):
# tau = exp(learnable_parameter)  # Let the model learn the optimal temperature itself
# But the range needs to be constrained to prevent degeneration
```

---

## Part 16: Generative Models Explained — The Complete Spectrum of the VAE Family

### 16.1 Motivation: From Autoencoders to VAE

Autoencoders can compress data but **cannot generate new data** — you cannot randomly sample in the latent space because you don't know which regions are valid.

The core motivation of VAE: give the latent space **structure** (standard normal distribution), so that sampling at any location yields meaningful output.

```python
# Autoencoder's latent space: like a disorganized parking lot
# There may be large blank regions between data points
# Sampling in blank regions → decodes to meaningless output

# VAE's latent space: like an organized map
# Through KL constraint, all data is "pushed" into the standard normal distribution region
# Sampling at any location has a high probability of yielding meaningful output
```

### 16.2 Deriving the VAE's ELBO

VAE optimizes the Evidence Lower Bound (ELBO):

```python
# Objective: maximize the log-likelihood of data log p(x)

# Derivation (Jensen's inequality):
# log p(x) = log integral(p(x|z) * p(z) dz)
#           = log integral(p(x|z) * p(z) * q(z|x)/q(z|x) dz)
#           >= integral(q(z|x) * log(p(x|z) * p(z) / q(z|x)) dz)
#           = E_q[log p(x|z)] - KL(q(z|x) || p(z))
#           = ELBO

# ELBO = Reconstruction term + KL regularization term
# Reconstruction term: E_q[log p(x|z)] → decoding quality
# KL term: -KL(q(z|x) || p(z)) → latent space regularization

# When q is Gaussian, KL has a closed-form solution:
# KL(N(mu, sigma^2) || N(0, 1)) = 0.5 * sum(mu^2 + sigma^2 - log(sigma^2) - 1)
```

### 16.3 beta-VAE and Disentanglement

```python
# beta-VAE: add a weight beta before the KL term
# Loss = reconstruction + beta * KL

# beta > 1: stronger regularization → more "disentangled" latent space
# Disentanglement: each dimension of the latent space independently controls one attribute
# For example: z[0] controls color, z[1] controls size, z[2] controls angle...

# Significance in recommendations:
# If each layer (codebook) of SID can represent different attributes in a disentangled way:
# Layer 1: Category (sports/beauty/electronics)
# Layer 2: Style (casual/formal/athletic)
# Layer 3: Price range (premium/mid-range/budget)
# Layer 4: Brand cluster

# Such disentangled SIDs are very valuable for interpretability and controllable recommendation
# In practice, RQ's residual structure naturally encourages a certain degree of disentanglement
```

### 16.4 VQ-VAE-2 (Hierarchical Quantization)

Multi-scale VQ-VAE proposed by DeepMind in 2019:

```python
# VQ-VAE-2: codebooks at different resolutions handle different levels of information
class VQVAE2:
    def __init__(self):
        # Bottom level: handles high-resolution/detail information
        self.encoder_bottom = ConvEncoder(stride=4)  # 4x downsampling
        self.codebook_bottom = Codebook(K=512, dim=64)

        # Top level: handles low-resolution/global information
        self.encoder_top = ConvEncoder(stride=2)  # Another 2x downsampling (8x cumulative)
        self.codebook_top = Codebook(K=512, dim=64)

    def encode(self, x):
        # Bottom-level encoding
        h_bottom = self.encoder_bottom(x)  # High-resolution feature map
        z_q_bottom, codes_bottom = self.codebook_bottom.quantize(h_bottom)

        # Top-level encoding (based on bottom-level results)
        h_top = self.encoder_top(h_bottom)  # Low-resolution feature map
        z_q_top, codes_top = self.codebook_top.quantize(h_top)

        return codes_top, codes_bottom  # Hierarchical codewords

# Difference from RQ-VAE:
# VQ-VAE-2: spatial hierarchy (from low resolution to high resolution)
# RQ-VAE: precision hierarchy (from coarse-grained to fine-grained)
# The two can be combined: use RQ for precision decomposition within each spatial level
```

### 16.5 dVAE (Discrete VAE in DALL-E)

The image Tokenizer used in the first generation of OpenAI DALL-E:

```python
# dVAE: differentiable discrete VAE using Gumbel-Softmax
class dVAE:
    def encode(self, x):
        logits = self.encoder(x)  # [batch, H*W, K] K codeword probabilities per position
        if self.training:
            # Gumbel-Softmax: differentiable discrete sampling
            z = gumbel_softmax(logits, temperature=self.tau)  # soft one-hot
        else:
            # Use argmax at inference (hard selection)
            z = one_hot(logits.argmax(dim=-1))
        return z

    def decode(self, z):
        # z: soft/hard one-hot → look up codebook → decode
        z_q = z @ self.codebook.weight  # [batch, H*W, dim]
        return self.decoder(z_q)

# DALL-E training:
# Stage 1: Train dVAE to convert 256x256 images → 32x32 codeword grid (1024 positions total)
# Stage 2: Train GPT to autoregressively generate these codewords (conditioned on text)
# Inference: text → GPT generates codewords → dVAE decodes → image

# Analogy with SID:
# DALL-E: image → dVAE → codewords → GPT generation
# SID: item → RQ-VAE → codewords → Transformer generation
# The structure is exactly the same! Just different application domains
```

### 16.6 AudioLM and Speech/Music Tokenization

Google's AudioLM in 2022 applied the VQ-VAE idea to audio:

```python
# AudioLM's three layers of tokens:
# 1. Semantic Tokens: high-level semantics (what is said / music style)
#    Source: w2v-BERT → K-Means quantization
# 2. Coarse Acoustic Tokens: coarse-grained timbre
#    Source: SoundStream Encoder → RVQ layers 1-4
# 3. Fine Acoustic Tokens: fine-grained audio quality
#    Source: SoundStream Encoder → RVQ layers 5-12

# Generation process (autoregressive):
# Stage 1: Generate Semantic Tokens (decide what to say)
# Stage 2: Generate Coarse Tokens (decide how to say/sing it)
# Stage 3: Generate Fine Tokens (decide audio quality)

# Perfect correspondence with recommendation SID:
# Semantic Tokens ↔ SID layer 1 (major category)
# Coarse Tokens ↔ SID layers 2-3 (medium category)
# Fine Tokens ↔ SID layer 4+ (specific item)
```

### 16.7 Diffusion Models and Discrete Diffusion

Diffusion models are another path to generation. Although SID currently uses autoregression, discrete diffusion is a worthwhile alternative to understand.

```python
# Continuous Diffusion (DDPM):
# Forward: gradually add noise x_0 → x_1 → ... → x_T ≈ N(0, I)
# Reverse: gradually denoise x_T → x_{T-1} → ... → x_0

# Discrete Diffusion (D3PM, Discrete Denoising Diffusion):
# Forward: gradually "corrupt" discrete tokens → eventually become uniform distribution
# Reverse: gradually "repair" tokens → recover the original sequence

# Discrete Diffusion for SID Generation:
# Advantage: parallel generation (not token-by-token, but denoising all positions simultaneously)
# Disadvantage: multi-step inference is slow, quality not yet matching autoregressive
# Research status: early exploration (2024-2025)
```

---

## Part 17: Distributed Training and Large-Scale Systems

### 17.1 Data Parallelism

The most commonly used distributed training approach: multiple GPUs each hold a complete copy of the model and process different data.

```python
# Data Parallelism (DP / DDP):
# GPU 0: model_copy_0.forward(batch_0) → grad_0
# GPU 1: model_copy_1.forward(batch_1) → grad_1
# GPU 2: model_copy_2.forward(batch_2) → grad_2
# GPU 3: model_copy_3.forward(batch_3) → grad_3

# AllReduce: aggregate gradients from all GPUs → average
# avg_grad = (grad_0 + grad_1 + grad_2 + grad_3) / 4

# Each GPU uses avg_grad to update its own model → stay synchronized
# Effective batch_size = per_gpu_batch * num_gpus

# PyTorch DDP (recommended):
model = DistributedDataParallel(model, device_ids=[local_rank])
# Automatically handles gradient synchronization
```

### 17.2 Model Parallelism

When the model is too large to fit on a single GPU:

```python
# Tensor Parallelism:
# Split one layer's weight matrix across multiple GPUs
# E.g.: FFN's W [4096, 16384] → GPU0: W[:, :8192], GPU1: W[:, 8192:]

# Pipeline Parallelism:
# Place different layers on different GPUs
# GPU 0: Layer 0-5
# GPU 1: Layer 6-11
# GPU 2: Layer 12-17
# GPU 3: Layer 18-23
# Data flows through each GPU sequentially like a "pipeline"

# In SID systems:
# RQ-VAE is usually not large (a few hundred MB) → data parallelism is sufficient
# Downstream Transformer (e.g., OneRec's 12-layer d=512) → may need model parallelism
# Embedding Table (1 billion items × 128 dims) → definitely needs sharding (Parameter Server)
```

### 17.3 Parameter Server and Embedding Sharding

The embedding table in recommendation systems can be hundreds of GBs — distributed storage is a must.

```python
# Parameter Server Architecture:
# Worker 0-7: responsible for forward/backward computation
# PS 0-3: responsible for storing and updating Embedding parameters

# Workflow:
# 1. Worker gets the item IDs involved in the batch
# 2. Pulls embeddings for those IDs from the corresponding PS (Pull)
# 3. Worker does forward + backward, obtains embedding gradients
# 4. Pushes gradients back to PS to update the corresponding embeddings (Push)

# Embedding Sharding Strategy:
# Hash-based sharding: item_id % num_ps → which PS
# Hotspot issue: a few popular items are frequently requested → special handling (replication/caching)

# In SID systems:
# Traditional: needs Parameter Server to store million-scale Embedding Table
# SID: only needs to store codebooks (L of them, each K×d, far smaller than million-scale Embeddings)
# → SID drastically reduces the engineering complexity of distributed Embeddings
```

### 17.4 Mixed Precision Training

```python
# FP32: 4 bytes/param, standard precision
# FP16: 2 bytes/param, may overflow/underflow
# BF16: 2 bytes/param, more exponent bits → more stable, but slightly lower precision

# Mixed precision strategy:
# Forward/backward: FP16 or BF16 (faster, saves memory)
# Parameter update: FP32 (precise, prevents accumulated errors)
# Loss Scaling: scale up loss to prevent small gradients from underflowing in FP16

from torch.cuda.amp import autocast, GradScaler
scaler = GradScaler()

for batch in dataloader:
    with autocast():  # FP16 forward
        loss = model(batch)
    scaler.scale(loss).backward()  # Scaled FP16 backward
    scaler.step(optimizer)          # FP32 update
    scaler.update()

# Result: 2x training speed, 50% memory savings, virtually no precision loss
```

### 17.5 Large-Scale K-Means / RQ-VAE Training

Industrial-scale RQ-VAE needs to handle billions of items:

```python
# Challenge: 1 billion items × 128 dims × 4 bytes ≈ 512 GB (doesn't fit in single-machine memory)

# Solution 1: Distributed K-Means
# Each iteration:
# - Each machine loads a portion of the data
# - Locally computes assignments (nearest codeword)
# - AllReduce to aggregate new centroids for codewords
# Communication volume: K × d × 4 bytes (codebook size, usually very small)

# Solution 2: Mini-Batch K-Means
# Doesn't need the full dataset, uses only one batch at a time
# Codewords updated via EMA
# Advantage: memory-friendly, can process in a streaming fashion
# Disadvantage: convergence may not be as good as full-batch

# Solution 3: SGD Training for RQOPQ
# Convert K-Means into a gradient descent problem
# Fully compatible with mini-batch training
# Can leverage GPU acceleration
# This is the choice made by Kuaishou's OneSearch

# Practical training configuration:
# Data: 1 billion items, sample 10 million for RQ-VAE training
# Hardware: 8 × A100 (80GB), data parallelism
# Time: ~4-8 hours to complete training
# Afterwards: run inference to assign SIDs to all 1 billion items (~30 minutes)
```

### 17.6 Online Serving Performance Optimization

```python
# Online inference optimization for SID generative retrieval:

# 1. KV Cache (covered in detail earlier)
# Avoid redundant computation of KV for historical sequences

# 2. Model Quantization (INT8/FP16)
# Model weights from FP32 → INT8: 2-4x inference speedup, 4x memory savings
# Precision loss < 0.1% (acceptable for recommendation systems)

# 3. Batch Inference
# Merge multiple user requests into a single batch for inference
# GPU utilization from 10% → 80%+

# 4. Early Stopping (in Beam Search)
# If all candidates in the beam score far below the best found → stop early
# Reduces unnecessary layer computations

# 5. Two-Stage Beam Search
# Stage 1: large beam (B=500) to generate the first 2 layers of codewords
# Stage 2: small beam (B=50) to generate the last 2 layers of codewords
# Because the first two layers have already filtered out most irrelevant directions

# 6. SID Index Precomputation
# SID → item mapping table is precomputed and stored in memory
# After generating a SID, O(1) table lookup to get the item
```

### 17.7 Monitoring and Alerting

```python
# Core monitoring metrics after SID system goes online:

monitoring_metrics = {
    # Model Quality
    "recon_loss_daily": "Daily average reconstruction error (should be stable/decreasing)",
    "codebook_utilization": "Codebook utilization per layer (30%-95% is healthy)",
    "collision_rate": "Collision rate (< 5% is healthy)",
    "layer_residual_norms": "Residual norms per layer (should decrease layer by layer)",

    # Online Performance
    "recall_hit_rate": "SID retrieval hit rate (generated SIDs that have corresponding items)",
    "recall_coverage": "Item coverage ratio (number of unique items retrieved)",
    "p99_latency_ms": "99th percentile latency (< 30ms)",
    "beam_search_avg_length": "Beam search average exploration depth",

    # Business Metrics
    "ctr_lift_vs_baseline": "CTR lift relative to baseline",
    "cold_start_ctr": "CTR for cold-start items (core value of SID)",
    "diversity_score": "Recommendation diversity (SID should improve long-tail impressions)",

    # System Health
    "sid_update_freshness": "Last SID update time (should be < 24h)",
    "index_consistency": "Consistency between SID table and item catalog",
}

# Alerting rules:
alerts = [
    "codebook_utilization < 30%",     # Codebook collapse
    "collision_rate > 10%",           # Collision rate too high
    "p99_latency > 50ms",            # Latency too high
    "cold_start_ctr day-over-day < -20%",  # Cold-start performance plummets
]
```

## Part 18: From Theory to Practice — Hands-On Toy RQ-VAE Implementation

### 18.1 Minimal Runnable RQ-VAE Implementation

```python
import torch
import torch.nn as nn
import torch.nn.functional as F

class ToyRQVAE(nn.Module):
    """Minimal runnable RQ-VAE implementation (for educational purposes)"""

    def __init__(self, input_dim=128, code_dim=32, num_codes=256, num_layers=3):
        super().__init__()
        self.num_layers = num_layers
        self.code_dim = code_dim

        # Encoder: input features → continuous latent vector
        self.encoder = nn.Sequential(
            nn.Linear(input_dim, 256),
            nn.ReLU(),
            nn.Linear(256, code_dim),
        )

        # Decoder: quantized vector → reconstruction
        self.decoder = nn.Sequential(
            nn.Linear(code_dim, 256),
            nn.ReLU(),
            nn.Linear(256, input_dim),
        )

        # L-layer codebooks, each layer has K codewords of code_dim dimensions
        self.codebooks = nn.ParameterList([
            nn.Parameter(torch.randn(num_codes, code_dim) * 0.1)
            for _ in range(num_layers)
        ])

        # EMA-related (for codebook updates)
        self.register_buffer('ema_count', torch.zeros(num_layers, num_codes))
        self.register_buffer('ema_sum', torch.zeros(num_layers, num_codes, code_dim))

    def quantize_layer(self, residual, layer_idx):
        """Single-layer quantization: find nearest codeword"""
        codebook = self.codebooks[layer_idx]  # [K, d]
        distances = torch.cdist(residual, codebook)  # [batch, K]
        indices = distances.argmin(dim=1)  # [batch]
        z_q = codebook[indices]  # [batch, d]
        return z_q, indices

    def forward(self, x):
        # Encode
        z_e = self.encoder(x)  # [batch, code_dim]

        # Residual quantization
        residual = z_e
        z_q_sum = torch.zeros_like(z_e)
        all_indices = []
        all_z_q = []

        for layer in range(self.num_layers):
            z_q, indices = self.quantize_layer(residual, layer)
            all_indices.append(indices)
            all_z_q.append(z_q)
            z_q_sum = z_q_sum + z_q
            residual = z_e - z_q_sum  # Update residual

        # STE: forward pass uses z_q_sum, backward pass passes gradients to z_e
        z_q_st = z_e + (z_q_sum - z_e).detach()

        # Decode
        x_hat = self.decoder(z_q_st)

        # Loss computation
        recon_loss = F.mse_loss(x_hat, x)
        commit_loss = F.mse_loss(z_e, z_q_sum.detach())

        total_loss = recon_loss + 0.25 * commit_loss

        return {
            'loss': total_loss,
            'recon_loss': recon_loss,
            'commit_loss': commit_loss,
            'indices': all_indices,  # SID!
            'x_hat': x_hat,
        }

    @torch.no_grad()
    def ema_update(self, z_e, all_indices, decay=0.99):
        """EMA codebook update"""
        residual = z_e
        z_q_sum = torch.zeros_like(z_e)

        for layer in range(self.num_layers):
            indices = all_indices[layer]
            codebook = self.codebooks[layer]

            # Count how many times each codeword is assigned and the vector sum
            one_hot = F.one_hot(indices, codebook.shape[0]).float()
            count = one_hot.sum(0)
            sum_vecs = one_hot.T @ residual

            # EMA update
            self.ema_count[layer] = decay * self.ema_count[layer] + (1-decay) * count
            self.ema_sum[layer] = decay * self.ema_sum[layer] + (1-decay) * sum_vecs

            # Update codewords (Laplace smoothing to prevent division by zero)
            n = self.ema_count[layer].unsqueeze(1) + 1e-5
            self.codebooks[layer].data = self.ema_sum[layer] / n

            # Update residual
            z_q = codebook[indices]
            z_q_sum = z_q_sum + z_q
            residual = z_e - z_q_sum
```

### 18.2 Training Loop

```python
def train_toy_rqvae():
    # Simulated data: 10000 items, each with 128-dim features
    data = torch.randn(10000, 128)
    dataset = torch.utils.data.TensorDataset(data)
    dataloader = torch.utils.data.DataLoader(dataset, batch_size=256, shuffle=True)

    model = ToyRQVAE(input_dim=128, code_dim=32, num_codes=256, num_layers=3)
    optimizer = torch.optim.Adam(model.parameters(), lr=1e-3)

    for epoch in range(100):
        total_loss = 0
        utilizations = [set() for _ in range(3)]

        for batch, in dataloader:
            output = model(batch)
            loss = output['loss']

            optimizer.zero_grad()
            loss.backward()
            optimizer.step()

            # EMA codebook update
            z_e = model.encoder(batch)
            model.ema_update(z_e, output['indices'])

            total_loss += loss.item()

            # Record utilization
            for layer, indices in enumerate(output['indices']):
                utilizations[layer].update(indices.tolist())

        # Print monitoring info
        avg_loss = total_loss / len(dataloader)
        utils = [len(u) / 256 * 100 for u in utilizations]
        print(f"Epoch {epoch}: loss={avg_loss:.4f}, "
              f"util=[{utils[0]:.0f}%, {utils[1]:.0f}%, {utils[2]:.0f}%]")

# Expected output:
# Epoch 0:  loss=1.2345, util=[45%, 38%, 32%]  (low utilization at the beginning)
# Epoch 10: loss=0.3456, util=[72%, 65%, 58%]  (utilization gradually improves)
# Epoch 50: loss=0.0567, util=[89%, 82%, 75%]  (approaching convergence)
```

### 18.3 Adding Sinkhorn Balancing

```python
def sinkhorn_assignment(distances, temperature=0.1, n_iters=3):
    """
    Sinkhorn codebook balanced assignment
    distances: [batch, K] distance from each sample to each codeword
    Returns: [batch] assigned codeword indices
    """
    # Convert to "preference" matrix (smaller distance = higher preference)
    Q = torch.exp(-distances / temperature)  # [batch, K]

    # Normalization initialization
    Q = Q / Q.sum()

    # Sinkhorn iterations
    for _ in range(n_iters):
        # Row normalization (each sample's assignment sums to 1/batch)
        Q = Q / Q.sum(dim=1, keepdim=True)
        # Column normalization (each codeword's total assignment sums to 1/K)
        Q = Q / Q.sum(dim=0, keepdim=True)

    # Take argmax from soft assignment to get hard assignment
    return Q.argmax(dim=1)

# Usage:
def quantize_layer_sinkhorn(self, residual, layer_idx):
    codebook = self.codebooks[layer_idx]
    distances = torch.cdist(residual, codebook)

    if self.training:
        indices = sinkhorn_assignment(distances, temperature=0.1)
    else:
        indices = distances.argmin(dim=1)  # Use argmin during inference

    z_q = codebook[indices]
    return z_q, indices
```

### 18.4 Evaluating SID Quality

```python
def evaluate_sid_quality(model, data):
    """Evaluate multi-dimensional quality metrics for SID"""
    model.eval()
    with torch.no_grad():
        output = model(data)

    # 1. Reconstruction error
    recon_error = F.mse_loss(output['x_hat'], data).item()

    # 2. Per-layer utilization
    utilizations = []
    for layer_indices in output['indices']:
        unique_codes = layer_indices.unique().numel()
        total_codes = model.codebooks[0].shape[0]
        utilizations.append(unique_codes / total_codes)

    # 3. Collision rate
    # Concatenate all layer indices to form SIDs
    sids = torch.stack(output['indices'], dim=1)  # [N, num_layers]
    sid_strings = [tuple(sid.tolist()) for sid in sids]
    unique_sids = len(set(sid_strings))
    collision_rate = 1 - unique_sids / len(sid_strings)

    # 4. Per-layer residual norms (should decrease layer by layer)
    z_e = model.encoder(data)
    residual = z_e
    z_q_sum = torch.zeros_like(z_e)
    residual_norms = []
    for layer in range(model.num_layers):
        z_q = model.codebooks[layer][output['indices'][layer]]
        z_q_sum = z_q_sum + z_q
        residual = z_e - z_q_sum
        residual_norms.append(residual.norm(dim=1).mean().item())

    # 5. Entropy of codeword usage distribution (more uniform is better)
    entropies = []
    for layer_indices in output['indices']:
        counts = torch.bincount(layer_indices, minlength=model.codebooks[0].shape[0])
        probs = counts.float() / counts.sum()
        probs = probs[probs > 0]  # Remove zeros
        entropy = -(probs * probs.log2()).sum().item()
        max_entropy = torch.log2(torch.tensor(model.codebooks[0].shape[0]).float()).item()
        entropies.append(entropy / max_entropy)  # Normalized to [0, 1]

    return {
        'recon_error': recon_error,
        'utilizations': utilizations,
        'collision_rate': collision_rate,
        'residual_norms': residual_norms,
        'normalized_entropies': entropies,
    }

# Healthy metric references:
# recon_error: should keep decreasing
# utilizations: each layer > 60%
# collision_rate: < 5%
# residual_norms: decreasing layer by layer (r1 > r2 > r3)
# normalized_entropies: > 0.8 (close to uniform)
```

## Part 19: Graph Neural Networks and Recommendation Systems

### 19.1 Why Does Recommendation Need Graphs?

The interactions between users and items naturally form a **bipartite graph**. Graph structure contains information that traditional sequential models cannot capture:

```python
# What traditional sequential models see:
# User A: [item1, item2, item3, item4]  → temporal order
# Cannot see that item1 and item5 are jointly liked by a similar user group

# What graph models see:
# User A -- clicked --> item1
# User A -- clicked --> item2
# User B -- clicked --> item1  (A and B may have similar tastes!)
# User B -- clicked --> item5
# → A might also like item5 (the graph version of collaborative filtering)

# This kind of "high-order collaborative signal" is the core value of graphs
```

### 19.2 GCN (Graph Convolutional Networks) Basics

```python
# Core operation: Message Passing
# A node's new representation = aggregate neighbors' old representations

# One GCN layer:
# h_v^{(l+1)} = sigma(W * MEAN(h_u^{(l)} for u in N(v) ∪ {v}))

# In recommendation:
# An item node's neighbors = all users who clicked it
# A user node's neighbors = all items they clicked
# After multiple rounds of propagation:
# Item embeddings contain "what types of users like it" information
# User embeddings contain "what similar users like" information

# Equivalent meaning of two-layer propagation:
# item ← user ← item' (co-clicked items influence each other)
# user ← item ← user' (users with similar behavior influence each other)
```

### 19.3 LightGCN (2020, Recommendation-Specific GCN)

LightGCN discovered that for recommendation tasks, GCN's nonlinear activations and feature transformations are actually **harmful**—removing them yields better results.

```python
# LightGCN: only normalized weighted aggregation, no activation, no transformation
class LightGCN:
    def propagate(self, adj_matrix, embeddings):
        # adj_matrix: normalized adjacency matrix (D^{-1/2} A D^{-1/2})
        # One propagation step = matrix multiplication
        return adj_matrix @ embeddings  # That's it

    def forward(self, user_ids, item_ids):
        # Initial embeddings
        all_emb = concat(self.user_emb, self.item_emb)

        # L layers of propagation, collecting results from each layer
        layers = [all_emb]
        for _ in range(self.num_layers):
            all_emb = self.propagate(self.adj, all_emb)
            layers.append(all_emb)

        # Final embedding = average of all layers (crucial!)
        final_emb = mean(layers)  # Not just the last layer

        user_emb = final_emb[user_ids]
        item_emb = final_emb[item_ids]
        return inner_product(user_emb, item_emb)

# Why take the average?
# Layer 0 = original embedding (personalization)
# Layer 1 = direct neighbor info (1-hop collaborative)
# Layer 2 = 2-hop neighbor info (broader collaborative)
# Average = balances information from different hops
```

### 19.4 PinSage (Pinterest, 2018) — Industrial-Scale Graph Recommendation

A GNN that handles graphs with billions of nodes:

```python
# PinSage's industrial design:

# 1. Random walk neighbor sampling (don't use all neighbors)
# Start random walks from the target node
# Track visit frequency of each node along the paths
# Select top-K most frequently visited nodes as "important neighbors"
# Advantage: limits computation while prioritizing more "relevant" neighbors

# 2. Mini-batch training (no need to load the full graph)
# Each batch only loads the involved subgraph
# MapReduce-style computation: suitable for distributed systems

# 3. Curriculum Negative Sampling
# Epochs 1-3: random negatives (easy)
# Epochs 4-6: ANN hard negatives (difficult)
# Gradually increasing difficulty → model progressively refines

# Scale: Pinterest 3 billion Pins, 18 billion edges
# Results: offline Hit@100 improved by 40%+
```

### 19.5 Combining Graphs with SID

```python
# In the SID system, graph signals can be injected at two stages:

# Stage 1: Enhance RQ-VAE's input embeddings
# Use LightGCN to obtain graph-enhanced item embeddings
# These embeddings simultaneously contain content semantics + collaborative signals
# Feed them to RQ-VAE for quantization → SIDs naturally contain collaborative info
# Advantage: RQ-VAE doesn't need modification, graph signals are injected via input

# Stage 2: Graph alignment in the downstream Transformer
# Add an auxiliary loss during autoregressive Transformer training:
# If two items are "neighbors" in the graph (co-clicked by many users)
# Then their SIDs should be close in the Transformer's hidden state space
# → Contrastive learning form: graph neighbors as positive samples

# Stage 3: Graph regularization after SID assignment
# Constraint: highly connected items in the graph should share SID prefixes
# Example: different models from the same brand → first two SID digits are the same
# Implementation: add a regularization term to the RQ-VAE loss
# reg = sum(distance(SID_i, SID_j) for (i,j) in graph_edges)
```
