## Part 20: Deep Dive into Optimizers — From SGD to Adam to Modern Variants

### 20.1 Why Do We Need Adaptive Learning Rates?

SGD uses the same learning rate for all parameters, but the loss surface shape varies drastically across different parameters. This problem is especially pronounced in recommendation systems: popular items' embeddings have already been updated by massive amounts of samples to near-optimal positions and only need small fine-tuning steps; while long-tail items' embeddings may only get updated once every few days, are far from optimal, and need large steps to catch up.

The core idea behind adaptive learning rates: **automatically slow down parameters with frequent and large gradients, and automatically speed up parameters with sparse and small gradients**. This is the starting point of the AdaGrad/Adam family of optimizers.

### 20.2 Full Derivation of Adam

Adam combines Momentum and RMSProp (adaptive step-size scaling) and is currently the most widely used optimizer.

```
Initialize: m_0 = 0 (first moment), v_0 = 0 (second moment), t = 0

Each update step:
  t = t + 1
  g_t = gradient(loss, theta)
  m_t = beta1 * m_{t-1} + (1 - beta1) * g_t       # Exponential moving average of gradients
  v_t = beta2 * v_{t-1} + (1 - beta2) * g_t^2     # Exponential moving average of squared gradients
  m_hat = m_t / (1 - beta1^t)                      # Bias correction
  v_hat = v_t / (1 - beta2^t)                      # Bias correction
  theta_t = theta_{t-1} - lr * m_hat / (sqrt(v_hat) + epsilon)
```

Intuitive interpretation: m_hat tells you "which direction to go" (with inertia, not swayed by single-step noise); v_hat tells you "how steep the terrain is in that direction" (the steeper it is, the smaller the step). Net effect: take large steps in flat directions, small steps in steep directions—automatically adapting to the terrain of different parameters.

Reason for bias correction: at the beginning of training, both m and v start from 0, so the moving averages in the first few steps are severely biased toward zero. Dividing by (1 - beta^t) compensates for this initial bias; as t grows, the correction approaches 1 (negligible).

### 20.3 AdamW (Weight Decay Correction)

An easy pitfall to fall into: **L2 regularization and weight decay are not equivalent in Adam**. They are equivalent in SGD, but completely different in adaptive optimizers.

Wrong approach (Adam + L2): adding the regularization term lambda * ||theta||^2 to the loss causes the regularization gradient to also be scaled by v_t, diluting its effect—frequently updated large parameters end up not being effectively penalized.

Correct approach (AdamW): independently subtract a decay term after the Adam update:

```
theta_t = theta_{t-1} - lr * (m_hat / (sqrt(v_hat) + eps) + wd * theta_{t-1})
                               ↑ Adam update term                ↑ Independent decay term
```

Practical advice: PyTorch's `torch.optim.Adam` implements L2 by default (the wrong way), while `torch.optim.AdamW` is the correct Weight Decay. Modern deep learning training should always use AdamW.

### 20.4 Learning Rate Scheduling (Warmup + Cosine)

**Why do we need Warmup?** At the start of training, both m and v begin at zero, making the estimates highly inaccurate. If you start with a large learning rate right away, the update direction is unreliable and can push parameters to positions that are hard to recover from. Warmup lets the learning rate grow linearly from near 0 to the target value over the first N steps, giving the momentum estimates enough "warm-up" time.

**Cosine Annealing**: After warmup, the learning rate smoothly decreases from peak to near zero following a cosine curve, with the formula lr(t) = lr_min + 0.5 * (lr_max - lr_min) * (1 + cos(t/T * pi)). Compared to Step Decay (staircase-style decay), Cosine is smoother and avoids training instability caused by abrupt learning rate drops.

Typical configuration in RQ-VAE training:

| Component | Learning Rate | Notes |
|------|--------|------|
| Encoder/Decoder | 3e-4 | Primary learning objective |
| Codebook | No gradient | Updated via EMA (decay coefficient 0.99) |
| Downstream Transformer | 1e-4 | Fine-tuning pretrained model, use smaller learning rate |

Warmup steps 1000, total steps 100K, beta1=0.9, beta2=0.99, weight_decay=0.01 is a common starting point.

### 20.5 LAMB / LARS (Large Batch Training)

When batch_size exceeds 4096, vanilla Adam's performance degrades — large batch gradients have low variance, and the model tends to converge to sharp minima (sharp local minima with poor generalization).

LAMB (Layer-wise Adaptive Moments) scales the learning rate for each layer based on parameter norms on top of Adam: layers with large norms automatically get a lower learning rate, and layers with small norms automatically get a higher one. The effect is that you can scale batch size from 256 to 65536 without losing performance.

Implications for SID training: RQ-VAE training typically uses batch_size between 2048–8192, and contrastive learning modules require even larger batches (more negative samples). LAMB makes training more stable in these large batch scenarios.

---

## Part 21: Evaluation and A/B Experiments for Recommendation Systems

### 21.1 Offline Evaluation Metrics

Offline evaluation metrics fall into three categories:

**Retrieval metrics** (measuring "how many can be found"):

| Metric | Meaning |
|------|------|
| Recall@K | Among the K recommended items, how many of the user's truly liked items are hit |
| Hit@K | The proportion of users for whom at least one of the K recommended items is a hit |
| NDCG@K | Position-weighted recall — hits ranked higher are more valuable |
| MRR | The average of the reciprocal of the position of the first hit item |

**Ranking metrics** (measuring "how accurate the ranking is"):

| Metric | Meaning |
|------|------|
| AUC | The probability that the model scores a randomly drawn positive sample higher than a randomly drawn negative sample |
| LogLoss | Cross-entropy between predicted probabilities and true labels; lower is better |
| GAUC | AUC computed per user group then weighted-averaged, eliminating the effect of user activity level differences |
| Calibration | Whether predicted probabilities match actual proportions (if the model says 10%, there really are 10% clicks) |

**SID system-specific metrics**:

| Metric | Meaning |
|------|------|
| Codebook utilization | Proportion of codewords actually assigned to data (examined per layer) |
| Collision rate | Proportion of different items mapped to the same SID |
| Reconstruction error | MSE between original input and the output after encoder → quantize → decoder |
| SID diversity | How many distinct codeword prefixes the generated candidate SID set covers |
| Cold-start recall | The proportion of cold-start items retrieved via SID — the core value metric for SID |

### 21.2 Pitfalls of Offline Evaluation

Offline evaluation seems scientific, but it conceals multiple systematic biases:

**Pitfall 1: Exposure Bias.** Offline data only contains "items that have been impressed." Items that were never impressed are implicitly treated as "items the user doesn't like" — but a user not having seen a niche movie doesn't mean they wouldn't like it. If a new model can discover these items, offline evaluation actually penalizes it.

**Pitfall 2: Popularity Bias.** Popular items get more impressions and more positive samples, so the model tends to recommend popular items. A "dumb" model that only recommends popular items can still achieve a high AUC, but it has zero personalization capability.

**Pitfall 3: Sequence Leakage.** If the train/test split is not strictly time-based, the model may "see future" interactions — offline metrics are inflated, but performance tanks after deployment. The correct approach is strict time-split: train on data before time t, test on data after time t.

**Pitfall 4: Evaluation-Objective Misalignment (SID-specific).** RQ-VAE optimizes reconstruction error, but the final evaluation is recommendation performance. Low reconstruction error doesn't mean good recommendations — it might be precisely reconstructing information irrelevant to recommendation (e.g., the item's copyright disclaimer text). The correct approach: end-to-end evaluation of SID's impact on downstream Recall/CTR.

### 21.3 Online A/B Experiments

A/B experiments are the "gold standard" for recommendation systems. Offline evaluation can only filter out obviously bad approaches; the final effect must be validated through online A/B testing — letting real users vote with real money.

Standard process: randomly split users into a control group (old strategy) and a treatment group (new strategy), observe for 7–14 days (covering weekday and weekend cycles), then compare core metrics.

Metrics are designed in three tiers:
- **Guardrail metrics** (DAU, retention rate): Red lines that must not worsen. If guardrail metrics drop, the change cannot go live regardless of how much core metrics improve.
- **Core metrics** (CTR, CVR, GMV): Primary optimization targets that require statistically significant improvement.
- **Observational metrics** (diversity, freshness): Not used for decision-making, but help understand the mechanism behind the strategy's effect.

Special considerations for SID A/B testing: SID typically doesn't replace ANN retrieval but is added as an additional retrieval channel — comparing "with SID channel vs. without." Cold-start performance needs to be analyzed separately (slice out new items within the first 24 hours for independent analysis). You should also observe whether SID brings more long-tail item impressions.

### 21.4 Statistical Foundations of A/B Experiments

Hypothesis testing answers one core question: is the observed difference real or just random fluctuation?

Basic logic: assume the new strategy has no effect (null hypothesis H0), and calculate the probability of observing the current difference (or something more extreme) under this assumption — this is the p-value. If p < 0.05, it means "such an extreme difference is almost impossible under the null hypothesis," so we reject the null hypothesis and accept that "the new strategy is effective."

**Sample size requirements** depend on how small an improvement you want to detect. Detecting a 0.5% relative lift in CTR (from 5.0% to 5.025%) requires about 1 million samples per group. The smaller the lift, the larger the required sample size (proportional to 1/lift^2).

**Multiple metric correction**: Looking at 10 metrics simultaneously with p=0.05 each, you expect 0.5 false positives. The solution is Bonferroni correction — divide the threshold by the number of metrics (p < 0.05/10 = 0.005).

A common form of "cheating": seeing p=0.06 and deciding to "run it for two more days." This is statistically wrong (the multiple testing problem). The correct approach is to determine the duration and decision criteria before the experiment starts, then evaluate uniformly when the period ends.

Bayesian A/B is another paradigm: instead of outputting a p-value, it directly gives the "posterior probability that the new strategy is better than the old" — e.g., P(treatment > control | data) > 95%. More intuitive and flexible, but requires choosing a reasonable prior.

### 21.5 Model Calibration

Calibration means: the probability values output by the model must be **trustworthy**. If the model says a certain ad has a CTR = 10%, then if you verify with 1000 impressions, there should be approximately 100 clicks.

**Why do ad systems have extremely high calibration requirements?** Because bidding directly depends on predicted probabilities. CPC bid = predicted CVR × conversion value. If CVR is overestimated by 2×, the bid is 2× too high, the budget is overspent — and the advertiser will complain. eCPM ranking (CTR × Bid) also relies on the absolute accuracy of CTR, not just relative ordering.

Three calibration methods:

**Temperature Scaling**: The simplest — learns only one parameter. logit_calibrated = logit / T. T > 1 makes predictions more conservative (reducing extreme probabilities), T < 1 makes them more aggressive. Find the optimal T via grid search on the validation set.

**Isotonic Regression**: Uses a monotonically increasing piecewise linear function to map predicted probabilities to calibrated probabilities. No parametric assumptions, highly flexible, but prone to overfitting on small validation sets.

**Platt Scaling**: Learns two parameters a and b, computing sigmoid(a * logit + b). One more bias term than temperature scaling, able to handle systematic overestimation/underestimation.

Calibration quality is evaluated using ECE (Expected Calibration Error): split predicted probabilities into 10 bins, compute |actual positive sample ratio - average predicted probability| within each bin, then take the weighted average. Lower ECE is better; industrial systems typically require ECE < 0.02.

### 21.6 PCOC (Predicted Click Over Click) Monitoring

PCOC is the most critical daily monitoring metric in ad systems: predicted clicks / actual clicks. PCOC = 1.0 indicates perfect calibration; > 1.0 indicates overestimation (predicted more than actual); < 1.0 indicates underestimation.

Alert rules: trigger recalibration when PCOC > 1.2 or < 0.8; alert that the model may have issues when the day-over-day change exceeds 20%.

Common causes of PCOC spikes:
1. Feature delay or missing (model can't see the latest information, uses stale fallback values)
2. Data distribution drift (a flood of new campaigns, changes in user composition)
3. Sudden increase in cold-start item proportion (first day of the SID system, many new items lack behavioral statistics features)
4. Model bugs (feature engineering errors, a feature showing unexpected zero values)

---

## Part 22: Feature Engineering — The "Fuel" of Recommendation Systems

### 22.1 Feature Categories

Features in recommendation/ad systems can be divided into four major categories:

| Feature Category | Subcategory | Content |
|----------|------|------|
| User Features | Static | Age, gender, registration date, device model |
| | Dynamic | Active days in last 30 days, purchase frequency, average order value |
| | Sequential | Recent clicked item list, search query list |
| Item Features | Static | Category, brand, price, listing date |
| | Content | Title text, cover image, video frame features |
| | Statistical | Historical CTR, historical CVR, impression count |
| Context Features | Time | Hour, day of week, whether it's a holiday |
| | Device | iOS/Android, screen size, network type |
| | Location | City, scenario (homepage/search/detail page) |
| Cross Features | Explicit | User city × item category, time slot × item type |
| | Implicit | Crosses automatically learned via FM/DCN |

### 22.2 Feature Storage and Timeliness

Feature storage in recommendation systems is layered — features with different timeliness requirements go through different computation and storage pipelines:

| Layer | Timeliness | Storage | Typical Features |
|------|--------|------|---------|
| Offline features (T+1) | Daily | Hive/HDFS → Redis | User 30-day behavior statistics, item historical CTR |
| Near-line features | Minute-level | Kafka + Flink → Redis | Clicks in the last 1 hour, real-time CTR sliding window |
| Real-time features | Millisecond-level | Computed online at request time | Number of items user has seen in current session, positional encoding |

The SID system has somewhat unique feature requirements: the training phase only needs offline features (item multimodal embeddings), while the online inference phase requires real-time user behavior sequences — the SID sequences of items the user recently interacted with must be available in real time so that the Transformer can autoregressively generate the next recommendation.

### 22.3 The Evolution of Feature Crossing

Feature crossing has evolved from purely manual to fully automatic, going through five stages:

**Stage 1: Manual Crossing (early 2010s).** Data scientists manually constructed combination features like "age_20-30 × category_sports." Interpretable and controllable, but labor-intensive and unable to cover all useful crosses.

**Stage 2: FM (2010).** Each feature learns a low-dimensional vector, and crosses are represented by vector inner products. Automatically learns all second-order crosses, but cannot capture higher-order patterns.

**Stage 3: Deep&Wide / DeepFM (2016–2017).** The Wide part handles memorization (manual or FM crosses), the Deep part handles generalization (MLP automatically learns high-order crosses), and the two complement each other.

**Stage 4: DCN-V2 (2021).** Explicitly models arbitrary-order crosses: x(l+1) = x0 \* (Wl \* xl + bl) + xl. Each layer adds one order → L layers yield L+1 order explicit crosses. No manual design needed, and parameter efficiency is much higher than MLP.

**Stage 5: Transformer Self-Attention.** Interactions between features are automatically learned through attention weights, with no preset crossing order. HSTU (Meta, 2024) tokenizes dozens of features and feeds them directly into a Transformer, letting the model decide which combinations to attend to.

### 22.4 Engineering Challenges of Embedding Tables

Embedding tables in recommendation systems can be unimaginably large:

- 1 million items × 128 dims × 4 bytes = 512 MB (manageable)
- 1 billion users × 64 dims × 4 bytes = 256 GB (doesn't fit on a single machine)
- Plus embeddings for various feature IDs → TB-scale in total

Industry solutions:

**Hash collision method**: raw ID → hash(ID) % table_size → embedding. Set table_size to 1/10 of the actual ID count (90% collision rate); in practice, performance drops only 1–2%—because collisions mainly affect low-frequency IDs, and low-frequency IDs already have poor embedding quality anyway.

**Multi-hash combination**: Use 2–3 different hash functions, then average or concatenate the results to reduce the negative impact of collisions.

**Distributed embedding**: Shard storage across multiple machines by ID (Parameter Server), fetching on demand during training.

**SID approach: a fundamental solution**. No need to store an independent embedding for every item—only the codebook parameters are needed: 4 layers × 1024 codewords × 128 dims = 2 MB. From TB-scale down to MB-scale, saving five orders of magnitude in storage.

### 22.5 Feature Timeliness and Consistency

Features used in online inference and offline training must be consistent; otherwise "feature time travel" (Feature Leakage) occurs—the most common and most insidious bug.

Incorrect example: during offline training, using an item's "historical CTR" to predict clicks for a given day, but the "historical CTR" in the training data inadvertently includes data from the day being predicted. The result is inflated offline AUC (because the model peeked at the future), followed by a cliff drop after going online.

The correct approach is a strict "observation window": features for prediction time T can only come from data before T, and labels come from actual behavior at time T.

The solution for guaranteeing online/offline consistency is a Feature Store: a unified feature serving layer where offline training performs point-in-time lookups (replaying historical snapshots) and online inference returns the latest values—same feature key, same computation logic online and offline.

---

## Part 23: Multimodal Learning — Fusing Vision, Text, and Behavior

### 23.1 What Is Multimodal?

"Modality" in recommendation systems refers to different source forms of information—visual (product images, video thumbnails), text (titles, descriptions, reviews), audio (short-video voiceovers), behavioral (click/purchase sequences), and graph-based (category trees, social relationships).

Why fuse multiple modalities? Because any single modality is incomplete. A picture may look great but the title is misleading—relying only on images leads to bad recommendations; the title may be accurate but the image blurry—relying only on text recommends low-quality items; behavioral signals only work for old items with historical data—new items have no behavior to leverage. Multimodal fusion gives the system a more complete understanding of each item.

### 23.2 Multimodal Encoders

Each modality requires a dedicated encoder that compresses raw data into a fixed-length vector:

| Modality | Mainstream Encoder | How It Works | Output |
|------|-----------|---------|------|
| Image | Vision Transformer (ViT) | Split image into 16×16 patches → linear projection → Transformer | [CLS] token → 768-dim embedding |
| Text | BERT / RoBERTa | Tokenize → token embedding → Transformer | [CLS] token → 768-dim embedding |
| Video | Video MAE / TimeSformer | Sample frames → spatiotemporal attention | 768-dim embedding |
| Behavior sequence | SASRec / BERT4Rec | Item ID → embedding → Transformer | 128-dim user interest embedding |

The outputs of these encoders are exactly the inputs to RQ-VAE—quantizing high-dimensional multimodal embeddings into discrete Semantic IDs.

### 23.3 CLIP and Alignment Learning

CLIP (Contrastive Language-Image Pre-training) is a milestone in multimodal alignment:

```python
# CLIP's training objective:
# Matched (image, text) pairs should be close in embedding space
# Unmatched (image, text) pairs should be far apart in embedding space

class CLIP:
    def forward(self, images, texts):
        # Encode each modality independently
        image_emb = self.image_encoder(images)  # [batch, dim]
        text_emb = self.text_encoder(texts)     # [batch, dim]

        # L2 normalization
        image_emb = normalize(image_emb)
        text_emb = normalize(text_emb)

        # Similarity matrix
        logits = image_emb @ text_emb.T * exp(temperature)
        # logits[i][j] = similarity between image_i and text_j

        # Diagonal entries are positive pairs, others are negatives
        labels = arange(batch_size)  # [0, 1, 2, ..., N-1]

        # Bidirectional contrastive loss
        loss_i2t = cross_entropy(logits, labels)      # image-to-text
        loss_t2i = cross_entropy(logits.T, labels)    # text-to-image
        return (loss_i2t + loss_t2i) / 2

# The brilliance of CLIP:
# After training, images and text live in the same space!
# You can retrieve images with text, or retrieve text with images
# "Zero-shot": no need to train separately for each task
```

### 23.4 Multimodal Fusion Strategies

```python
# Three mainstream fusion approaches:

# 1. Early Fusion:
# All modalities are concatenated/interleaved at the input layer
# Example: ViT patch tokens + text tokens fed into Transformer together
# Advantage: deep cross-modal interaction
# Disadvantage: high compute cost, requires large amounts of aligned data

# 2. Late Fusion:
# Each modality is encoded independently, then concatenated/weighted at the end
# Example: image_emb ⊕ text_emb → MLP → final_emb
# Advantage: each encoder can be pre-trained independently
# Disadvantage: limited cross-modal interaction

# 3. Cross-Modal Attention:
# One modality queries information from another modality
# Example: text tokens as Q, image patches as K/V
# Q_text @ K_image^T → attention → V_image
# Advantage: flexible, selective interaction
# Disadvantage: requires designing the position and number of interaction layers

# SID systems typically use late fusion:
# Step 1: Use CLIP or similar models to obtain per-modality embeddings
# Step 2: Concatenate or weighted-average → unified embedding
# Step 3: Feed into RQ-VAE → discretize into SID
# This way the SID encodes both visual semantics and textual semantics
```

### 23.5 The Era of Large Multimodal Models (LMMs)

```python
# 2023-2025: Large multimodal models process multiple modalities in a unified way

# How GPT-4V / Gemini / Qwen-VL work:
# Image → ViT → visual tokens (analogous to text tokens)
# Text → tokenizer → text tokens
# All tokens are fed into a large model (unified Transformer)

# Impact on SID systems:
# Old paradigm: manually designed multimodal fusion → embedding → RQ-VAE
# New paradigm: multimodal large model directly outputs unified embedding → RQ-VAE

# Advantages:
# - No need to manually design fusion strategies
# - The large model's world knowledge helps understand items
# - Deeper cross-modal understanding (describing images, understanding brand meanings)

# Disadvantages:
# - High inference cost (running every item through the large model)
# - Usually only used offline (freeze the embeddings once obtained)
# - Large models may hallucinate → erroneous semantic representations
```

---

## Part 24: Online Learning and Model Update Strategies

### 24.1 Why Do We Need Online Learning?

```python
# The core challenge facing recommendation systems: Distribution Shift

# Scenario 1: Influx of new items
# E-commerce flash sales: tens of thousands of new products listed simultaneously
# Video platforms: millions of new videos uploaded daily
# If the model doesn't update → new items cannot be effectively recommended

# Scenario 2: Shifting user interests
# During the World Cup: everyone is watching football
# During a pandemic: demand for home goods surges
# If the model is stuck on last week's data → it cannot adapt to sudden trends

# Scenario 3: The system's own feedback loop
# Recommended A → user clicked → recommend more A → data biased toward A
# If not updated promptly → recommendations get narrower and narrower
```

### 24.2 The Model Update Strategy Spectrum

Update strategies from slowest to fastest:

| Strategy | Method | Latency | Use Case | Advantage | Disadvantage |
|------|------|------|----------|------|------|
| Offline retraining (T+1 day) | Retrain the model daily on full data | 24 hours | Content semantic models (CLIP/RQ-VAE) | Stable, reproducible, easy to debug | Cannot chase trending topics; 24h cold start for new items |
| Incremental training (hourly) | Fine-tune the existing model on the latest data | 1–6 hours | CTR/CVR models | Balances freshness and stability | May forget old knowledge |
| Real-time learning (minute-level) | Update with each mini-batch in real time | Minutes | Embedding layers, user interest models | Instantly reflects latest behavior | Unstable, noise-sensitive, engineering-complex |
| FTRL (request-level) | Trigger an update on every impression/click event | Seconds | Sparse weights in linear CTR models | Ultimate timeliness | Only applicable to convex models; not suitable for DNNs |

### 24.3 FTRL (Follow The Regularized Leader)

```python
# FTRL: Proposed by Google in 2013, designed specifically for online sparse learning
# Core capability: produces sparse solutions while learning online (many weights are exactly 0)

# Why do we need sparsity?
# A linear CTR model may have billions of features
# Most feature weights should be 0 (unimportant)
# SGD cannot produce exact 0s → the model is too large
# FTRL uses L1 regularization → unimportant feature weights are pushed to exactly 0

# Update rule:
# w_{t+1} = argmin_w (sum_{s=1..t} g_s * w + 1/2 * sum sigma_s * ||w - w_s||^2 + lambda1*|w|)

# Simplified understanding:
# If a feature's cumulative gradient < lambda1 → weight = 0 (pruned)
# If cumulative gradient > lambda1 → normal update

# Application in ads systems:
# Large model (DNN): retrained offline daily
# Small model (linear layer + FTRL): updated online in real time
# Ensemble of both: large model provides base estimates, small model applies real-time corrections
```

### 24.4 Update Strategy for SID Systems

Optimal update frequency for each component of the SID system:

| Component | Update Frequency | Reason |
|------|----------|------|
| Multimodal Encoder | Monthly / essentially never | Extremely high training cost for large models; semantics change slowly |
| RQ-VAE (Tokenizer) | Weekly / biweekly | The codebook needs to cover new items, but updating too frequently causes SID drift. After each update, all items need SID reassignment |
| Autoregressive Transformer | Daily (incremental training) | User behavior patterns change quickly and need to be tracked |
| SID Table (SID → Item mapping) | Real-time (assign SID as soon as a new item goes live) | New items cannot wait until the next retraining cycle to get a SID. The approach is to run the frozen RQ-VAE on the new item to obtain its SID |

New item SID assignment flow: New item listed (T=0) → extract multimodal features → Encoder → embedding (T+5s) → frozen RQ-VAE inference → SID (T+5.1s) → write to SID Table (T+5.2s) → can be retrieved by the generative model (T+5.3s). Cold-start time is under 10 seconds (vs. traditional collaborative filtering, which requires day-level data accumulation).

### 24.5 Catastrophic Forgetting and Solutions

```python
# Core problem of online / incremental learning: catastrophic forgetting
# The model forgets knowledge from old data while learning on new data

# Symptoms:
# Day 1: model accurately recommends category A
# Day 2: a flood of category B data arrives; after incremental training
# Day 3: recommendation quality for category A drops severely

# Solutions:

# 1. Replay Buffer
# Retain a portion of old data and mix it with new data for training
replay_data = random_sample(historical_data, 20%)  # keep 20% old data
training_batch = concat(new_data_80%, replay_data_20%)

# 2. Elastic Weight Consolidation (EWC)
# Restrict update magnitude for "important" parameters
# "Important" = parameters with large Fisher information matrix values
loss = task_loss + ewc_lambda * sum(F_i * (theta_i - theta_old_i)^2)

# 3. Knowledge Distillation
# Use the old model's outputs as "soft targets" for the new model
loss = alpha * hard_label_loss + (1-alpha) * KL(new_model_logits, old_model_logits)

# 4. Engineering approach: hybrid of periodic full retraining + incremental updates
# Weekly full retraining (prevent drift accumulation)
# Daily incremental training (track real-time changes)
# If incremental performance degrades → trigger early full retraining
```

## Part 25: Information Theory Fundamentals — Understanding the Theoretical Limits of Quantization

### 25.1 Shannon Entropy

```python
# Shannon Entropy: a measure of the "average uncertainty" of a random variable
# H(X) = -sum(p(x) * log2(p(x)))

# Intuition: flipping a fair coin → H = 1 bit (need 1 bit to encode the outcome)
# Flipping an unfair coin (99% heads) → H ≈ 0.08 bit (almost no encoding needed)

# Calculation example:
# Codebook K=1024, if used perfectly uniformly:
# H = -sum(1/1024 * log2(1/1024)) = log2(1024) = 10 bits
# This is the maximum information a single-layer codebook can carry

# If codebook usage is non-uniform (only 100 codewords are frequently used):
# Effective entropy << 10 bits
# → The codebook's information-carrying capacity is wasted

# Multi-layer RQ-VAE:
# Ideal case: L layers, K codewords per layer → total information = L * log2(K) bits
# 4 layers × 1024 codewords = 4 × 10 = 40 bits
# Enough to uniquely identify 2^40 ≈ 1 trillion distinct items
```

### 25.2 Mutual Information

```python
# Mutual Information: the "shared information" between two random variables
# I(X; Y) = H(X) - H(X|Y) = H(Y) - H(Y|X)
# "How much does uncertainty about X decrease after knowing Y?"

# In recommendation:
# I(User; Item) = how much uncertainty about what item to recommend decreases after knowing who the user is
# → Higher mutual information = stronger association between user and item

# In contrastive learning (InfoNCE):
# Maximize I(z_i; z_j) (mutual information between positive-pair representations)
# = Make positive pairs share more information = Make representations retain task-useful information

# In SID:
# I(SID; Item_features) should be as large as possible
# = SID should retain as much of the item's original information as possible
# = Reconstruction error should be as small as possible
# Information-theoretic perspective: reconstruction error ∝ H(Item|SID) = residual uncertainty about the item given its SID
```

### 25.3 KL Divergence

```python
# KL(P||Q): the "information distance" of P relative to Q
# KL(P||Q) = sum(P(x) * log(P(x) / Q(x)))
# Note: asymmetric! KL(P||Q) ≠ KL(Q||P)

# Properties:
# KL >= 0 (always non-negative)
# KL = 0 if and only if P = Q

# In VAE:
# KL(q(z|x) || p(z)) = distance between the encoder output distribution and the prior (standard normal)
# Minimizing KL → latent space approaches standard normal → can sample from standard normal to generate

# In recommendation (model calibration):
# KL(P_true || P_predicted) = gap between true distribution and predicted distribution
# Well-calibrated model: KL ≈ 0

# Two "directions" of KL:
# KL(P||Q): "forward KL" → Q cannot have mass where P is 0 (mode-seeking)
# KL(Q||P): "reverse KL" → Q tends to cover all modes of P (mass-covering)
# VAE uses reverse KL → tends to "cover" all data modes
# GAN uses forward KL (implicitly) → tends to "focus" on the sharpest modes
```

### 25.4 Rate-Distortion Theory

Rate-distortion theory directly answers a core question: **with a finite number of bits, how low can you push reconstruction error?**

```python
# Rate-Distortion Function R(D):
# Given a maximum allowable distortion D, what is the minimum number of bits (Rate) needed?

# R(D) = min_{p(z|x): E[d(x,x')] <= D} I(X; Z)

# Intuition:
# The harder you compress (lower Rate) → the more information lost → the greater the distortion
# The more distortion you tolerate → the fewer bits needed to represent the data

# From the RQ-VAE perspective:
# Rate = L * log2(K) bits (length of the SID)
# Distortion = reconstruction_error
# Training objective = minimize Distortion for a given Rate
# = find the optimal point on the Rate-Distortion curve

# Practical implications:
# Increasing RQ layers L → Rate increases → reconstruction error can be lower
# But increasing L → longer SID → slower Transformer generation
# Need to trade off between precision and efficiency
# Empirically: L=4, K=1024 is the current sweet spot (40 bits)
```

### 25.5 Optimal Transport

Optimal transport theory is the mathematical foundation of Sinkhorn codebook balancing.

```python
# Problem: you have a pile of "dirt" (data distribution) and some "holes" (codewords)
# How do you move dirt into holes to minimize total transport distance,
# while ensuring each hole receives the same amount of dirt?

# This is the Monge-Kantorovich optimal transport problem!

# Formalization:
# P = data distribution (uniform: each sample has weight 1/N)
# Q = target distribution (uniform: each codeword should be assigned N/K samples)
# C = distance matrix (C[i][j] = distance from sample i to codeword j)
# Objective: find transport plan T that minimizes <T, C>, subject to T's row sums = P, column sums = Q

# Sinkhorn algorithm = approximately solve OT with entropic regularization
# With the entropy regularizer, the optimal solution can be found quickly via alternating normalization (Sinkhorn iterations)
# Complexity: O(N*K) per iteration, typically 3-5 iterations suffice

# In RQ-VAE:
# At each quantization layer, use Sinkhorn to solve the optimal "data-to-codeword" assignment
# Constraint: each codeword is assigned the same amount of data → balanced utilization
# Result: utilization goes from 30% → 85%+, while maintaining reconstruction quality
```

---

## Part 26: The Past and Present of Tokenization

### 26.1 Text Tokenization

Understanding text tokenization helps explain why item tokenization (SID) follows a similar philosophy.

```python
# Evolution of text tokenization:

# 1. Character-level:
# "hello" → ['h', 'e', 'l', 'l', 'o']
# Small vocabulary (~100), but sequences are too long; hard to learn long-range dependencies

# 2. Word-level:
# "hello world" → ['hello', 'world']
# Short sequences, but huge vocabulary (millions); severe OOV problem

# 3. Subword — BPE/WordPiece:
# "unbelievable" → ['un', 'believ', 'able']
# Moderate vocabulary (~32K-100K); balances coverage and sequence length
# GPT uses BPE, BERT uses WordPiece

# BPE algorithm:
# Initialize: each character is a token
# Repeat: find the most frequent token pair, merge into a new token
# Until the vocabulary reaches the target size

# Example:
# "th" appears most frequently in the corpus → merge into "th"
# Then "the" appears most frequently → merge into "the"
# Then "ther" appears most frequently → merge into "ther"
# ...
```

### 26.2 Image Tokenization

```python
# Evolution of image tokenization:

# 1. ViT Patch Embedding (2020):
# Image 224x224 → split into 14x14 patches (each 16x16)
# Each patch is linearly projected into a token
# This is not "true" tokenization — patches are continuous vectors

# 2. VQ-VAE / dVAE (2017/2021):
# Image → Encoder → feature map → each spatial position quantized to a discrete codeword
# 256x256 image → 32x32 codeword grid → 1024 discrete tokens
# DALL-E used this approach to turn images into tokens for autoregressive generation

# 3. VQGAN (2021):
# VQ-VAE + GAN discriminator → higher quality reconstruction
# Larger codebook (16384 codewords) → finer-grained tokenization
# Stable Diffusion's latent space follows a similar idea

# 4. Semantic Tokens (2023-2024):
# Not pursuing reconstruction quality, but semantic meaning
# CLIP image features → VQ quantization → semantic tokens
# These tokens can be directly understood and generated by LLMs
```

### 26.3 Audio Tokenization

```python
# Audio Tokenization (SoundStream / EnCodec):

# Method: quantize audio with RVQ (Residual Vector Quantization)
# Exactly the same method as RQ-VAE used in recommendation!

# SoundStream (Google 2021):
# 24kHz audio → Encoder (convolution, 320x downsampling) → 75 frames/sec
# Each frame quantized with RVQ: 12 codebook layers, 1024 codewords each
# Bitrate = 75 * 12 * 10 = 9000 bps (vs uncompressed 384kbps)
# Compression ratio > 40x, while maintaining high audio quality

# EnCodec (Meta 2022):
# Similar architecture, variable bitrate (1.5 - 24 kbps)
# Fewer layers → lower bitrate → worse audio quality
# First few layers: semantic information (what is said)
# Later layers: acoustic details (how it's said, timbre)

# Perfect analogy with recommendation SIDs:
# Audio RVQ layers 1-4: semantic layers (content) ↔ SID layers 1-2: broad category
# Audio RVQ layers 5-12: detail layers (timbre)   ↔ SID layers 3-4: specific item
# Both follow: coarse-to-fine residual quantization!
```

### 26.4 Item Tokenization (SID)

Item Tokenization is the recommendation system's version of "BPE." BPE maps infinite text to a finite vocabulary (32K tokens); SID maps infinite items to a finite code table (K^L combinations).

The key differences between the two:

| Dimension | Text BPE | Item SID |
|------|----------|----------|
| Input | Character sequence | Continuous embedding |
| Method | Frequency statistics + merge rules | RQ-VAE quantization |
| Granularity | Subword (3-10 characters) | Semantic atom (one codeword) |
| Vocabulary size | 32K-100K | K^L (1024^4 ≈ 10^12) |
| Reversibility | Fully reversible (lossless) | Approximately reversible (lossy: reconstruction error exists) |

SID has three additional advantages over BPE: hierarchical semantics — shared SID prefixes imply semantic similarity, whereas BPE subwords don't necessarily have this property; cold-start capability — a new item only needs a single forward pass through RQ-VAE to obtain its SID, while a new word in BPE must be assembled from existing subwords; and the ability to inject collaborative signals — items liked by similar users can be given similar SIDs, whereas BPE is based purely on text statistics and does not consider downstream tasks.

---

## Part 27: Scaling Laws and the Large-Model Paradigm

### 27.1 Scaling Law (Kaplan et al. 2020)

```python
# Scaling Law: Model performance can be predicted by power laws

# L(N, D, C) ≈ a/N^alpha + b/D^beta + c/C^gamma
# L = test loss
# N = number of model parameters
# D = amount of data (number of tokens)
# C = compute (FLOPs)
# alpha, beta, gamma ≈ 0.05-0.1

# Implications:
# Increasing parameters by 10x → loss decreases by ~0.5-1.0
# Increasing data by 10x → loss decreases by ~0.5-1.0
# And the gains from both are approximately independent

# Chinchilla Law (2022):
# Given a compute budget C, parameter count N and data size D should grow proportionally
# Optimal: D ≈ 20 * N (each parameter needs about 20 tokens)
# GPT-3: 175B params, 300B tokens → severely undertrained
# LLaMA-2: 7B params, 2T tokens → over-trained for its size
# → Smaller models can catch up to larger models by training on more data
```

### 27.2 The "Scaling Law" Dilemma in Recommendation Systems

Why can't recommendation systems directly apply NLP's Scaling Law?

NLP's premises: 1) A finite vocabulary (~100K tokens) → more parameters = richer representations per token; 2) Compositional semantics — understanding "cat sat on mat" = understanding each word + compositional rules; 3) Pre-training data is virtually unlimited (internet text).

The reality of recommendation systems (Hash IDs): 1) Vocabulary = number of items (hundreds of millions and continuously growing) → more parameters = more independent embeddings; 2) Each item ID is an independent symbol with no compositionality — "Item_12345" and "Item_12346" have no semantic relationship; 3) Training data per item is limited (long-tail items have very few impressions).

The result: In NLP, scaling parameters from 1B → 100B produces qualitative leaps in capability (emergence); in RecSys, scaling the Embedding Table from 1B → 100B yields only marginal improvements — because the added parameters are all spent on "memorizing more independent items" and produce no generalization.

How SID changes this picture: A finite vocabulary of K^L SIDs (e.g., 1024^4 = 10^12) but only L*K codewords; compositionality — the semantics of SID [3,7,2,5] = the combination of each codeword; shared representations — items sharing the same prefix share representations, enabling natural generalization. SID creates the preconditions for a Scaling Law in recommendation systems.

### 27.3 The Pre-training → Fine-tuning Paradigm

```python
# The successful paradigm of LLMs: Pre-training → Fine-tuning → Alignment

# Pre-training:
# Large-scale unsupervised learning to learn general representations
# Task: next token prediction
# Data: full-scale internet text

# Fine-tuning:
# Small-scale supervised learning to adapt to specific tasks
# Task: dialogue/code/math
# Data: high-quality labeled data

# Alignment:
# RLHF / DPO, making the model conform to human preferences
# Task: respond in ways humans expect
# Data: human preference comparison data

# Analogous paradigm for SID systems:
# Pre-training: RQ-VAE learns general semantic representations of items (unsupervised)
# Fine-tuning: Transformer learns user preferences (supervised: behavioral data)
# Alignment: recommendation diversity/freshness/exploration (business constraints)
```

### 27.4 Emergent Abilities

```python
# Emergence: new capabilities suddenly acquired once model scale exceeds a certain threshold
# Example: GPT-3 (175B) suddenly gained few-shot reasoning ability
# Example: GPT-4 can pass the bar exam, GPT-3 cannot

# Does emergence exist in recommendation systems?
# Current observations:
# Scaling up → steady performance improvement (no "sudden" jumps)
# Possible reason: recommendation capabilities are more "continuous" than language understanding

# Potential emergence points with SID + large models:
# When the Transformer is large enough (>1B params) + enough training data:
# Possible emergent abilities:
# - Cross-category reasoning ("people who like sneakers also like sports earbuds")
# - Seasonality understanding (automatically learn to recommend down jackets in winter)
# - Long-term interest modeling (not just looking at recent behavior)
# - Need understanding (inferring "currently renovating a new home" from behavior)
# But these are still speculation, not yet confirmed by large-scale experiments
```

### 27.5 MoE (Mixture of Experts) and Recommendations

```python
# MoE: not all parameters participate in every computation
# A gating network selects the top-k "experts" to process the current input
# Effect: parameters increase by Nx, but compute only increases by ~2x

class MoELayer(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, num_experts=8, top_k=2, dim=512):
        self.experts = nn.ModuleList([FFN(dim) for _ in range(num_experts)])
        self.gate = nn.Linear(dim, num_experts)

    def forward(self, x):
        # Gating: select top-k experts
        gate_logits = self.gate(x)  # [batch, num_experts]
        top_k_values, top_k_indices = topk(gate_logits, k=2)
        weights = softmax(top_k_values)  # Normalize weights

        # Only compute the selected experts
        output = 0
        for i, (idx, w) in enumerate(zip(top_k_indices.T, weights.T)):
            expert_output = self.experts[idx](x)
            output += w.unsqueeze(-1) * expert_output
        return output

# MoE applications in recommendations:
# Different "experts" handle different types of users/scenarios
# Expert 1: e-commerce shopping users
# Expert 2: content consumption users
# Expert 3: social interaction users
# Gating: automatically routes based on user features

# MoE + SID:
# Different RQ-VAE experts handle different item categories
# Beauty items → Expert 1 (focuses on color, texture)
# Electronics items → Expert 2 (focuses on specs, brand)
# Food items → Expert 3 (focuses on flavor, origin)
# Gating: automatically routes based on item category/features
```

### 27.6 Large Model Inference Optimization

When SID systems use large Transformers, inference optimization is critical:

| Technique | Principle | Savings |
|------|------|------|
| KV Cache | Cache already-computed Keys/Values to avoid redundant computation | Autoregressive generation of N tokens from O(N^2) → O(N) |
| Flash Attention | Exploit GPU memory hierarchy to reduce HBM accesses | Attention computation 2-4x speedup, memory from O(N^2) → O(N) |
| Continuous Batching | Don't wait for all requests to finish; fill in new ones as each completes | GPU utilization from 30% → 80%+ |
| Speculative Decoding | Small model rapidly generates a draft, large model verifies in parallel | Generation speed 2-3x, quality unchanged |
| Quantization (INT8/INT4) | Represent weights with lower-precision numerics | Memory 2-4x, speed 1.5-2x, accuracy loss under 1% |

Optimizations specific to SID systems: 1) Prefix sharing — multiple beams may share the first 1-2 layers of codewords → KV Cache sharing; 2) Early termination — if the first two layers of codewords have already determined the category, later layers only refine; 3) Codeword constraints — after softmax at each layer, keep only "valid" codewords (certain codeword combinations correspond to non-existent items → skip them directly).

## Part 28: A Complete Overview of Regularization Techniques

### 28.1 Why Do We Need Regularization?

```python
# Overfitting: the model performs well on training data but poorly when generalizing to new data
# Essence: the model "memorized" noise in the training data rather than learning the true patterns

# Manifestations of overfitting in recommendation systems:
# - High offline AUC, but poor performance after going online
# - Good recommendations for popular items, but fails on new items
# - Extreme predictions for certain users (CTR > 0.99), clearly unreasonable

# Signal: training loss keeps decreasing, but validation loss starts increasing
# → Regularization is needed to "constrain" model complexity
```

### 28.2 L1 / L2 Regularization

```python
# L2 Regularization (Weight Decay):
# loss = original_loss + lambda * sum(w^2)
# Effect: pushes all weights toward 0, but not exactly to 0
# Intuition: "every feature is somewhat useful, but don't rely too much on any single one"
# Geometry: constrains weights within a sphere

# L1 Regularization (Lasso):
# loss = original_loss + lambda * sum(|w|)
# Effect: pushes unimportant weights to exactly 0 → feature selection
# Intuition: "keep only the truly useful features, cut everything else"
# Geometry: constrains weights within a diamond → exact zeros at the corners

# In recommendations:
# Embedding Tables typically use L2 (prevent embeddings from growing too large)
# Linear feature weights use L1 (automatic feature selection, paired with FTRL)
# DNN hidden layer weights use L2 (i.e., weight_decay in AdamW)
```

### 28.3 Dropout

```python
# Dropout: randomly "turn off" a portion of neurons during training (set to 0)
# At inference time, use all neurons (but scale weights proportionally)

class DropoutLayer:
    def forward(self, x, training=True, p=0.1):
        if training:
            mask = (torch.rand_like(x) > p).float()
            return x * mask / (1 - p)  # Scale to keep expectation unchanged
        else:
            return x  # No dropout at inference time

# Why does it work?
# 1. Prevents feature co-adaptation: each neuron can't rely on specific other neurons
# 2. Implicit ensembling: equivalent to training an ensemble of 2^n sub-networks
# 3. Noise injection: similar to data augmentation, improves robustness

# Empirical Dropout values in recommendation models:
# Embedding layer: p=0.0-0.1 (can't be too large, otherwise information is lost)
# MLP hidden layers: p=0.1-0.3
# Attention: p=0.0-0.1 (generally small in Transformers)
# Output layer: no dropout

# Note: using Batch Normalization and Dropout together can cause conflicts
# BN relies on batch statistics, Dropout changes the statistical distribution
# Modern practice: use LayerNorm + Dropout (no conflict)
```

### 28.4 Early Stopping

```python
# The simplest regularization: stop training when the validation set starts deteriorating

class EarlyStopping:
    def __init__(self, patience=5, min_delta=1e-4):
        self.patience = patience
        self.min_delta = min_delta
        self.best_loss = float('inf')
        self.counter = 0

    def should_stop(self, val_loss):
        if val_loss < self.best_loss - self.min_delta:
            self.best_loss = val_loss
            self.counter = 0
            return False  # Still improving
        else:
            self.counter += 1
            return self.counter >= self.patience  # No improvement for N consecutive times

# Early Stopping in RQ-VAE training:
# Monitored metrics: validation reconstruction error + codebook utilization
# Don't just look at the lowest loss — also check whether utilization is stable
# Sometimes loss is still decreasing, but utilization starts collapsing → should also stop
```

### 28.5 Label Smoothing

```python
# Label Smoothing: "soften" hard labels
# Original: y = [0, 0, 1, 0, 0]  (one-hot)
# Smoothed: y = [0.02, 0.02, 0.92, 0.02, 0.02]  (epsilon=0.08)

# Formula: y_smooth = (1 - epsilon) * y_hard + epsilon / K
# K = number of classes, epsilon = smoothing degree (typically 0.05-0.2)

# Why does it work?
# Prevents the model from being overconfident → logits won't tend toward infinity
# Equivalent to saying: "I'm not entirely sure the label is 100% correct"

# Application in SID generation:
# When the Transformer predicts the next codeword, label smoothing can be applied
# Original: P(code=37) = 1.0
# Smoothed: P(code=37) = 0.9, P(each of the other 1023 codewords) = 0.1/1023
# Effect: The model won't be overly certain that "the next codeword must be 37"
# → beam search can explore more candidates → recommendation diversity improves
```

### 28.6 Data Augmentation as Regularization

```python
# Data augmentation is essentially regularization: making the model insensitive to "reasonable variations"

# Data augmentation in recommendation systems:
data_augmentation_recsys = {
    "Sequence level": [
        "random_crop: crop subsequences (simulating a user joining midway)",
        "random_mask: randomly remove some items (simulating missing data)",
        "random_reorder: locally shuffle order (items with close timestamps may have no strict ordering)",
    ],
    "Feature level": [
        "feature_dropout: randomly drop certain features (simulating missing features)",
        "noise_injection: add Gaussian noise to continuous features",
        "mixup: mix features and labels of two samples",
    ],
    "Negative sampling level": [
        "hard_negative: select hard-to-distinguish negative samples",
        "curriculum: negative samples from easy to hard",
    ],
}

# Data augmentation for RQ-VAE:
# Add noise to item embeddings during training:
# z_e_noisy = z_e + gaussian_noise * sigma
# Effect: The codebook learns the "center" of a region, not overfitting to a single point
# sigma is usually very small (0.01-0.05); too large will damage semantics
```
