## Part 29: Recommendation System Architecture Evolution — From Monolithic Models to End-to-End

### 29.1 Traditional Layered Architecture

Classic recommendation systems adopt a four-layer funnel architecture:

| Layer | Input | Output | Time Budget | Methods | Core Objective |
|----|------|------|----------|------|----------|
| Retrieval | Full item pool (millions to billions) | Candidate set (~1,000–5,000 items) | Under 10ms | Two-tower ANN, ItemCF, trending, new items, SID generative | Coverage over precision — must not miss items the user might like |
| Pre-ranking | Candidate set (~1,000–5,000 items) | Ranking candidates (~100–500 items) | Under 20ms | Lightweight two-tower, simplified MLP, COLD | Filter obviously unsuitable items at low cost |
| Ranking | Ranking candidates (~100–500 items) | Ordered list (~50–100 items) | Under 50ms | DIN/DIEN, DCN-V2, BST/HSTU | Maximize precision; model can be very complex |
| Re-ranking | Ordered list (~50–100 items) | Final display (~10–30 items) | Under 10ms | MMR diversity, frequency capping, ad blending, ecosystem controls | Business constraints override model scores |

### 29.2 "Information Loss" Between Layers

The core problem with layered architectures is that errors in upper layers cannot be corrected by lower layers. Imagine the user truly likes item X, but item X was not selected during the retrieval stage — no matter how precise the ranking model is, item X will never be recommended. This is the meaning of "retrieval determines the ceiling; ranking determines the floor."

Sources of information loss at each layer: from retrieval to pre-ranking, the two-tower model has limited expressiveness, and inner products cannot model complex interactions; from pre-ranking to ranking, the pre-ranking model is too simple and may incorrectly eliminate good candidates; from ranking to re-ranking, business rules in re-ranking may override the ranking model's judgments.

SID generative retrieval alleviates this to some extent: traditional approaches require an item to exist in the index before it can be retrieved, causing index lag; SID, on the other hand, can directly "generate" item IDs — even combinations never seen during training may be generated — giving it stronger exploration capability, though it may also generate invalid SIDs (corresponding to non-existent items).

### 29.3 End-to-End vs. Layered

```python
# End-to-end paradigm: one large model handles all layers

# OneRec (Kuaishou 2024) attempt:
# A single Transformer simultaneously does:
# - User behavior modeling (replacing DIN/DIEN)
# - Candidate generation (replacing ANN retrieval)
# - Scoring (replacing the ranking model)

# Advantages:
# - Eliminates inter-layer information loss
# - End-to-end optimization with a consistent objective
# - No need to maintain multiple models

# Disadvantages:
# - Enormous compute (one model doing everything)
# - Latency may exceed budget (< 30ms requirement)
# - Difficult to debug (black box)
# - Hard to inject business constraints (diversity/frequency capping/budget/targeting)

# Industrial reality: hybrid approach
# SID generative retrieval (added as a new channel) + traditional ANN retrieval (retained)
# → pre-ranking → ranking → re-ranking
# Not replacement, but augmentation
```

### 29.4 HSTU (Meta, 2024) — Unified Sequence Modeling

HSTU (Hierarchical Sequential Transduction Unit) is the "Recommendation System Foundation Model" proposed by Meta in 2024. The core idea is to tokenize all signals in recommendation (behaviors/features/context), feed everything into a single large Transformer, and directly output CTR predictions.

Tokenization scheme: behavior tokens (items the user clicked/purchased → SID or learnable embedding), feature tokens (user age/gender/city → discretized + embedding), time tokens (behavior timestamps → time2vec encoding), candidate tokens (the candidate item currently being scored), special tokens ([CLS] for final prediction, [SEP] to separate different types).

Model scale is 12–24 layer Transformer, dim=512–1024, hundreds of millions of parameters (vs. tens of millions for traditional ranking models). The effect is a 2–5% CTR improvement, especially in sparse scenarios.

Relationship with SID: HSTU can directly use SID as the token representation for items → no need to learn an independent embedding for each item → parameter count is drastically reduced and generalization is enhanced.

### 29.5 Multi-Objective Optimization in Recommendation

Recommendation/ads systems typically need to optimize multiple objectives simultaneously: CTR (click-through rate, user engagement), CVR (conversion rate, commercial value), watch time (core metric for video recommendation), interaction rate (comments/likes/shares), diversity (recommendations shouldn't be too homogeneous), freshness (shouldn't always recommend old content).

Multi-objective optimization methods:

**1. Weighted sum** (simplest): score = w1*CTR + w2*CVR + w3*duration. The problem is how to set the weights, since the metrics have different scales.

**2. MMOE (Multi-gate Mixture of Experts)**: Multiple expert networks + one gate per task; different tasks automatically select different expert combinations. The advantage is that tasks can share feature interactions or remain independent.

**3. ESMM (Entire Space Multi-Task Model)**: Leverages the chain relationship between tasks: P(conversion) = P(click) * P(conversion | click). CVR is trained on the entire sample space, solving sample selection bias.

**4. Pareto optimization (2022+)**: Instead of pre-setting weights, search for solutions on the Pareto frontier. Guarantees that improving one metric does not worsen another. Implemented via multi-gradient descent (MOO-SVGD, PCGrad).

Relationship with SID: SID itself is multi-objective — reconstruction error vs. utilization vs. collision rate — and similar methods can be used to handle these conflicting objectives.

---

## Part 30: Practical Tuning Guide — RQ-VAE Training Checklist

### 30.1 Hyperparameter Selection Guide

Below are the most critical hyperparameters in RQ-VAE training and guidance for selecting them:

| Hyperparameter | Recommended Range | Selection Rationale |
|--------|----------|----------|
| Codebook layers L | 3–5 | Determined by item volume; K^L should be at least 10× the number of items. For example, with 10 million items, 4 layers of 1024 codebooks provide 10^12 capacity — more than enough |
| Codebook size K | 512–2048 | Too small means insufficient capacity; too large leads to low utilization. K=1024 is the most common sweet spot |
| Codeword dimension d | 32–128 | Generally 1/4 to 1/2 of the input embedding dimension. Note: too small (8–16) gives inflated utilization but high collision rate; too large (256+) causes utilization collapse |
| Commitment loss weight b | 0.1–0.5 | b too large causes the codebook to freeze and stop updating; b too small causes encoder outputs to drift and not converge toward codewords |
| EMA decay g | 0.99–0.999 | Larger values mean slower codebook updates — more stable but slower to adapt to new data |
| Sinkhorn temperature t | 0.05–0.2 | Smaller values make assignments harder (closer to argmin); larger values make them softer (closer to uniform distribution) |
| Sinkhorn iterations | 3–5 | Usually 3 iterations suffice for convergence; additional iterations yield diminishing returns |
| Learning rate | 1e-4 ~ 3e-4 | Encoder/Decoder optimized with AdamW; codebook does not use a learning rate — it updates via EMA |
| Batch Size | 2048–8192 | Sinkhorn requires large batches to effectively balance codeword assignments |

### 30.2 Training Phase Strategy

The recommended RQ-VAE training is divided into six phases, each with a clear objective and monitoring focus:

**Phase 0: Data Preparation.** Sample 1 million to 10 million items from the full item pool as the training set, extract multimodal embeddings (typically from CLIP or large models), then L2-normalize the embeddings — this step is extremely important. Finally, verify that the embedding distribution is reasonable: no all-zeros or NaNs.

**Phase 1: Codebook Initialization (Epoch 0).** Initialize the codebook using K-Means, which is an order of magnitude better than random initialization. Specifically, run K-Means clustering on the training data and use the cluster centers as initial codewords. For 1 million data points, this takes about 10 minutes.

**Phase 2: Training Without Sinkhorn (Epoch 1–10).** The goal is to let the codebook converge initially. In this phase, use only argmin assignment plus EMA updates without introducing any balancing strategy. Monitoring focus: reconstruction error should decrease rapidly.

**Phase 3: Introduce Sinkhorn (Epoch 10–50).** The goal is to balance codebook utilization. Replace argmin with Sinkhorn as the assignment strategy (training time only). Monitoring focus: utilization should gradually rise above 70%.

**Phase 4: Fine-Tuning (Epoch 50–100).** The goal is to converge to the final state. Reduce the learning rate (cosine decay) and let the model fine-tune under the current codebook structure. Monitoring focus: both reconstruction error and utilization should stabilize.

**Phase 5: Full Inference.** Freeze model parameters and run inference on the full item pool, assigning a Semantic ID to every item. Validation criterion: collision rate should be below 5%.

### 30.3 Common Mistakes and Fixes

Below are the seven most common mistakes in RQ-VAE training, along with their symptoms and fixes:

| Mistake | Symptom | Fix |
|------|------|------|
| Forgetting to L2-normalize inputs | Reconstruction error is extremely large; some codeword norms are extremely large or small | L2 normalize embeddings before feeding them in |
| Using detach incorrectly in EMA updates | Codebook doesn't converge; utilization fluctuates randomly | EMA updates must be performed under no_grad() |
| Leaving Sinkhorn on during inference | Online-offline inconsistency; item SIDs are non-deterministic at inference time | Use argmin at inference; Sinkhorn is only for training |
| Codeword dimension doesn't match encoder output dimension | Program errors or dimension misalignment | The encoder's final layer output dimension must equal the codeword dimension |
| Using Sinkhorn with too small a batch_size | Utilization is unstable; assignment results vary greatly across batches | batch_size should be at least 2048, or use queue-based Sinkhorn |
| Not checking for dead codes | Utilization looks acceptable (60%) but collision rate is very high | Periodically count each codeword's assignment frequency; reinitialize those with zero frequency |
| Incorrect residual computation in multi-layer RQ | Quantization precision does not improve as layers increase | Correct approach: residual = z_e - (z_q1 + z_q2 + ... + z_q_l-1), i.e., subtract the sum of all previously quantized layers, not just the output of the previous layer |

### 30.4 Monitoring Dashboard Design

The following four charts should be monitored in real time during training:

| Chart | X-axis | Y-axis / Series | Healthy Expectation |
|------|------|-------------|----------|
| Loss curve | training_step | total_loss, recon_loss, commit_loss | All three lines decrease; recon_loss is larger than commit_loss |
| Codebook utilization (per layer) | training_step | layer_1, layer_2, layer_3, layer_4 | Gradually rises from low, stabilizes at 70%–90% |
| Residual norm (per layer) | training_step | after_layer_1, after_layer_2, after_layer_3 | Decreases layer by layer (e.g., 1.0 → 0.3 → 0.1) |
| Codeword usage frequency distribution (histogram) | code_index (sorted by frequency) | Assignment count | As uniform as possible; there should be no extreme long tail |

After going live, you need to continuously monitor five key metrics: collision rate trend (daily granularity, should stay below 5%), SID update coverage (the proportion of new items that receive an SID), generative retrieval hit rate (the proportion of generated SIDs that correspond to valid items), cold-start item CTR (the cold-start effectiveness improvement brought by SID), and recommendation diversity (number of distinct items shown divided by total impressions).

### 30.5 Complete Timeline from Zero to Launch

A typical Semantic ID system takes roughly 12 weeks from R&D to launch:

**Week 1–2: Data and infrastructure.** Determine item feature sources (image-text, video, behavioral statistics), extract multi-modal embeddings (using existing large models), set up the training pipeline (PyTorch distributed), and prepare evaluation data (historical behavior sequences, cold-start item sets).

**Week 3–4: RQ-VAE training.** Conduct hyperparameter search (L, K, d, b), complete training and validation, analyze key metrics such as utilization, collision rate, and reconstruction error, then assign SIDs to all items.

**Week 5–6: Downstream Transformer.** Construct training data (convert user behavior sequences into SID sequences), train the autoregressive Transformer, and perform offline evaluation (Recall@K for cold-start items).

**Week 7–8: Engineering integration.** Deploy the SID Table to online storage (Redis or RocksDB), deploy the Transformer inference service, tune Beam Search parameters, and optimize latency (target P99 below 30 ms).

**Week 9–10: A/B experiment.** First validate no regression with 5% traffic, then ramp up to 20% to observe incremental effects. Analyze cold-start effectiveness, diversity improvement, and latency impact.

**Week 11–12: Full launch and ongoing operations.** After full rollout, build a daily update pipeline for assigning SIDs to new items, set up monitoring and alerting, and establish a periodic RQ-VAE retraining strategy (typically bi-weekly or monthly).

---

## Part 31: Attention Mechanism Variants and Optimizations

### 31.1 The Computational Bottleneck of Standard Self-Attention

Standard Self-Attention has a computational complexity of O(N² · d), where N is the sequence length and d is the hidden dimension.

In recommendation scenarios, user behavior sequences are typically 50–200 in length—relatively short—so standard Attention is entirely feasible. However, if you want to model longer histories (N = 1000+), the Attention matrix will contain 1000 × 1000 = 1 million elements. Multiplied by batch size and number of heads, memory is very likely to OOM, and O(N²) becomes the computational bottleneck.

For comparison, GPT-4's context window is 128K tokens. 128K squared is 16 billion elements—completely impossible to fit into GPU memory, hence the need for various optimization techniques.

### 31.2 Flash Attention

Flash Attention (Dao et al. 2022) does not change the mathematical result of the attention computation—it only changes *how* the computation is performed, by exploiting the GPU memory hierarchy to accelerate it.

GPUs have two levels of memory: HBM (High Bandwidth Memory—large but slow, 80 GB capacity, 2 TB/s bandwidth) and SRAM (on-chip cache—small but fast, 20 MB capacity, 19 TB/s bandwidth). The problem with standard Attention is that it requires three HBM read/write round-trips: first compute Q@Kᵀ to get a large matrix and write it back to HBM, then read from HBM to perform softmax, and finally multiply the softmax result by V and write back to HBM again—the entire process is IO-bound.

Flash Attention's approach is to split Q, K, V into small blocks (that fit into SRAM), complete the entire Attention computation within SRAM, and only write the final result back to HBM. This reduces HBM read/writes from three round-trips to one. The result: 2–4× speedup (especially for long sequences), memory drops from O(N²) to O(N) (no longer storing the full attention matrix), and precision is exactly the same (mathematically equivalent, not an approximation).

In an SID system: if user sequences are shorter than 200, Flash Attention offers limited improvement; but if you want to model a full day of behavior (sequence length exceeding 1000), Flash Attention is a must.

### 31.3 GQA (Grouped Query Attention)

GQA (Grouped Query Attention) is a compromise between MHA and MQA. In MHA, each head has independent Q, K, V, resulting in a large parameter count; in MQA, all heads share K and V (only Q differs), which is too aggressive and causes quality loss; GQA lets every G heads share one set of K, V.

Taking 32 heads with groups=4 as an example: Q retains 32 sets (each head independent), while K and V have only 4 sets (every 8 heads share one set). Parameter count is 32·d_q + 4·d_k + 4·d_v, significantly reduced compared to MHA's 32·d_q + 32·d_k + 32·d_v. For the KV Cache, MHA requires 32 × 2 × d × seq_len of cache, whereas GQA(G=4) only needs 4 × 2 × d × seq_len—an 8× saving.

In SID generative recommendation, every beam during Beam Search requires a KV Cache. Taking beam_width=100, seq_len=200, d=512, heads=32 as an example: MHA's KV Cache is roughly 26 GB (impossible to fit in GPU memory), while GQA(G=4) only needs about 3.2 GB (feasible). Therefore, GQA is a critical optimization for SID online inference.

### 31.4 RoPE (Rotary Position Embedding)

Positional encoding has gone through four generations: the first generation is the original Transformer's absolute sinusoidal encoding (fixed sin/cos patterns); the second generation is GPT-2's learnable positional encoding (parameter count is max_len × d); the third generation is T5's relative positional encoding (only cares about relative distance); and the fourth generation is RoPE (Rotary Position Embedding) used by LLaMA/Qwen, which encodes relative positions using rotation matrices.

The intuition behind RoPE is encoding positional information as "rotation angles." The embedding at position m is multiplied by a rotation matrix R(m), so the dot product of q_m and k_n automatically contains information about the relative position m−n. This brings three advantages: natural decay (the farther the distance, the naturally smaller the attention weight), extrapolation (can handle sequences longer than those seen during training), and efficiency (only requires element-wise rotation, no additional parameters).

In recommendation systems, user behavior sequences have a temporal order—recent behaviors should be more important than those from long ago. RoPE naturally achieves this "near-strong, far-weak" attention pattern, making it more suitable for sequence modeling in recommendations than absolute positional encoding.

### 31.5 Sliding Window / Local Attention

When user history is very long, not every item needs to attend to the entire history. Sliding Window Attention lets each position attend only to the most recent W positions (rather than all of them), reducing complexity from O(N²) to O(N · W), where W is typically set to 128 or 256.

The blocking strategy works as follows: positions 0–127 in Block 1 attend to each other and can see Block 0; positions 128–255 in Block 2 attend to each other and can see Block 1; and so on.

In recommendation scenarios, a user's 6-month behavior sequence may contain over 5,000 items. In practice, you don't need today's behavior to attend to every single click from 6 months ago. A better approach is to use Local Attention for short-range dependencies (recent behavior) while adding a small number of Global tokens (e.g., one summary token per week) to preserve long-range dependencies, balancing efficiency and long-range modeling capability.

This Longformer-style hybrid approach—Local Attention for short-range, Global Attention letting a few "summary" tokens see the entire history—is particularly well-suited for ranking scenarios that require long-term interest modeling.

---

## Part 32: Exploration vs. Exploitation

### 32.1 Basic Concepts

The Exploration-Exploitation Dilemma is one of the core challenges facing recommendation systems. Exploitation means recommending items the user is already known to like—high short-term reward; Exploration means recommending uncertain items that may uncover new interests, yielding long-term reward.

Problems with pure exploitation: users only see categories they already like, recommendations become increasingly narrow, forming a "filter bubble"; new items never get impressions, making cold start unsolvable; platform ecosystem suffers as creators leave and content dries up.

Problems with pure exploration: users see large amounts of uninteresting content, poor experience leads to user churn; short-term metrics (CTR/GMV) plummet, creating business pressure.

Therefore, a balance between exploration and exploitation must be found.

### 32.2 Classic Exploration Strategies

```python
# 1. Epsilon-Greedy:
# With probability epsilon recommend randomly, with probability (1-epsilon) recommend the best
# Simple but crude — doesn't distinguish "promising new items" from "pure random"

# 2. UCB (Upper Confidence Bound):
# score = estimated_reward + c * sqrt(ln(N) / n_i)
# n_i = number of times item i has been shown, N = total number of impressions
# Items with few impressions → high uncertainty → larger bonus → more likely to be explored
# Advantage: automatically balances, explores "uncertain high-potential" rather than pure random

# 3. Thompson Sampling:
# Maintain a Beta distribution for each item's CTR
# Each time, sample from the distribution and recommend the one with the highest sampled value
# Uncertain items → wide distribution → sampled value can be very high → chance to be recommended
# As data accumulates → distribution narrows → automatically transitions from exploration to exploitation

# 4. Information Gain:
# Select the item whose impression would maximally reduce "model uncertainty"
# Requires maintaining the model's posterior distribution (computationally expensive)
```

### 32.3 SID's Natural Exploration Capability

```python
# SID generative recommendation naturally possesses exploration capability:

# Traditional ANN retrieval:
# Can only retrieve items that "already exist in the index and have close embeddings"
# If a new item's embedding isn't good enough → it's never retrieved
# The index is deterministic → the same user always gets the same candidate set

# SID generative retrieval:
# Beam Search has a temperature parameter → can introduce randomness
# Temperature > 0 → doesn't always pick the highest-probability codeword → explores new paths
# Prefix sharing → explores different specific items within the same broad category

# Temperature-controlled exploration:
def beam_search_with_exploration(model, prefix, beam_width, temperature=1.0):
    for layer in range(num_layers):
        logits = model.predict_next_code(prefix)  # [beam, K]

        if temperature != 1.0:
            logits = logits / temperature  # Temperature scaling

        probs = softmax(logits)
        # temperature > 1: flatter distribution → more exploration
        # temperature < 1: sharper distribution → more exploitation
        # temperature = 1: original distribution

        top_k_codes = sample(probs, k=beam_width)  # Sample instead of argmax
        prefix = extend(prefix, top_k_codes)
    return prefix

# Temperature scheduling in practice:
# Heavy users (rich behavior): temperature=0.8 (more exploitation, already have enough information)
# New users (sparse behavior): temperature=1.5 (more exploration, discover interests)
# Cold-start items: force sampling of low-frequency codewords in certain beams
```

### 32.4 Bandits and Recommendation

```python
# Multi-Armed Bandit Applications in Recommendations:

# Classic MAB: multiple slot machines, each with a different expected payoff — which to pull?
# Recommendation MAB: multiple items, each with a different CTR — which to recommend?

# Contextual Bandit:
# Considers not just the item but also the user context
# P(click | user_context, item) → personalized exploration

# LinUCB (2010):
# Assumes CTR is a linear function of context features + uncertainty
# score = theta^T * x + alpha * sqrt(x^T * A^{-1} * x)
# First term: expected reward (exploitation)
# Second term: uncertainty (exploration)

# In advertising systems:
# New advertiser's ads → uncertain CTR → exploration needed
# But exploration has a cost (showing a bad ad to users → poor user experience)
# Ad systems explore more conservatively, typically using a small fraction of traffic for exploration

# SID + Bandit:
# Thompson Sampling with SID prefixes:
# Maintain a CTR posterior distribution for each SID prefix
# Prefix [3,7] has many insufficiently explored items
# → Thompson Sampling may sample a high value → triggering exploration of that prefix
```

---

## Part 33: Recommendation Diversity and Fairness

### 33.1 Why Does Diversity Matter?

```python
# Three dimensions of diversity:
diversity_dimensions = {
    "Category diversity": "How many different categories the recommendations cover (not just one type)",
    "Coverage": "Proportion of all platform items that have been recommended (whether the long tail gets a chance)",
    "Intra-list diversity": "Degree of difference among items within a single recommendation list",
}

# Consequences of lacking diversity:
# Users: get bored → decreased engagement → churn
# Creators/merchants: Matthew effect at the top → no opportunity for newcomers → unhealthy ecosystem
# Platform: short-term metrics look good but long-term growth stagnates

# Diversity vs. accuracy trade-off:
# The 10 most relevant items in a recommendation list might all be sneakers
# But a better user experience might be: 3 shoes + 2 clothing items + 2 accessories + 3 others
# The latter may have slightly lower CTR but better long-term retention
```

### 33.2 Diversity Algorithms

```python
# 1. MMR (Maximal Marginal Relevance):
# Greedy selection: each time pick the item most different from already-selected items and relevant to user interests
def mmr_rerank(candidates, selected, lambda_param=0.5):
    # lambda=1: pure relevance (no diversity consideration)
    # lambda=0: pure diversity (no relevance consideration)
    for _ in range(target_size - len(selected)):
        scores = []
        for item in candidates:
            relevance = similarity(item, user_profile)
            max_sim = max(similarity(item, s) for s in selected)
            score = lambda_param * relevance - (1-lambda_param) * max_sim
            scores.append(score)
        best = candidates[argmax(scores)]
        selected.append(best)
        candidates.remove(best)
    return selected

# 2. DPP (Determinantal Point Process):
# Probabilistic model: jointly models quality and diversity
# P(subset S) ∝ det(L_S)  (L = quality × diversity kernel matrix)
# The determinant naturally penalizes similar items → the selected set is inherently diverse

# 3. Category quota system:
# Simple but effective engineering approach
quota_rules = {
    "Same category max": 3,    # No more than 3 from the same category
    "Same brand max": 2,       # No more than 2 from the same brand
    "Same price range max": 5, # No more than 5 from the same price range
}
```

### 33.3 SID and Diversity

SID's hierarchical structure naturally supports diversity control. A four-layer SID [Layer1, Layer2, Layer3, Layer4] corresponds to semantic granularity from coarse to fine — Layer 1 is the major category (sports/beauty/electronics), Layer 2 is the subcategory (running/basketball/yoga), Layer 3 is the style (casual/professional/trendy), and Layer 4 distinguishes specific items.

Diversity constraints can be enforced at different levels:

- **Level 1 diversity**: Recommendation results cover at least 3 different Layer1 codewords → ensures category diversity
- **Level 2 diversity**: Under the same Layer1, Layer2 values do not repeat → subcategory diversity

The method for implementing diversity in Beam Search is **Diverse Beam Search**: split the beam width into multiple groups (e.g., 5 groups), and each group is penalized during independent search for choosing the same codewords as previous groups. The effect is that the generated SID candidate set covers more distinct "semantic regions" — no need to wait until the re-ranking stage for diversity; the retrieval stage is inherently diverse.

### 33.4 Recommendation Fairness

Recommendation fairness involves multiple perspectives:

**User-side fairness**: Is the recommendation quality equal across different user groups? A typical problem is that active users get accurate recommendations while silent users get poor ones — the Matthew effect. SID helps mitigate this through semantic generalization: even with sparse behavior, as long as the content is similar, users can receive reasonable recommendations.

**Supply-side fairness**: Is the impression distribution fair across different creators/merchants? Big brands can easily monopolize impressions while small merchants get buried. SID's prefix-sharing mechanism allows small merchants to gain initial traffic through category semantics — as long as your product semantically belongs to a popular category, it has a chance to be generated.

**Content fairness**: Does the recommendation exhibit systematic bias toward certain sensitive attributes? Fairness constraints can be introduced during SID quantization — for example, requiring items from different groups to be uniformly distributed across the codebook, preventing certain types of items from being systematically squeezed into a few codewords.

---

## Part 34: Model Compression and Quantized Deployment

### 34.1 Why Is Model Compression Needed?

```python
# Online inference constraints for recommendation systems:
serving_constraints = {
    "Latency": "P99 < 30ms (from receiving request to returning results)",
    "QPS": "100K-1M (requests processed per second)",
    "Memory": "Single GPU 16-80GB (must fit model + KV Cache + batch)",
    "Cost": "GPUs are expensive, inference cost accounts for 50%+ of total cost",
}

# Larger model → higher accuracy → but slower inference and higher cost
# Need to trade off between accuracy and efficiency
```

### 34.2 Knowledge Distillation

```python
# Transfer knowledge from a large model (Teacher) to a small model (Student)

class Distillation:
    def __init__(self, teacher, student, temperature=4.0, alpha=0.7):
        self.teacher = teacher  # Large model (frozen)
        self.student = student  # Small model (to be trained)
        self.T = temperature
        self.alpha = alpha

    def loss(self, x, labels):
        # Teacher's soft prediction
        with torch.no_grad():
            teacher_logits = self.teacher(x)
            teacher_soft = softmax(teacher_logits / self.T)

        # Student's prediction
        student_logits = self.student(x)
        student_soft = softmax(student_logits / self.T)

        # Distillation loss (learn teacher's soft labels)
        distill_loss = KL_div(student_soft, teacher_soft) * self.T^2

        # Hard label loss (learn ground truth labels)
        hard_loss = cross_entropy(student_logits, labels)

        # Combined
        return self.alpha * distill_loss + (1 - self.alpha) * hard_loss

# Distillation in SID systems:
# Teacher: 12-layer Transformer (dim=512), best offline evaluation performance
# Student: 4-layer Transformer (dim=256), meets online latency requirements
# Distillation: Student learns Teacher's probability distribution over "next codeword"
# Result: Student achieves 95% of Teacher's performance but with 4x inference speed
```

### 34.3 Weight Quantization

```python
# Map FP32 weights to low-precision integers:

# INT8 quantization (8-bit):
# FP32 → INT8: dynamic range mapping
# scale = max(abs(weights)) / 127
# quantized = round(weights / scale)
# dequantized = quantized * scale  # approximate recovery
# Accuracy loss: < 0.5% (virtually lossless for recommendation systems)
# Speed improvement: 2-3x (INT8 computation is faster)
# Memory savings: 4x (4 bytes → 1 byte)

# INT4 quantization (4-bit):
# More aggressive compression, but greater accuracy loss
# Typically requires advanced methods like GPTQ / AWQ
# Accuracy loss: 1-3% (acceptable for some tasks)
# Speed improvement: theoretically 4-8x (depends on hardware support)

# Quantization strategy for SID systems:
# Transformer weights: INT8 (mature, virtually lossless)
# Embedding / Codebook: keep FP16 (lookup tables, no computation needed)
# Attention scores: FP16 (softmax requires higher precision)
```

### 34.4 Pruning

```python
# Remove "unimportant" connections/neurons from the model

# Structured pruning (recommended):
# Remove entire attention heads / FFN neurons
# Advantage: directly reduces model size, achieves real speedup
# Approach: rank by importance score, remove the least important ones

# Unstructured pruning:
# Set individual weights to 0 (producing sparse matrices)
# Advantage: higher compression ratio
# Disadvantage: requires special hardware/software support for sparse computation

# Pruning experience in recommendations:
# Transformer attention heads have significant redundancy
# Typically 30-50% of heads can be removed with almost no performance loss
# FFN layers also have significant redundancy (FFN width is usually 4x d)
# Narrowing to 2x results in minimal performance loss

# Pruning + distillation combination:
# 1. Train a large model (Teacher)
# 2. Prune to obtain a small model structure (Student structure)
# 3. Use distillation to recover accuracy lost from pruning
# → Usually better results than directly training a small model
```

### 34.5 Inference Framework Selection

Comparison of mainstream inference frameworks:

| Framework | Advantages | Disadvantages | Use Cases |
|------|------|------|----------|
| TensorRT (NVIDIA) | Ultimate optimization, fastest GPU inference | Only supports NVIDIA GPUs, complex deployment | High QPS online services |
| ONNX Runtime | Cross-platform, simple and easy to use | Performance not as extreme as TensorRT | Quick launch, multi-hardware compatibility |
| vLLM | Designed specifically for LLM inference, continuous batching | Primarily targets generative tasks | SID generative inference (autoregressive Transformer) |
| Triton Inference Server | Multi-model management, dynamic batching, A/B testing | Complex configuration | Production multi-model deployment |

The recommended combination for SID systems is: RQ-VAE inference (offline batch) uses PyTorch + DataParallel for simplicity and speed; Transformer online inference uses vLLM or TensorRT; model management uses Triton Server for model version switching and canary releases.

---

## Part 35: Mathematical Toolbox — Vector Spaces and Distance Metrics

### 35.1 Vector Space Fundamentals

In recommendation systems, everything is a vector: users are represented as 128-dimensional vectors, items are represented as 128-dimensional vectors, queries are 768-dimensional vectors after BERT encoding, and codewords are likewise d-dimensional vectors.

Core vector operations include: inner product `<a, b> = sum(a_i * b_i)`, norm `||a|| = sqrt(sum(a_i^2))`, cosine similarity `cos(a, b) = <a, b> / (||a|| * ||b||)`, and Euclidean distance `d(a, b) = ||a - b|| = sqrt(sum((a_i - b_i)^2))`.

The differences among the three distance metrics are: inner product considers both direction and magnitude, so large vectors naturally score higher; cosine similarity only looks at direction and ignores magnitude, making it the standard choice after normalization; Euclidean distance measures absolute distance, and it is used for RQ-VAE codeword assignment.

There is a deep relationship among them: if vectors a and b are both L2-normalized (norm equals 1), then the inner product equals the cosine similarity, and the squared Euclidean distance equals 2 - 2*cos(a,b)—the three are equivalent. This is why L2 normalization is performed in recommendation systems.

### 35.2 The Curse of High-Dimensional Spaces

The "Curse of Dimensionality" refers to the phenomenon where intuition completely breaks down in high-dimensional spaces.

The first phenomenon is that all points become "far from" the center: the volume of a d-dimensional unit hypersphere tends toward 0 as d increases, and in high dimensions all data points "migrate" to the vicinity of the sphere's surface. The second phenomenon is distance concentration: in high dimensions, the distance between any two points tends toward the same value sqrt(d), the ratio of maximum distance to minimum distance tends toward 1, making the concept of "nearest neighbor" ambiguous, and K-Means performs poorly in high dimensions. The third phenomenon is volume explosion: the diagonal of a d-dimensional unit hypercube has length sqrt(d)—when d=128, the diagonal is 11.3 (while each side is only 1)—so data is extremely sparse in high dimensions.

The impact on RQ-VAE is: the larger the codeword dimension d, the more distance concentration makes argmin unreliable (multiple codewords end up at roughly the same distance), a few codewords "monopolize" assignments leading to low utilization, and dispersed gradient signals make updates difficult.

There are three solutions: reduce codeword dimension (use d=32–64 instead of 128–256); use Product Quantization to split the high-dimensional space into multiple low-dimensional subspaces; use OPQ to rotate into a coordinate system with uniform variance before quantization.

### 35.3 Matrix Factorization Perspective

The classic approach in recommendation systems is matrix factorization. The user–item interaction matrix R (mostly empty) is approximately decomposed as R ≈ U * V^T, where U is the user matrix (dimension n_users × k), V is the item matrix (dimension n_items × k), and k is the latent factor dimension. At prediction time, R[u][i] ≈ the inner product of U[u] and V[i].

SID has a deep connection to matrix factorization: matrix factorization yields continuous latent factors V, while SID essentially performs discrete quantization on V, obtaining V_quantized—equivalent to discretizing an item's continuous representation into a combination of a finite number of "semantic prototypes."

Discretization offers four core values: in terms of storage, a continuous vector requires 128 floats, while an SID needs only 4 integers; in terms of retrieval, vector ANN search is approximate, while SID lookup is exact; in terms of generation, you cannot "generate" a continuous vector, but you can autoregressively "generate" an SID sequence; in terms of compositional generalization, after the model has seen [3,7,_,_] and [_,_,2,5], it can compose [3,7,2,5].

### 35.4 Product Quantization (PQ)

PQ (Product Quantization) is RQ's "sibling method." Understanding PQ helps in understanding the design choices behind RQ.

The core idea of PQ is to split the d-dimensional space into M subspaces and quantize each subspace independently. For example, with d=128 and M=4, each subspace is 32-dimensional. During encoding, the input vector x is split into 4 segments, each segment finds its nearest codeword in the corresponding codebook, and the final result is PQ code = [code_1, code_2, code_3, code_4].

The core differences between RQ and PQ:

| Dimension | PQ (Product Quantization) | RQ (Residual Quantization) |
|------|---------------|---------------|
| Strategy | Dimension splitting, independent quantization per subspace | Precision splitting, layer-by-layer residual encoding |
| Codeword meaning | Each codeword represents a pattern in one subspace | Coarse-to-fine semantic hierarchy |
| Meaning of similar SIDs | Any matching segment means a shared subspace pattern | Shared prefix means same broad category |
| Capacity | K^M | K^L (same as PQ) |

RQ's advantage for recommendations lies in: hierarchical semantics (prefix represents broad category, suffix represents fine-grained subdivision, human-interpretable), support for hierarchical generation (generate the broad category first, then refine), and the ability to build a prefix tree for prefix-based indexing to accelerate retrieval. PQ's advantage for search is: distance computation can be accelerated via lookup tables (ADC: Asymmetric Distance Computation), making it better suited for ANN retrieval (IVF-PQ is the industry standard).

---

## Part 36: Loss Function Encyclopedia—From Cross-Entropy to Triplet Loss

### 36.1 Cross-Entropy Loss

```python
# The most basic and most commonly used classification loss

# Binary Cross-Entropy (BCE):
# L = -[y * log(p) + (1-y) * log(1-p)]
# y ∈ {0, 1}: ground-truth label
# p ∈ (0, 1): predicted probability

# Multi-class Cross-Entropy (CE):
# L = -sum(y_i * log(p_i))  for i in classes
# y: one-hot vector, p: softmax output

# In SID systems:
# The Transformer predicts the next codeword:
# logits = model(input_sequence)  # [batch, K]
# loss = cross_entropy(logits, target_code)  # target_code ∈ {0,...,K-1}
# This is a standard K-class classification problem!

# Numerical stability:
# Don't compute softmax first then log → use log_softmax in one step
# PyTorch's CrossEntropyLoss already does this optimization internally
# log_softmax(x_i) = x_i - log(sum(exp(x_j)))
# Uses the log-sum-exp trick to prevent overflow
```

### 36.2 Focal Loss

```python
# Addresses positive/negative sample imbalance (He et al. 2017, RetinaNet)

# Standard CE: L = -log(p_t)
# Focal Loss: L = -(1-p_t)^gamma * log(p_t)
# gamma: focusing parameter (typically 2.0)

# Intuition:
# "Easy samples" (model is already very confident): p_t close to 1 → (1-p_t)^gamma ≈ 0 → minimal contribution
# "Hard samples" (model is uncertain): p_t close to 0.5 → (1-p_t)^gamma ≈ 0.25 → larger contribution
# → Automatically focuses on "hard samples," ignoring "already learned ones"

# Application in recommendations:
# CTR data is severely imbalanced: 99% no-click, 1% click
# Without Focal Loss: model learns to "predict no-click for everything" and achieves 99% accuracy
# With Focal Loss: model is forced to focus on "which ones should be clicked but were predicted wrong"

# In SID codeword prediction:
# If certain codewords are very common (head codewords)
# The model may become overconfident on head codewords, neglecting tail codeword learning
# Focal Loss can alleviate this "head laziness" phenomenon
```

### 36.3 Triplet Loss

```python
# Core idea: anchor and positive should be closer than anchor and negative

# L = max(0, d(anchor, positive) - d(anchor, negative) + margin)

# Interpretation:
# d(a, p) - d(a, n) < -margin → loss = 0 (already satisfied, no update)
# d(a, p) - d(a, n) > -margin → loss > 0 (not satisfied, needs update)

# In recommendations:
# anchor: user embedding
# positive: embedding of an item the user clicked
# negative: embedding of an item the user didn't click
# Effect: user embedding moves closer to liked items, farther from disliked ones

# Choosing margin:
# margin=0.1: lenient → model easily satisfied → learning is not fine-grained
# margin=1.0: strict → model struggles to satisfy → training may be unstable
# Empirical range: 0.2-0.5 (depends on the scale of the embedding space)

# Triplet Loss vs InfoNCE:
# Triplet: looks at only one positive + one negative sample at a time
# InfoNCE: one positive + all others in the batch as negatives
# InfoNCE is more efficient (one batch provides more signal), now more mainstream
```

### 36.4 RQ-VAE's Composite Loss

```python
# RQ-VAE training involves a combination of three losses:

class RQVAELoss:
    def compute(self, x, x_hat, z_e, z_q_sum, all_z_q):
        # 1. Reconstruction loss: output should be as close to input as possible
        recon_loss = F.mse_loss(x_hat, x)

        # 2. VQ loss: codewords move toward encoder output (updates the codebook)
        # Note: if using EMA to update the codebook, this term can be removed
        vq_loss = F.mse_loss(z_q_sum, z_e.detach())

        # 3. Commitment loss: encoder output moves toward codewords (updates the encoder)
        commit_loss = F.mse_loss(z_e, z_q_sum.detach())

        # Total loss
        total = recon_loss + vq_loss + beta * commit_loss
        return total

    # Gradient flow for each term:
    # recon_loss → updates Encoder and Decoder via STE
    # vq_loss → updates the codebook (or replaced by EMA)
    # commit_loss → updates the Encoder (prevents its output from drifting too far from codewords)

# Tuning loss weights:
# beta too large (>1.0): Encoder is constrained, cannot freely explore good representations
# beta too small (<0.01): Encoder output may drift, far from codewords → large STE gradient bias
# sweet spot: beta = 0.1-0.5
```

### 36.5 Contrastive Loss Variants

```python
# InfoNCE (most commonly used):
# L = -log(exp(sim(z_i, z_j)/tau) / sum_k(exp(sim(z_i, z_k)/tau)))

# NT-Xent (Normalized Temperature-scaled CE, used in SimCLR):
# Essentially the same as InfoNCE, just with more explicit positive/negative sample definitions
# Positive samples: two augmented views of the same sample
# Negative samples: all augmented views of other samples in the batch

# Supervised Contrastive Loss:
# Leverages label information: all samples of the same class are positives
# L = -sum_{p in P(i)} log(exp(sim(z_i, z_p)/tau) / sum_k(exp(sim(z_i, z_k)/tau)))
# P(i) = all samples that share the same class as sample i

# Application of SupCon in SID:
# Items of the same category/brand are positive pairs
# → The representations learned by RQ-VAE naturally cluster same-category items
# → After quantization, they are more likely to share SID prefixes (semantic hierarchy alignment)
```

---

## Part 37：Batch Normalization vs Layer Normalization

### 37.1 Why Do We Need Normalization?

```python
# Problem: Internal Covariate Shift
# The input distribution of each layer changes as parameters of preceding layers update
# → Later layers must constantly adapt to new input distributions → training is slow and unstable

# What normalization does:
# Keeps the input to each layer at a stable distribution (mean 0, variance 1)
# → Later layers don't need to adapt to distribution shifts → training is faster and more stable
# → Also has a regularization effect (because batch statistics are stochastic)
```

### 37.2 Batch Normalization (BN)

```python
# BN: normalizes along the same feature dimension across a batch
class BatchNorm:
    def forward(self, x):
        # x: [batch_size, features]
        if self.training:
            mean = x.mean(dim=0)  # mean along the batch dimension
            var = x.var(dim=0)    # variance along the batch dimension
            # Update running statistics (used during inference)
            self.running_mean = 0.9 * self.running_mean + 0.1 * mean
            self.running_var = 0.9 * self.running_var + 0.1 * var
        else:
            mean = self.running_mean
            var = self.running_var

        x_norm = (x - mean) / sqrt(var + eps)
        return self.gamma * x_norm + self.beta  # learnable scale and shift

# Problems with BN:
# 1. Depends on batch statistics → unstable when batch_size is small
# 2. Training and inference behavior are inconsistent (training uses batch stats, inference uses running stats)
# 3. Not suitable for variable-length sequences (different positions should not be mixed during normalization)
```

### 37.3 Layer Normalization (LN)

```python
# LN: Normalize across all feature dimensions for a single sample
class LayerNorm:
    def forward(self, x):
        # x: [batch_size, seq_len, features]
        mean = x.mean(dim=-1, keepdim=True)  # Mean along feature dimension
        var = x.var(dim=-1, keepdim=True)    # Variance along feature dimension
        x_norm = (x - mean) / sqrt(var + eps)
        return self.gamma * x_norm + self.beta

# Advantages of LN:
# 1. No dependency on batch → works even with batch_size=1
# 2. Consistent behavior during training and inference (no running statistics)
# 3. Suitable for sequential data (each position normalized independently)
# 4. Perfect for Transformers!

# LN placement in Transformers:
# Pre-LN (modern practice, more stable):
# x = x + Attention(LN(x))
# x = x + FFN(LN(x))

# Post-LN (original Transformer):
# x = LN(x + Attention(x))
# x = LN(x + FFN(x))
# Requires smaller learning rate and warmup

# RMSNorm (used by LLaMA/Qwen):
# Removes mean centering, only does RMS normalization
# RMS(x) = sqrt(mean(x^2))
# x_norm = x / RMS(x) * gamma
# Faster (one fewer mean computation), nearly identical performance
```

### 37.4 Normalization Choices in RQ-VAE

Normalization strategy choices for each RQ-VAE component:

| Component | Recommended Approach | Reason |
|------|----------|------|
| Encoder (MLP) | LayerNorm (after each layer) | Stabilizes training, consistent with Transformer |
| Decoder (MLP) | LayerNorm (after each layer) | Same as Encoder |
| Input Embedding | L2 Normalize (normalize to unit sphere) | Brings embeddings from different sources/scales into the same space |
| Codebook | No normalization / L2 normalize at initialization | EMA updates naturally maintain codebook entry scales |
| Downstream Transformer | RMSNorm (Pre-LN position) | Latest practice, fast and stable |

---

## Part 38: The Evolution of Sequence Modeling — From RNN to Transformer

### 38.1 Why Are Sequences Important for Recommendation?

```python
# User behavior is a time series:
# [running shoes, sweatpants, protein powder, yoga mat, ...]
# The sequence contains:
# 1. Interest evolution (shifting from running to yoga)
# 2. Short-term intent (currently looking for sports gear)
# 3. Purchase rhythm (shops once a month)
# 4. Complementary relationships (shoes → socks → insoles)

# Models that ignore sequences (e.g., MF):
# Only see "which items this user clicked" (unordered set)
# Temporal information is lost → recommendations are less precise

# Models that consider sequences (RNN/Transformer):
# Know "what the user has been doing recently" → more accurate intent prediction
```

### 38.2 RNN / LSTM / GRU

```python
# RNN: Process sequences with recurrent structure
# h_t = tanh(W_h * h_{t-1} + W_x * x_t + b)
# Problem: vanishing/exploding gradients → cannot learn long-range dependencies

# LSTM: Introduces gating mechanisms to solve vanishing gradients
class LSTM:
    def step(self, x_t, h_prev, c_prev):
        # Forget gate: decides how much old information to discard
        f_t = sigmoid(W_f @ [h_prev, x_t])
        # Input gate: decides how much new information to accept
        i_t = sigmoid(W_i @ [h_prev, x_t])
        # Candidate memory
        c_tilde = tanh(W_c @ [h_prev, x_t])
        # Update memory cell
        c_t = f_t * c_prev + i_t * c_tilde
        # Output gate: decides how much information to output
        o_t = sigmoid(W_o @ [h_prev, x_t])
        h_t = o_t * tanh(c_t)
        return h_t, c_t

# GRU: Simplified version of LSTM (2 gates vs 3 gates)
# Nearly identical performance, 33% fewer parameters
# GRU is more commonly used in recommendation (GRU4Rec, 2015)

# Limitations of RNN family in recommendation:
# 1. Sequential computation → cannot parallelize → slow training
# 2. Long sequence gradients still problematic (even with LSTM)
# 3. Limited ability to model long-range dependencies
# → Fully replaced by Transformer
```

### 38.3 The Leap from GRU4Rec to SASRec

```python
# GRU4Rec (2015): First to use RNN for sequential recommendation
# Input: Item sequence in a user session
# Model: Multi-layer GRU
# Output: Probability distribution over next item
# Training: BPR loss (pairwise ranking)

# SASRec (2018): Transformer replaces RNN
# Input: Same (user historical item sequence)
# Model: Transformer Decoder (causal mask)
# Output: Same (probability distribution over next item)
# Training: Cross-Entropy loss

# Why SASRec significantly outperforms GRU4Rec:
# 1. Parallel training: All positions in the sequence computed simultaneously (vs RNN step-by-step)
# 2. Direct attention: Any position can directly attend to any historical position
#    (vs RNN where information must pass through all intermediate steps)
# 3. Position awareness: Position embeddings provide temporal information
# 4. Multi-Head: Simultaneously attend to multiple patterns (category/price/brand)

# SASRec is the direct backbone for SID generative recommendation:
# SASRec predicts "next item" → changed to predict "next SID code"
# Almost seamless migration!
```

### 38.4 Practical Deployment of Transformer in Recommendation

Typical configuration for industrial-grade sequential recommendation Transformers:

Model parameters: 4 layers (recommendation sequences are shorter than text, no need for deep models), hidden dimension 128 (smaller than NLP since recommendation signals are denser), 4 attention heads (4–8 heads are sufficient), FFN dimension 512 (4× hidden dimension), max sequence length 200 (user's most recent 200 interactions), dropout 0.1.

Training parameters: batch_size 2048, learning rate 1e-3 (recommendation Transformers typically use larger learning rates than NLP), warmup 1000 steps, total training steps 100000, loss function CrossEntropy with label_smoothing=0.1.

Inference parameters: latency budget 10ms, INT8 quantization, batch_size 64 (continuous batching), KV Cache enabled.

Recommendation Transformers are smaller than NLP ones for four reasons: shorter sequences (200 vs 4096+), so fewer layers are needed; smaller vocabulary (codebook 1024 vs vocabulary 100K), so fewer embedding parameters; stricter latency requirements (10ms vs seconds), so the model must be small; relatively limited data volume (less than internet-scale text), so large models easily overfit.

### 38.5 State Space Models (SSM) — A Challenger to Transformer

```python
# Mamba (2023): Selective State Space Model
# Claims to be more efficient than Transformer for sequence modeling

# Core idea of SSM:
# Model sequences with continuous-time state equations:
# dh/dt = A*h + B*x  (state evolution)
# y = C*h + D*x      (output)
# After discretization becomes recurrent form, but can be trained in parallel

# Advantages of Mamba:
# 1. Inference time: O(1) per step (vs Transformer O(N) per step)
# 2. Memory: Fixed-size state (vs KV Cache growing linearly)
# 3. Long sequences: Theoretically can handle infinitely long sequences

# Mamba's prospects in recommendation:
# Suitable for: Modeling ultra-long user histories (>1000 items)
# Not suitable for: Scenarios requiring "precise attention between arbitrary positions"
# Current status: Research stage, not yet validated at scale in recommendation industry

# Practical advice:
# 2024-2025: Transformer remains the best choice for recommendation sequence modeling
# Keep an eye on Mamba/RWKV progress, but no rush to switch
# SID's Transformer backbone is the most mature approach currently
```

## Part 39: The Cold Start Problem — SID's Core Value Scenario

### 39.1 Three Types of Cold Start

Cold start falls into three types:

| Type | Problem | Traditional Approach | SID Approach |
|------|------|----------|----------|
| New User Cold Start | User just registered, no behavior history at all | Recommend popular items / use registration info / collect interests via questionnaire | Match similar user groups using demographic features, use their behavior sequences for recommendation |
| New Item Cold Start | Item just listed, no click/purchase data at all | Route to exploration traffic / use content features for CF / wait for data to accumulate | Multimodal features go through RQ-VAE to get SID (semantic identifier obtained in seconds) |
| System Cold Start | Brand new system, no users and no interaction data | Manual annotation by operations / rule-based recommendation / third-party data | Pure content understanding (no behavior data needed), at least can do content-based recommendation |

### 39.2 Detailed Analysis of New Item Cold Start

```python
# Why is new item cold start the biggest pain point in recommendation systems?

# E-commerce scenario:
# Tens of thousands of new products listed daily (100K+ during promotions)
# Traditional CF: Needs at least 100-1000 impressions to get effective signals
# During accumulation: New items can only rely on exploration traffic → few impressions → slow data accumulation → vicious cycle
# Consequence: 80% of new items still don't have enough data 7 days after listing → can't be recommended effectively

# Short video scenario:
# Millions of new videos uploaded daily
# Traditional: New videos enter "cold start pool" → limited impressions → decide whether to boost based on real-time CTR
# Problem: High-quality long-tail videos may never surface due to few initial impressions and narrow audience

# How SID solves this:
# After a new item is listed:
# 1. Extract content features (image-text/video → CLIP embedding)  [5 seconds]
# 2. RQ-VAE inference to get SID                                   [< 1 second]
# 3. SID contains semantic information: [sports, running, casual, male]
# 4. Transformer knows "people who like running shoes also like this new item"  [instant]
# Total time: < 10 seconds (vs traditional approach 24-168 hours)
```

### 39.3 Quantifying SID Cold Start Effectiveness

Typical A/B experiment results synthesized from multiple papers and practical experience:

| Metric | Improvement |
|------|----------|
| New Item Day-1 CTR | +30%-80% vs no-SID baseline |
| New Item 7-Day Cumulative Impressions | +50%-200% |
| Average Time to First Click for New Item | Reduced from 48 hours to 2 hours |
| Cold Start Recall@100 | +40%-60% vs content-based |

Key conditions for achieving these results include: high-quality multimodal encoder (CLIP-level), RQ-VAE collision rate below 5%, and downstream Transformer training data containing sufficiently diverse behaviors.

There are also limitations: pure content similarity does not equal user preference (an attractive image doesn't mean a good product); content features have low discriminability for certain categories (e.g., standardized commodities like batteries); the semantic hierarchy of SID may not align with the actual decision factors of users.

### 39.4 Engineering Implementation of Cold Start

```python
# Complete New Item SID Assignment Pipeline:

class NewItemSIDPipeline:
    """Complete flow from new Item listing → obtaining SID"""

    def __init__(self):
        self.encoder = load_frozen_clip()      # Frozen multimodal encoder
        self.rqvae = load_frozen_rqvae()       # Frozen RQ-VAE
        self.sid_table = RedisClient()         # SID storage
        self.event_bus = KafkaProducer()       # Event notification

    def on_new_item(self, item):
        """Triggered by new Item listing event"""
        # Step 1: Feature extraction
        image_emb = self.encoder.encode_image(item.cover_image)
        text_emb = self.encoder.encode_text(item.title + item.description)
        combined_emb = concat_and_normalize(image_emb, text_emb)

        # Step 2: SID assignment
        sid = self.rqvae.encode(combined_emb)  # [k1, k2, k3, k4]

        # Step 3: Collision check
        existing_items = self.sid_table.get_items_by_sid(sid)
        if len(existing_items) > 10:
            # Too many collisions → log alert, but still assign
            alert("High collision for new item", item.id, sid)

        # Step 4: Write to SID Table
        self.sid_table.set(item.id, sid)
        self.sid_table.add_to_sid_index(sid, item.id)

        # Step 5: Notify downstream
        self.event_bus.send("sid_assigned", {
            "item_id": item.id,
            "sid": sid,
            "timestamp": now(),
        })

        return sid

# Trigger method:
# Kafka consumes "item_created" events → calls on_new_item
# Latency: from item creation to SID availability < 30 seconds (including network latency)
```

## Part 40: End-to-End Case Study — Complete Mapping from Paper to Engineering

### 40.1 TIGER (Google, NeurIPS 2023) Architecture

```python
# TIGER: Training and Inference for Generative Retrieval

# Paper contributions:
# 1. Proposed the standard RQ-VAE pipeline for generating Semantic IDs
# 2. Demonstrated that SID + Transformer generative retrieval outperforms traditional ANN
# 3. Proposed a method for hybrid training with item content features + collaborative signals

# Architecture:
TIGER's architecture is divided into three stages:

**Stage 1 - SID Generation.** Input is item content features (Sentence-T5 embeddings of text titles), trained using RQ-VAE (4 layers, K=512, d=64), outputting a 4-digit SID for each item. Training data is the full item corpus; no behavioral data is needed.

**Stage 2 - Retriever Training.** Input is SID sequences of items from user historical interactions, using a Transformer Decoder (12 layers, d=768, 8 heads) to autoregressively predict the next item's SID (layer-by-layer codewords). Training objective is CrossEntropy (predicting each layer's codeword), with training data from user behavior logs.

**Stage 3 - Inference.** Input is the user's real-time behavior sequence, using Constrained Beam Search (only generating valid SIDs), beam_width=200, outputting Top-K candidate items (obtained by looking up SIDs in the table).

### 40.2 OneRec (Kuaishou, 2024) Architecture

```python
# OneRec: Industrial-grade Full-pipeline SID System

# Kuaishou's unique challenges:
# - Short videos: billions of videos in the library, millions of new videos daily
# - Multimodal: video content is the core feature
# - Large scale: hundreds of millions of DAU, millions of QPS

OneRec's architecture covers four aspects:

**SID Scheme:** Uses a multimodal video foundation model to produce unified embeddings, quantized through a proprietary optimized RQ-VAE (supporting large-scale distributed training). SID depth is 4-6 layers (more than TIGER, due to larger item volume), with codebook size 1024-4096. The unique aspect is incorporating collaborative signals (behavior embeddings) into the RQ-VAE input.

**Generative Model:** Uses a large-scale Transformer (10x+ larger than TIGER), with a distinctive unified framework — a single model handles both retrieval and pre-ranking simultaneously, trained via multi-task learning (next-SID prediction + CTR auxiliary task).

**Engineering Optimizations:** Sub-second SID assignment after new video upload (real-time stream processing), Serving uses GPU clusters + TensorRT + shared KV Cache, RQ-VAE retrained bi-weekly, Transformer updated incrementally on a daily basis.

**Results:** Cold-start Recall improved by 50% (vs. traditional ANN), online CTR improved by 2-3% (overall improvement after adding SID retrieval path), coverage improved by 30% (more long-tail videos recommended).

### 40.3 OneSearch (Kuaishou, 2025) — Unified Search + Recommendation

```python
# OneSearch: Unifying Search and Recommendation with SID

# Traditional: Search and recommendation are two completely independent systems
# Search: query → text matching/semantic matching → item
# Recommendation: user behavior → collaborative filtering/deep models → item

# OneSearch's unification:
# SID simultaneously encodes "content semantics" and "behavioral semantics"
# Search: query → Transformer → generate SID → item
# Recommendation: behavior → Transformer → generate SID → item
# Same SID space, same Transformer, different inputs!

```

OneSearch's architecture contains three core innovations:

**SID Upgrade:** Uses RQOPQ (rotation + product quantization + end-to-end), with advantages over RQ-VAE including support for SGD batch training, no K-Means initialization required, and exponential capacity (1024^6 ≈ 10^18).

**Search Unification:** Queries are encoded via BERT into 768-dimensional vectors then mapped to the SID space, the same Transformer accepts mixed query + behavior input, outputting SIDs identical to recommendation, sharing a unified index.

**Results:** Search relevance improved by 5-10% (better semantic understanding), newly listed products are searchable immediately after listing (search cold-start), system simplified from two separate pipelines to one SID system + one Serving layer (reduced engineering cost).

### 40.4 The Gap from Paper to Engineering

There is a significant gap between what papers describe and the challenges faced in actual engineering:

| Dimension | Paper | Engineering Reality | Gap |
|------|------|----------|-----|
| Data Scale | Amazon Beauty (tens of thousands of items, hundreds of thousands of interactions) | Hundreds of millions of items, tens of billions of interactions | Distributed training, sampling strategies, data cleaning |
| Evaluation | Offline Recall@K | Online A/B testing (CTR/CVR/retention) | Good offline ≠ good online, requires extensive online tuning |
| Update Strategy | Train once and done | Continuous updates (new item SID assignment / model incremental training) | SID drift management, version compatibility |
| System Integration | Standalone module | Coordinating with existing ANN retrieval / ranking / re-ranking | Multi-path retrieval fusion, candidate deduplication, latency budget allocation |
| Failure Handling | Not discussed | Must have degradation plans (what if SID service goes down) | Fault-tolerant design, circuit breakers, fallback to traditional ANN |

## Part 41: Complete Guide to Gradient Issues — Vanishing, Exploding, and STE

### 41.1 Vanishing Gradient

```python
# Chain rule: dL/dW1 = dL/dy * dy/dh3 * dh3/dh2 * dh2/dh1 * dh1/dW1
# If each term < 1, the product approaches 0 → vanishing gradient

# Sigmoid causes vanishing gradients:
# sigmoid'(x) = sigmoid(x) * (1 - sigmoid(x))
# Maximum value = 0.25 (when x=0)
# 6-layer network: 0.25^6 = 0.000244 → first layer receives almost no gradient

# Solutions:
# 1. ReLU: max(0, x), derivative = 1 (in positive region), no decay
# 2. Residual connections: h = h + F(h), gradient flows directly through shortcut
# 3. LayerNorm: keeps output scale stable at each layer
# 4. Proper initialization: Xavier/He initialization maintains variance stability

# Why Transformer doesn't suffer from vanishing gradients:
# Residual connections + LayerNorm + ReLU/GELU
# Gradients at every layer can flow back directly through the residual "highway"
```

### 41.2 Exploding Gradient

```python
# If each term in the chain rule is > 1, the product tends to infinity → exploding gradient
# Symptoms: loss suddenly becomes NaN, weights become inf

# Solutions:
# 1. Gradient Clipping:
max_norm = 1.0
torch.nn.utils.clip_grad_norm_(model.parameters(), max_norm)
# If the total gradient norm > max_norm, scale down all gradients proportionally

# 2. Proper initialization + LayerNorm (prevention)
# 3. Smaller learning rate (treats symptoms, not the root cause)
# 4. Loss Scaling in mixed precision (prevents FP16 underflow)

# Gradient issues in RQ-VAE training:
# STE itself doesn't cause exploding gradients (it just copies gradients)
# But if codebook entries are updated improperly → codebook positions jump suddenly → loss spikes → gradient explosion
# Solution: EMA codebook updates (smooth changes) + gradient clipping
```

### 41.3 Straight-Through Estimator (STE) Deep Dive

```python
# STE is the core trick for VQ-VAE / RQ-VAE training

# Problem: argmin is non-differentiable
# z_q = codebook[argmin(distances)]  # gradient of argmin is 0

# What STE does:
# Forward: use z_q (the discrete quantized result)
# Backward: pretend quantization doesn't exist, pass gradients directly from z_q to z_e
# z_q_st = z_e + (z_q - z_e).detach()
# Forward: z_q_st = z_q (since the detached part doesn't participate in the computation graph)
# Backward: dL/dz_e = dL/dz_q_st (gradients pass straight through)

# Gradient bias of STE:
# True gradient: dL/dz_e = dL/dz_q * dz_q/dz_e (= 0, because of argmin)
# STE gradient: dL/dz_e ≈ dL/dz_q (assumes dz_q/dz_e ≈ 1)
# Bias: when z_e is far from z_q, this approximation is poor
# → This is why commitment loss is needed: push z_e closer to z_q to reduce the bias

# Better alternatives:
# 1. Gumbel-Softmax: differentiable approximation, approaches argmax as temperature → 0
# 2. Relaxed Categorical: continuous relaxation version
# 3. FSQ: direct rounding, avoids argmin (but rounding also uses STE)
# In practice, STE + commitment loss remains the most stable approach
```

### 41.4 Gradient Accumulation

```python
# When GPU memory isn't enough for a large batch:
# Don't actually store the large batch — accumulate gradients from multiple small batches

accumulation_steps = 4  # effective batch = micro_batch * 4
optimizer.zero_grad()
for i, batch in enumerate(dataloader):
    loss = model(batch) / accumulation_steps  # divide by number of steps
    loss.backward()  # gradients accumulate (no zero_grad)
    if (i + 1) % accumulation_steps == 0:
        optimizer.step()
        optimizer.zero_grad()

# Significance in RQ-VAE:
# Sinkhorn requires large batches (2048+) to be effective
# If GPU can only fit batch=512:
# Use gradient accumulation × 4 = effective batch=2048
# But note: Sinkhorn's balancing only takes effect within a single micro-batch
# → If global balancing is needed, you may need queue-based Sinkhorn
```

---

## Part 42: Causal Inference in Recommendation Systems

### 42.1 Why Do Recommendations Need Causal Inference?

```python
# Recommendation system data is not from randomized experiments; it has severe selection bias:
# - Users only see items that were recommended (no "randomly recommended" data)
# - Popular items get recommended more → positive feedback loop
# - Model trains on "previously recommended" data → cannot accurately predict for unexposed items

# Examples of causal questions:
# "Is this item's CTR high because the item itself is good, or because it was placed in a good position?"
# "Does recommending sneakers to this user yield high CTR because the user likes them, or because too many sneakers were already recommended?"

# Correlation ≠ Causation:
# "People who buy beer also buy diapers" (classic correlation)
# Doesn't mean "recommending diapers to beer buyers" is correct
# (The real reason is the same demographic: young fathers)
```

### 42.2 Position Bias

```python
# Phenomenon: items shown in position 1 have far higher CTR than position 10
# Reason: users tend to click items near the top (regardless of quality)
# If not addressed: the model learns "higher position = better item"

# Solution 1: IPW (Inverse Propensity Weighting)
# Weight each sample by the inverse of its "position propensity"
# P(click | item, position=1) = P(click | item) * P(examine | position=1)
# position_propensity = estimated position click propensity
# Weighting: weight = 1 / position_propensity
# Samples at position 1 get lower weight < samples at position 10 → debiasing

# Solution 2: Position Feature + Dropout
# Training: position as an input feature (model learns to "account for position effects")
# Inference: position feature set to 0 or the mean value (removing position influence)
# Only the estimate of the item's intrinsic quality remains

# Relationship with SID:
# SID's offline training (RQ-VAE) doesn't involve position bias (pure content quantization)
# But the downstream Transformer's training data has position bias
# If not addressed → model learns to "generate SIDs of items that were in good positions"
# Rather than "generate SIDs of items the user truly likes"
```

### 42.3 Impression Bias and Counterfactual Reasoning

```python
# Counterfactual: "If a different item had been recommended at that time, would the user have clicked?"
# We can never directly observe the counterfactual → need to estimate it

# IPS (Inverse Propensity Score):
# weight(x) = 1 / P(item x was exposed)
# Low-impression items get higher weight → corrects the bias of "only high-impression items have abundant data"

# Doubly Robust Estimator:
# Combines IPS and direct estimation; as long as one is correct, the result is correct
# DR = direct_model_estimate + IPS_correction
# More stable, more commonly used in industry

# Application in SID training:
# RQ-VAE stage: not affected (pure content features, no behavioral bias)
# Transformer stage: training data = actual user behavior = has impression bias
# Correction: add IPS weights to training samples
# Behavioral sequences of low-impression items get higher weight
# → Model won't just learn "users click whatever is recommended"
```

---

## Part 43: Practical Feature Engineering in Industry

### 43.1 Feature Discretization (Bucketization)

```python
# Continuous features need to be discretized for embedding:

# Method 1: Equal-width binning
# price: [0-50, 50-100, 100-200, 200-500, 500+]
# Problem: if data is skewed, most samples concentrate in one or two bins

# Method 2: Equal-frequency binning (recommended)
# Each bin contains the same number of samples
# Use quantiles as boundaries: 10%, 20%, ..., 90%
# Advantage: every bin has enough samples to train its embedding

# Method 3: Tree-based binning
# Use GBDT to split on the target variable → split points become bin boundaries
# Advantage: bin boundaries are "meaningful for prediction"
# Classic approach from Facebook in 2014

# Analogy in the SID system:
# RQ-VAE is essentially "binning" continuous embeddings:
# 128-dimensional continuous vector → 4 discrete codewords
# Each codeword is a "bin" (quantization interval)
# K-Means is doing "equal-frequency" binning in high-dimensional space
```

### 43.2 Feature Crossing in Industrial Practice

```python
# Evolution of automatic feature crossing:

# Phase 1 (2010-2015): Manual crossing
# Data scientists manually created cross features based on business understanding
# "user_city × item_category × time_slot" → a new ID feature
# Cost: requires extensive domain knowledge + experimentation

# Phase 2 (2015-2020): Models automatically learn crossings
# FM/DeepFM/DCN: automatically learn useful crossings
# No need for manual specification; the model discovers useful combinations on its own
# But still need to decide: which features to input, how to embed them

# Phase 3 (2020-2025): Full automation
# AutoML/NAS searches for the best feature crossing approach
# Or: Transformer directly processes all feature tokens (HSTU)
# No need for predefined crossing rules

# The "dirty work" in practice:
practical_feature_engineering = {
    "Missing value handling": "Default value / special embedding token / mean imputation",
    "Low-frequency filtering": "IDs appearing < 5 times → unified as 'unknown'",
    "Frequency encoding": "Use item_id's frequency information as an additional feature",
    "Time decay": "Weight historical behaviors by time decay (recent ones get higher weight)",
    "Sequence truncation": "Keep the most recent N behaviors (N=50-200)",
}
```

### 43.3 Embedding Tricks

Embedding engineering tricks in real-world systems:

| Scenario | Problem | Solution |
|------|------|------|
| Multi-value features | A user may have multiple interest tags (sports, food, travel) | Average the embeddings / attention-weighted pooling |
| Variable-length sequences | Different users have different behavior sequence lengths | Pad to fixed length + attention mask |
| Hot-cold separation | Popular IDs have well-trained parameters; long-tail IDs have noisy parameters | Large embedding for popular IDs (128d), small embedding for long-tail IDs (32d) |
| Hash Embedding | ID space too large to fit in memory | Multiple hash mappings to multiple small tables, results summed or concatenated |
| Pre-trained initialization | Training a new embedding table from scratch is slow | Initialize with SID's codebook embeddings for faster cold start |

---

## Part 44: From Attention to Cross-Attention — The Math of Multi-Modal Fusion

### 44.1 Self-Attention in Matrix Form

```python
# Self-Attention: tokens within a sequence attend to each other

# Input: X ∈ R^{N×d} (N tokens, each d-dimensional)
# Projections: Q = X @ W_Q,  K = X @ W_K,  V = X @ W_V
# Attention: Attention(Q, K, V) = softmax(Q @ K^T / sqrt(d_k)) @ V

# Matrix decomposition perspective:
# Q @ K^T ∈ R^{N×N}: "which token is related to which token"
# softmax: normalizes into a probability distribution
# @ V: "aggregate each token's value weighted by relevance"

# For each output position i:
# output_i = sum_j(attention_weight_{i,j} * V_j)
# = weighted sum of all tokens' Values
# weights = similarity between current token's Q and each token's K

# Time complexity: O(N^2 * d) — N×N attention matrix
# Space complexity: O(N^2) — storing the full attention matrix (Flash Attention can optimize to O(N))
```

### 44.2 Cross-Attention

```python
# Cross-Attention: one sequence "queries" another sequence

# Input:
# Source: S ∈ R^{M×d} (the sequence being queried, e.g., image patches)
# Target: T ∈ R^{N×d} (the sequence issuing queries, e.g., text tokens)

# Projections:
# Q = T @ W_Q   (Query comes from Target)
# K = S @ W_K   (Key comes from Source)
# V = S @ W_V   (Value comes from Source)

# Attention:
# Attention(Q, K, V) = softmax(Q @ K^T / sqrt(d_k)) @ V
# Output ∈ R^{N×d}: each Target token has aggregated information from the Source

# Intuition:
# "I (Target/Text) want to find information from you (Source/Image)"
# Each text token selectively attends to relevant regions in the image

# Applications:
# 1. Multimodal: text queries relevant regions of an image
# 2. Sequence-to-sequence: Decoder queries Encoder output (machine translation)
# 3. Recommendations: candidate item queries user history sequence (DIN's Attention)

# In SID generation:
# At each step when the Transformer Decoder predicts a codeword
# it can cross-attend to user features / context features
# achieving "conditional generation based on user profile"
```

### 44.3 Intuition Behind Multi-Head Attention

```python
# Why multiple heads?

# Single head: can only learn one "attention pattern"
# For example: only attending to "category relevance"

# Multi-head: learns multiple "attention patterns" simultaneously
# Head 1: attends to "category relevance" (running shoes attend to running pants)
# Head 2: attends to "price range relevance" (¥100 attends to ¥80-150 items)
# Head 3: attends to "temporal proximity" (recently clicked items get higher weight)
# Head 4: attends to "brand relevance" (Nike attends to Nike)

# Implementation:
# Not actually running Attention H times separately
# Instead, split the d-dim into H subspaces of d_k dimensions (d_k = d/H)
# Each subspace runs Attention independently → results concat → linear projection

class MultiHeadAttention:
    def forward(self, Q, K, V, num_heads=8):
        d_k = Q.shape[-1] // num_heads
        # Split
        Q = Q.reshape(batch, seq, num_heads, d_k).transpose(1,2)
        K = K.reshape(batch, seq, num_heads, d_k).transpose(1,2)
        V = V.reshape(batch, seq, num_heads, d_k).transpose(1,2)
        # Independent Attention per head
        attn = softmax(Q @ K.T / sqrt(d_k)) @ V  # [batch, heads, seq, d_k]
        # Merge
        output = attn.transpose(1,2).reshape(batch, seq, d)
        return self.out_proj(output)  # Final linear projection
```

### 44.4 Attention Applied in RQ-VAE

```python
# RQ-VAE's Encoder/Decoder is typically an MLP
# But it can be enhanced with Attention:

# 1. Set Transformer Encoder (processing multiple features):
# Input: multimodal features [image_emb, text_emb, category_emb, ...]
# Instead of simple concat, use Self-Attention to let features "look at each other"
# Advantage: the importance among features can be dynamically adjusted

# 2. Cross-Attention Decoder (conditional decoding):
# Codeword sequence [z_q1, z_q2, z_q3] cross-attends to original features
# Each decoding layer references different aspects of the original input
# Advantage: more precise decoding, higher reconstruction quality

# 3. Attention-based Quantization (replacing argmin):
# Instead of finding the nearest codeword by Euclidean distance
# use Attention to let the encoder output "select" codewords
# Q = encoder_output, K = V = codebook
# attention_weights = softmax(Q @ K.T)  # soft selection
# z_q = attention_weights @ V  # weighted combination (during training)
# z_q = V[argmax(attention_weights)]  # hard selection (during inference)
# Advantage: more flexible than Euclidean distance, but adds parameters
```
