## Part 45: Hands-On — A Complete Evaluation Framework for SID Systems

### 45.1 Evaluation System Design

The evaluation of an SID system is divided into four layers:

**Layer 1 - Quantization Quality (RQ-VAE itself).** Evaluated after RQ-VAE training is complete and before going online. Core metrics include: reconstruction_MSE (reconstruction error, lower is better), codebook_utilization (codebook utilization rate, 70–90% is healthy), collision_rate (collision rate, below 5% is acceptable), residual_decay (residual decay rate across layers), and entropy_ratio (ratio of the entropy of codeword usage distribution to maximum entropy).

**Layer 2 - Semantic Quality (SID space).** Evaluated after SID assignment is complete. Core metrics include: intra_category_prefix_share (prefix sharing rate among items in the same category), cross_category_separation (discrimination of SID prefixes across different categories), and nearest_neighbor_consistency (overlap between SID nearest neighbors and embedding nearest neighbors).

**Layer 3 - Offline Recommendation Performance.** Evaluated after Transformer training is complete and before A/B experiments. Core metrics include: Recall@K (proportion of positive samples hit among K generated SIDs), NDCG@K (position-aware hit quality), cold_start_Recall (retrieval rate for new items only), and diversity (category coverage of the generated candidate set).

**Layer 4 - Online Business Performance.** Evaluated during A/B experiments. Core metrics include: overall_CTR_lift (overall CTR improvement after adding SID retrieval), cold_start_CTR (CTR of new items vs. baseline), coverage (number of unique items recommended, measuring diversity), and user_retention_7d (7-day retention rate, measuring long-term impact).

### 45.2 Ablation Study Design

Ablation studies remove components one at a time to observe the impact on performance. The following five experiments are recommended:

**Experiment 1: Value of Sinkhorn.** Control group uses RQ-VAE with Sinkhorn; experimental group removes Sinkhorn (pure argmin assignment). Expected result: without Sinkhorn, utilization drops significantly and collision rate increases.

**Experiment 2: Impact of Residual Depth.** Control group uses 4-layer RQ; experimental group A uses 2-layer RQ; experimental group B uses 6-layer RQ. Expected result: 2 layers lead to high collision rate (insufficient capacity); 6 layers yield marginal improvement but slower inference.

**Experiment 3: Contribution of Input Features.** Control group uses image-text + behavioral statistics; experimental group A uses image-text only (no behavior); experimental group B uses behavior only (no image-text). Expected result: image-text contributes semantic information, behavior contributes collaborative information; the two are complementary.

**Experiment 4: Impact of Beam Width.** beam=10 is fast but may miss good candidates; beam=50 is the sweet spot; beam=200 yields richer candidates but slower inference; beam=500 shows diminishing marginal returns.

**Experiment 5: Sweet Spot of Codeword Dimension.** d=16 gives high utilization but high collision rate; d=32 may be the sweet spot; d=64 is the standard configuration; d=128 may see declining utilization.

### 45.3 Visualization Analysis

Visualization helps build intuitive understanding of SID space quality:

| Visualization Type | Method | Healthy Expectation |
|-----------|------|----------|
| t-SNE / UMAP Dimensionality Reduction | Reduce item embeddings to 2D, color by category; additionally annotate SID prefixes | Same-category items cluster together, different categories separate; prefixes should correspond to categories |
| Codeword Usage Frequency Heatmap | X-axis is codeword index, Y-axis is RQ layer, color is usage frequency | Colors should be as uniform as possible; there should be no large blank areas (dead codewords) |
| SID Prefix Tree Visualization | Tree diagram, one level per layer, leaf nodes are items | Items under the same branch should be semantically similar (e.g., all items under prefix [3,7] are sports category) |
| Beam Search Path Visualization | Show beam branching and pruning at each layer | Early layers quickly converge to a few directions; later layers do refinement |
| Reconstruction Error Distribution | Histogram, grouped by category | Error should be similar across all categories; if a particular category is notably high, it means that category's items have too much diversity or insufficient features |

### 45.4 Online-Offline Consistency Checks

The most common bug in SID systems is good offline performance but poor online performance. Three consistency checks are needed:

| Check Item | Method | Threshold | Common Causes |
|--------|------|------|----------|
| Quantization Operator Consistency | Sample 1000 items, compare SIDs from offline PyTorch argmin vs. online TensorRT | diff rate below 0.01% | FP16 precision differences / library version differences / operator implementation differences |
| Feature Consistency | Compare offline and online item embeddings (L2 distance) | average L2 diff below 0.001 | Feature pipeline version mismatch / different normalization methods |
| Beam Search Consistency | Compare top-K candidate sets from offline Python vs. online C++ given the same input | top-10 overlap rate above 95% | Different tie-breaking rules in floating-point sorting |

## Part 46: Future Outlook — What's Next for SID

### 46.1 Variable-Length SID

```python
# Current: all items have SIDs of the same length (e.g., 4 layers)
# Problem: popular items don't need fine-grained SIDs (2 layers suffice)
#          long-tail items need more fine-grained SIDs (6 layers to distinguish)

# Variable-Length SID approach:
# Popular items: SID = [k1, k2]  (2 layers, short)
# Mid-range items: SID = [k1, k2, k3, k4]  (4 layers, medium)
# Long-tail items: SID = [k1, k2, k3, k4, k5, k6]  (6 layers, long)

# Benefits:
# - More efficient utilization of codebook space
# - Faster inference for popular items (fewer tokens to generate)
# - Lower collision rate for long-tail items

# Challenges:
# - How does the Transformer handle variable-length targets?
# - How to decide how many layers each item should get?
# - How to build a unified index for SIDs of different lengths?

# Current research status: multiple papers exploring this in 2024–2025, not yet industrialized at scale
```

### 46.2 Distribution-Level SID

```python
# Current: each item has one deterministic SID
# New idea: each item has a SID distribution (allowing multiple SIDs)

# Motivation:
# An item may belong to multiple semantic categories:
# "Sports Bluetooth earbuds" → both "sports accessories" and "electronics"
# Assigning it one deterministic SID → must choose between the two categories
# Assigning it a SID distribution → it can be retrieved from both categories

# Implementation:
# Instead of argmin (one codeword), use top-k or soft assignment
# Each item has top-3 SIDs (ranked by probability)
# item_A: SID_1 (prob=0.6), SID_2 (prob=0.3), SID_3 (prob=0.1)

# During generation:
# The Transformer can reach the same item via multiple paths
# This improves recall, but increases indexing and storage costs
```

### 46.3 Unified Multimodal Tokenization

```python
# Ultimate vision: one Token system unifying all modalities

# Text: BPE tokens (already exists)
# Image: VQ-VAE tokens (already exists)
# Audio: SoundStream RVQ tokens (already exists)
# Item: SID tokens (actively developing)
# User: User tokens? (being explored)
# Behavior: Action tokens? (being explored)

# Once fully unified:
# A single large Transformer processes:
# [user_token, action_token, item_SID, text_token, image_token, ...]
# All information expressed in the same "language"

# This is the "GPT moment for recommender systems":
# When all modalities are tokenized,
# recommendation becomes a "continuation problem over multi-modal token sequences"
# fundamentally no different from LLM text generation
```

## Part 47: Applications of Reinforcement Learning in Recommender Systems

### 47.1 Why Does Recommendation Need RL?

```python
# Traditional supervised learning recommendation:
# - Optimizes: immediate reward of a single click/conversion
# - Ignores: the impact of recommendations on user long-term behavior
# - Problem: may recommend "clickbait" for short-term clicks, but hurt long-term retention

# RL framework:
# - State: user's current state (historical behavior sequence, profile)
# - Action: selecting items to recommend (or their arrangement)
# - Reward: click / watch duration / purchase / retention etc.
# - Transition: user state changes as a result of the recommendation
# - Objective: maximize cumulative discounted reward (not just immediate CTR)

# Classic case:
# YouTube 2019: used RL to optimize watch time (not single-click CTR)
# → Found that recommending more long videos + series content increased total watch time by 5%
# → But pure CTR optimization would recommend more short videos / clickbait

# Combining with SID:
# RL decides "what direction to recommend" (exploration/exploitation tradeoff)
# SID generative retrieval is responsible for "generating specific candidates in that direction"
# The two are complementary, not substitutes
```

### 47.2 Contextual Bandits

```python
# The simplest RL: no state transitions, independent decisions at each step

# Multi-Armed Bandit:
# K arms (items), each with an unknown reward distribution
# Objective: find the optimal arm after a limited number of trials
# Core tradeoff: exploration (try new ones) vs exploitation (pick the known best)

# Epsilon-Greedy:
# With probability epsilon explore randomly, with probability 1-epsilon pick the best
epsilon = 0.1  # 10% exploration

# UCB (Upper Confidence Bound):
# Select the item with the largest upper confidence bound
# UCB(item) = mean_reward(item) + c * sqrt(log(N) / n_item)
# Items selected fewer times → higher uncertainty → larger UCB → more likely to be selected

# Thompson Sampling:
# Maintain a Beta distribution of rewards for each item
# Each time, sample from each distribution and select the one with the largest sample value
# Automatically balances exploration/exploitation, most commonly used in industry
# prior = Beta(alpha=1, beta=1)  # uniform prior
# Each success: alpha += 1
# Each failure: beta += 1
# Sample: theta = Beta(alpha, beta).sample()

# Exploration in SID systems:
# Problem: SIDs generated by Beam Search are always "high confidence"
# → Will over-concentrate on popular items
# Solution: Add UCB bonus to beam scores
# score(beam) = log_prob + c * exploration_bonus
# New items / low-impression items get extra score
```

### 47.3 Offline RL (Batch RL)

```python
# Problem with online RL: exploration may recommend very bad items → hurts user experience
# Offline RL: learn a policy from historical data, no online interaction needed

# Mainstream methods:
# 1. BCQ (Batch-Constrained Q-learning):
#    Only select actions that appeared in the history (conservative)
# 2. CQL (Conservative Q-Learning):
#    Add penalties to Q-values of unobserved actions
# 3. IQL (Implicit Q-Learning):
#    Don't explicitly learn Q; implicitly extract optimal behavior from data

# Typical application in recommendation:
# Objective: maximize 7-day cumulative GMV (not single click)
# State: user's behavior in the past 30 days + profile
# Action: recommendation slate (a page of item list)
# Reward: user's total spending within 7 days
# Discount factor gamma = 0.95 (emphasizes short-term but also considers long-term)

# Challenges:
# 1. Action space is too large (permutations of millions of items)
# 2. Delayed reward (content recommended today, effect known only after 7 days)
# 3. Distribution shift (historical policy vs current policy discrepancy)
```

### 47.4 Insights from RLHF for Recommendation

```python
# RLHF (Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback):
# ChatGPT uses RLHF for alignment with human preferences

# Three stages:
# 1. Pretrain: language model learns to generate (corresponds to: SID generator learns to continue sequences)
# 2. Reward Model: train a model that judges "good vs bad"
# 3. PPO optimization: use RL to adjust the generator so outputs better match human preferences

# RLHF approach in recommendation:
# 1. Pretrain: Transformer learns to predict next item (standard SASRec)
# 2. Reward Model: train a model to predict user satisfaction
#    - Not just CTR, also includes "completion rate" / "not interested" feedback
#    - Can use human annotation to label "which recommendation list is better"
# 3. PPO optimization: adjust the SID generator so generated candidates are more "good"
#    - "good" = high reward model score
#    - Constraint: KL penalty to prevent drifting too far from the pretrained model

# DPO (Direct Preference Optimization) in recommendation:
# No explicit reward model needed
# Learn directly from pair-wise preference data
# (A is better than B) → adjust model so P(A) > P(B)
# Simpler, but requires large amounts of preference pair data
```

---

## Part 48: Distributed Training in Practice — From Single GPU to Multi-Machine

### 48.1 Why Does RQ-VAE Need Distributed Training?

```python
# Computational bottlenecks of RQ-VAE:
# 1. Large batch requirement: Sinkhorn needs 2048+ batch to be effective
# 2. Codebook search: distance computation against K=1024 codewords at each layer
# 3. Multi-modal encoder: processing images/text requires massive compute
# 4. Item scale: tens of millions to billions of items require iterating over all data

# Single GPU limitations:
# A100 80GB → batch size ≈ 512 (with 128d embeddings)
# Need 4-8 GPUs to reach batch=2048

# Training time estimate:
# 100 million items, batch=2048, 3 epochs
# = 1e8 * 3 / 2048 = ~146K steps
# Each step ~0.5s (A100) → ~20 hours (single machine 8 GPUs)
# If adding multi-modal encoder: × 3-5 → 60-100 hours
```

### 48.2 Data Parallel

```python
# Most common: each GPU holds the full model, different GPUs process different data

# DDP (Distributed Data Parallel):
import torch.distributed as dist
from torch.nn.parallel import DistributedDataParallel as DDP

dist.init_process_group("nccl")
model = DDP(model.cuda(), device_ids=[local_rank])
# Each GPU does forward pass independently; during backward, all-reduce gradients to average

# Special issues with RQ-VAE DDP:
# Codebook is a shared parameter → needs cross-GPU synchronization:
# 1. During EMA update: first all-gather assignment counts from each GPU
#    then all-reduce to compute global average → update codebook
# 2. Sinkhorn assignment: do it on the global batch? Or independently on each GPU?
#    - Global: high communication overhead, but better balancing
#    - Local: each GPU balances independently, but may be globally uneven
#    - Practical compromise: local Sinkhorn per GPU + periodic global correction

# Combined with gradient accumulation:
accumulation = 4
# Effective batch = per_gpu_batch * num_gpus * accumulation
# = 512 * 4 * 4 = 8192 (very large, Sinkhorn is very effective)
```

### 48.3 Model Parallel and Mixed Precision

```python
# When the encoder is too large (e.g., using CLIP ViT-L) to fit on a single GPU:
# Option 1: Split encoder and RQ-VAE across different GPUs
# Option 2: Gradient Checkpointing (trade time for memory)
# Option 3: DeepSpeed ZeRO Stage 2/3 (shard parameters/gradients/optimizer states)

# Mixed precision training (AMP):
from torch.cuda.amp import autocast, GradScaler

scaler = GradScaler()
with autocast(dtype=torch.float16):
    # Encoder forward: FP16 (faster computation, saves memory)
    embeddings = encoder(inputs)
    # Distance computation: FP16 may have precision issues!
    # Key: argmin is sensitive to precision → use FP32
    with autocast(enabled=False):
        distances = torch.cdist(embeddings.float(), codebook.float())
        indices = distances.argmin(dim=-1)
    # Reconstruction: FP16
    reconstructed = decoder(quantized)
    loss = compute_loss(inputs, reconstructed)

scaler.scale(loss).backward()
scaler.step(optimizer)
scaler.update()

# NOTE: Codebook distance computation MUST be FP32
# Otherwise quantization results may be inconsistent (a common cause of offline/online diff)
```

### 48.4 Large-Scale RQ-VAE Training Pipeline

The industrial-grade training workflow consists of five steps:

**Step 1 – Data Preparation.** The input is item features (pre-computed embeddings), stored as TFRecord / Parquet / custom binary format. File-level + sample-level double shuffle is applied, and preprocessing includes normalization, truncation, and padding.

**Step 2 – Pre-train Encoder (Optional).** Methods include contrastive learning, self-supervised learning, or using a pre-trained large model. After pre-training, freeze the parameters and output fixed-dimension item embeddings.

**Step 3 – RQ-VAE Training.** Input comes from the embeddings of Step 2 (or raw features), using 4–8 A100 GPUs, training for 12–48 hours. Key hyperparameters include codebook size K, number of layers L, dimension d, and Sinkhorn temperature. Early stopping when validation reconstruction error stops decreasing.

**Step 4 – SID Assignment.** All items are passed through the encoder plus RQ-VAE, outputting an item_id → SID mapping table. Verify collision rate, utilization rate, and category consistency.

**Step 5 – Downstream Transformer Training.** Input is user behavior sequences (already converted to SIDs), with the training objective of predicting the next SID. This step is independent of RQ-VAE—RQ-VAE is frozen; only the Transformer is trained.

---

## Part 49: Model Compression and Deployment Optimization

### 49.1 Knowledge Distillation

```python
# Scenario: Large model (Teacher) has good performance but slow inference
#           Small model (Student) has fast inference but worse performance
# Distillation: Let the Student learn the Teacher's "knowledge", not the original labels

# Standard Distillation (Hinton 2015):
# Teacher outputs soft labels (softmax with temperature T)
# Student learns both soft labels + hard labels
# Loss = alpha * KL(teacher_soft, student_soft) + (1-alpha) * CE(hard_label, student)

# Role of temperature:
# T=1: Normal softmax (close to one-hot)
# T=5: Smoothed distribution (exposes the Teacher's confidence details across classes)
# T→∞: Uniform distribution (no information)
# Recommended T=3-10

# Distillation applications in the SID system:
```

There are two typical distillation scenarios in a SID system:

**Scenario 1 – Transformer Compression.** The Teacher is a 12-layer 768d Transformer (trained offline), and the Student is a 4-layer 256d Transformer (for online inference). The distillation method is to have the Student learn the Teacher's probability distribution over codewords at each layer. Benefit: inference latency reduced by 3×, with less than 2% performance loss.

**Scenario 2 – Encoder Distillation.** The Teacher is CLIP ViT-L (500M params), and the Student is a lightweight MLP (10M params). The distillation method is to have the Student's output approximate the Teacher's embedding. Benefit: item embedding computation is 100× faster.

### 49.2 Quantized Inference (INT8/INT4)

```python
# Training uses FP32/FP16, inference uses INT8 → 4x throughput improvement

# Post-Training Quantization (PTQ):
# Directly quantize weights after training
# FP32 → INT8: Each value is mapped to [-128, 127]
# scale = max(abs(weight)) / 127
# int_weight = round(weight / scale)
# During inference: output = (int_weight * scale) @ (int_input * input_scale)

# Special considerations for RQ-VAE quantized inference:
quantization_notes = {
    "codebook": "Keep FP32 (only K*d parameters, very small)",
    "distance computation": "Can be accelerated with INT8 (matrix multiplication)",
    "argmin": "Must be exact (INT8 distances can still find the correct nearest neighbor)",
    "Transformer": "Standard INT8 quantization (large savings)",
    "Beam Search": "Keep log_prob at FP16 (sorting requires precision)",
}

# TensorRT deployment:
# 1. Export ONNX: torch → ONNX
# 2. TensorRT optimization: operator fusion + INT8 calibration
# 3. Triton Inference Server: dynamic batching + multi-stream
# Expected: single-GPU QPS from 500 → 2000+ (with INT8)
```

### 49.3 Service Architecture

The typical architecture for SID online serving consists of four components:

| Component | Function | Deployment | QPS | Latency |
|------|------|------|-----|------|
| Embedding Service | Receives item features, returns SID | GPU (with encoder) or CPU (pre-computed embeddings) | ~10K (batch update, not real-time) | Non-critical path |
| Generation Service (Core) | Receives user sequence, returns top-K candidate SIDs | GPU (Transformer + Beam Search), INT8 + KV Cache + Dynamic Batching | ~5K-10K per GPU | Less than 50ms (P99) |
| SID Reverse Lookup Service | Converts SID to item_id list | Redis / in-memory index (simple hash lookup) | ~100K | Less than 1ms |
| Cache Layer | Caches candidate lists for hot users | TTL 30s-5min (based on user activity level) | — | Expected hit rate 40-60% |

---

## Part 50: MLOps — Full Model Lifecycle Management

### 50.1 Feature Pipeline

Item feature production and updates follow a layered architecture:

| Layer | Source | Latency | Examples | Storage |
|------|------|------|------|------|
| Real-time Features | Kafka user behavior stream | Seconds | Item CTR in the last 1 hour, real-time interaction count | Redis |
| Near-real-time Features | Flink/Spark Streaming | Minutes | Last 24-hour statistics, collaborative filtering scores | Redis + Hive |
| Offline Features | Spark batch processing | Hours/Days | Item profiles, multimodal embeddings, quality scores | Hive + HDFS |

SID systems typically use offline / near-real-time features as input to RQ-VAE (excluding real-time features), because SIDs are recomputed periodically and do not require second-level updates—the typical update frequency is a full recomputation daily or weekly.

### 50.2 Model Training Automation

Key components of an industrial ML pipeline:

**Data Quality Monitoring**: Before training, check for feature distribution drift, missing rates, and outliers. If the KL divergence of a feature's distribution exceeds a threshold, pause training and trigger an alert—a model trained on dirty data is even more dangerous once deployed online.

**Hyperparameter Search**: Use Bayesian Optimization or Grid Search to find the optimal lr, batch_size, K (codebook size), L (number of layers), d (dimension), sinkhorn_temperature, etc. Optuna / Ray Tune are commonly used platforms.

**Model Validation Gate**: After training, automatically evaluate offline metrics (reconstruction error / collision rate / Recall@K). The model must beat the baseline to be registered for release. After passing, it enters the A/B experiment queue awaiting deployment.

**Automated Workflow**: Training complete → automatic evaluation → if passed, register model version → wait for A/B experiment scheduling → canary rollout → full rollout. The entire process requires no manual intervention (unless an alert is triggered).

**A/B Experiments**: User-level traffic splitting (not request-level), observation period of 7–14 days, with core metrics being CTR / retention / GMV / coverage.

### 50.3 Model Monitoring and Alerting

Continuous multi-level monitoring is required after going live:

**Real-time System Metrics**: Inference latency (P50/P95/P99, alert if exceeding 50ms), QPS (alert on sudden drops/spikes in request volume), error rate (alert if 5xx/timeout rate exceeds 0.1%), GPU utilization (below 30% is wasteful, above 90% requires scaling up).

**Model Performance Metrics**: Relative change in online CTR compared to baseline, candidate diversity (unique item coverage), SID coverage (proportion of valid items among generated SIDs), Beam success rate (proportion of Beam Searches that return at least one valid candidate).

**Data Quality Metrics**: Whether feature updates arrive on time, whether new items are promptly assigned SIDs, periodic spot-checks of offline/online SID consistency.

**Alert Response Levels**:

| Level | Condition | Action |
|------|------|------|
| P0 (Immediate) | Inference error rate exceeds 1% | Automatic rollback |
| P1 (30 minutes) | Performance metrics drop by more than 5% | Manual confirmation |
| P2 (Same day) | Coverage/diversity decline | Investigate root cause |

### 50.4 Model Update Strategy

Update cadence for each component of the SID system:

**RQ-VAE (Codebook)**: Full retraining weekly or biweekly. Triggered when new items accumulate beyond 10% or performance degradation is observed. After a codebook update, the SID mapping table must be updated in sync, and downstream models need to be aware of this change. Old SIDs are gradually deprecated, and new SIDs are gradually activated.

**Transformer (Generator)**: Daily incremental training (fine-tune a few epochs on the previous day's data), weekly full training (train from scratch to prevent forgetting and accumulated errors).

**SID Mapping Table**: Updated in sync with RQ-VAE. Must be updated atomically—the new table is fully written first, then switched in all at once; reading and writing simultaneously is not allowed. The previous two versions are retained so rollback is always possible.

**Canary Release Strategy**: Start at 1% → 5% → 20% → 100%, observing for 24–48 hours at each stage. Roll back immediately if core metrics drop by more than 3%.

---

## Part 51: Privacy Protection and Federated Learning

### 51.1 Privacy Challenges in Recommendation Systems

Recommendation systems are built on user behavioral data—more data means better recommendations, but also greater privacy risk. Key challenges include:

- **Sensitivity of Data Collection**: Browsing / clicks / purchases / dwell time form a very fine-grained behavioral profile
- **Model Memorization**: Large models may memorize specific users' behavior patterns (membership inference attack)
- **Embedding Leakage**: User embeddings may be reverse-engineered to recover original behavior sequences
- **Cross-platform Tracking**: Behavior on the same item across different platforms can be correlated to form a full-spectrum profile

Regulatory constraints are tightening: GDPR (Europe) requires users to have the right to delete data and understand recommendation reasons; CCPA (California) allows users to opt-out of data collection; the Personal Information Protection Law (China) requires no excessive collection and explicit consent.

### 51.2 Differential Privacy

Core idea: Introduce carefully designed noise into the computation so that the presence or absence of any single user's data does not affect the final output.

Formal definition: For any two datasets D1 and D2 that differ by exactly one record, mechanism M satisfies P(M(D1) in S) &lt;= exp(epsilon) * P(M(D2) in S). A smaller epsilon means stronger privacy protection, but greater model performance loss.

Two applications in recommendation systems:

**DP-SGD** (Adding noise during training): Each sample's gradient is first clipped to norm C, then Gaussian noise is added. The cost is 5–10× slower convergence and 5–15% performance degradation.

**Local Differential Privacy (LDP)**: Noise is added on the user's device before uploading; the server recovers statistics from the noisy data. Privacy guarantees are stronger, but more user data is needed to extract a useful signal.

Relationship with SID: SID itself is an encoding of items and does not directly involve user privacy. However, training the generative Transformer requires user behavior sequences—if DP protection is applied to behavior sequences, it will affect the quality of sequential patterns the Transformer can learn.

### 51.3 Federated Learning

Core principle: Data stays in its domain; the model goes to the data, not the data to the model.

Typical scenario: Multiple apps want to jointly train a recommendation model but cannot share user data. The approach is: a central server distributes the global model; each app trains a few steps on local data and uploads gradients (not data); the central server aggregates them (FedAvg: weighted average of gradients); then repeat.

Key challenges: Data distributions across platforms differ significantly (non-IID), making FedAvg perform poorly; model parameters are large, with per-round communication overhead at the GB level; malicious participants can upload fake gradients (poisoning attacks).

A compromise for SID scenarios: The RQ-VAE part is trained independently by each platform (item-dimension, no privacy issues); the Transformer part uses federated learning (involves user behavior), but with a shared RQ-VAE codebook—the codebook is public item semantic space knowledge and contains no user privacy.

---

## Part 52: Information Theory Deep Dive — Rate-Distortion and RQ-VAE

### 52.1 Rate-Distortion Theory

```python
# Shannon (1959) Rate-Distortion Theory:
# Given a source X and distortion measure d(x, x_hat):
# Rate-Distortion function R(D) = min_{P(x_hat|x)} I(X; X_hat)
# subject to: E[d(X, X_hat)] ≤ D

# Intuition:
# R(D) = "To compress data with distortion no more than D, how many bits are needed at minimum"
# Smaller D (higher precision requirement) → Larger R (more bits needed)

# Direct connection to RQ-VAE:
# RQ-VAE's Rate = log2(K) * L bits (4 layers with 1024-entry codebook = 40 bits)
# RQ-VAE's Distortion = reconstruction error E[||x - x_hat||^2]
# Training RQ-VAE is finding a point on the Rate-Distortion curve

# Important corollaries:
# 1. Given codebook size and number of layers, there exists a theoretical minimum reconstruction error
# 2. If the actual error is far larger than the theoretical value → training is insufficient / architecture is inadequate
# 3. Information theory provides a lower bound on performance
```

### 52.2 Mutual Information and Codebook Design

```python
# Mutual information: I(X; Z) = H(X) - H(X|Z)
# "The reduction in uncertainty about X after observing Z"

# An ideal SID should maximize:
# I(Item_features; SID) = "How much item information does the SID contain"

# Decomposed by layers:
# SID = [k1, k2, k3, k4]
# I(X; SID) = I(X; k1) + I(X; k2|k1) + I(X; k3|k1,k2) + ...
# Decreasing per layer: the first layer captures the most information, subsequent layers supplement details

# The problem of uneven information allocation:
# If I(X; k1) ≈ I(X; SID): the first layer is sufficient, subsequent layers are useless
# Symptom: residuals become very small after the first layer, subsequent layers have low utilization
# Fix: limit the first layer's codebook size / use smaller dimensions → force information to flow to subsequent layers

# Optimal codebook = roughly uniform information across layers:
# I(X; k1) ≈ I(X; k2|k1) ≈ I(X; k3|k1,k2) ≈ ...
# In practice: keep all layers with the same codebook size and dimension (default)
# Advanced: different sizes per layer (first layer larger, subsequent layers smaller) — potentially better
```

### 52.3 Optimal Transport and Sinkhorn

```python
# Optimal Transport problem:
# Given two distributions P (input data distribution) and Q (codeword distribution)
# Find the optimal mapping T that minimizes total transport cost:
# min_T E[cost(x, T(x))]  s.t. T#P = Q

# In RQ-VAE:
# P = distribution of encoder outputs
# Q = uniform distribution (we want each codeword to be used equally)
# cost = Euclidean distance
# → Optimal transport assigns the same number of data points to each codeword

# Sinkhorn algorithm is an efficient approximation of optimal transport:
# Through alternating row and column normalization, iteratively solves the entropy-regularized optimal transport problem
# min_P <C, P> + ε * H(P)  (H = negative entropy regularization term)
# Large ε: smoother but deviates from true optimal transport
# Small ε: more precise but numerically unstable

# Relationship to K-Means:
# K-Means: each point assigned to nearest center → no balance guarantee
# Sinkhorn K-Means: each center must have N/K points → enforced balance
# RQ-VAE with Sinkhorn: codeword assignment at each layer is balanced

# Continuous relaxation:
# Hard assignment: each point → one codeword (argmin)
# Soft assignment: each point → probability distribution over all codewords (softmin)
# Sinkhorn iterates on soft assignments → after convergence take argmax → hard assignment
```

### 52.4 Information Bottleneck

```python
# Tishby (1999) Information Bottleneck Theory:
# Find an intermediate representation Z that maximizes information about prediction target Y while minimizing information about input X
# max I(Z; Y) - β * I(Z; X)
# Z = compressed representation (SID)
# X = original features (item embedding)
# Y = downstream task (user preference / relevance)

# Applied to SID:
# A good SID should:
# 1. Retain information useful for recommendation → large I(SID; Y) (Y = whether user likes it)
# 2. Compress away useless information → small I(SID; X) (no need to remember every pixel)

# Meaning of β:
# Large β: more compression, SID retains only core semantics (but may lose details)
# Small β: more fidelity, SID retains more original information (but may contain noise)

# RQ-VAE's commitment loss is an implicit control on I(Z; X):
# Small commitment loss → z_e far from z_q → coarser quantization → more compression
# Large commitment loss → z_e close to z_q → finer quantization → more retention

# End-to-end SID training can explicitly optimize the information bottleneck:
# Loss = -I(SID; user_preference) + β * I(SID; raw_features)
# = downstream_loss + β * reconstruction_loss
# This is why end-to-end is theoretically better: it directly optimizes "information useful for recommendation"
```

---

## Part 53: Graph Neural Networks and Recommendation

### 53.1 Basic Concepts of Graphs

```python
# Recommendation systems are naturally a graph:
# Nodes: users + items
# Edges: user-item interactions (clicks/purchases/ratings)

# Homogeneous graph: only one type of node and edge
# Heterogeneous graph: multiple node and edge types
# Recommendation graphs are heterogeneous:
# - Nodes: User, Item, Brand, Category, ...
# - Edges: click, purchase, browse, belong_to, ...

# Adjacency matrix A:
# A[i][j] = 1 if user_i interacted with item_j
# Sparse matrix (mostly 0s)
# Number of users M, number of items N → A ∈ R^{M×N}

# Degree matrix D:
# D[i][i] = sum(A[i]) (number of neighbors of node i)
# Normalized adjacency: A_norm = D^{-1/2} * A * D^{-1/2}
```

### 53.2 GCN (Graph Convolutional Network)

```python
# Core idea: a node's representation = aggregation of its neighbors' information

# Single-layer GCN:
# H^{l+1} = σ(A_norm @ H^l @ W^l)
# Each node's new representation = weighted average of neighbors' representations → then linear transform

# In plain language:
# "Who I am" = aggregation of "who my friends are"
# User's representation = average of representations of Items they interacted with
# Item's representation = average of representations of Users who interacted with it

# Multi-layer GCN = multi-hop aggregation:
# 1 layer: look at direct neighbors (user → directly clicked items)
# 2 layers: look at 2-hop neighbors (user → item → other users who clicked that item)
# 3 layers: look at 3-hop neighbors (user → item → user → item)
# → Captures collaborative filtering signals (similar users like similar items)

# Over-Smoothing problem:
# Too many layers → all node representations converge → loss of discriminability
# In recommendation, typically only 2-3 layers are used
```

### 53.3 LightGCN (GCN Designed for Recommendation)

```python
# SIGIR 2020: Simplified GCN, specifically designed for recommendation

# Key simplifications:
# 1. Remove nonlinear activation (σ)
# 2. Remove linear transformation (W)
# Only keep: neighbor aggregation (message passing)

# Single layer:
# e_u^{l+1} = sum(1/sqrt(|N_u|*|N_i|) * e_i^l)  for i in N_u
# e_i^{l+1} = sum(1/sqrt(|N_i|*|N_u|) * e_u^l)  for u in N_i

# Final representation: average of representations across all layers
# e_u = mean(e_u^0, e_u^1, e_u^2, ..., e_u^L)

# Why does simplification actually work better?
# Recommendation graphs are very sparse → complex transforms easily overfit
# Collaborative signals mainly come from graph structure → message passing is sufficient
# Linear aggregation actually generalizes better

# Training:
# BPR Loss: positive sample score > negative sample score
# L = -log(sigmoid(e_u @ e_pos - e_u @ e_neg))
```

### 53.4 PinSage (Industrial-Scale Graph Recommendation)

PinSage, published by Pinterest in 2018, was the first large-scale industrial GNN. It introduced four key innovations to address industrial-scale challenges:

**Random Walk Sampling**: Full-graph message passing at O(N^2) is infeasible. Instead, random walks start from each node, and only neighbors with high visitation frequency are aggregated. This achieves linear complexity and automatically selects important neighbors.

**Hard Negative Mining**: Random negatives are too easy to distinguish and don't yield useful learning signal. Instead, nodes within the same cluster but not connected are used as hard negatives, enabling finer-grained discrimination.

**Mini-batch Training**: The full graph doesn't fit in GPU memory, so subgraphs are sampled and each subgraph is trained independently.

**Producer-Consumer Architecture**: Sampling and computation cannot be serial. CPUs perform sampling and push results into a queue; GPUs pull batches from the queue for training.

The item embeddings produced by PinSage can serve as input to RQ-VAE. Graph embeddings encode collaborative signals (who co-interacted with whom), and when combined with multimodal embeddings, the two sources of information complement each other—SIDs simultaneously encode content similarity (multimodal) and behavioral similarity (graph).

### 53.5 Combining Graph Learning with SID

There are three main ways to combine graph learning with SID:

**Approach 1: Graph embeddings as RQ-VAE input features.** Node embeddings produced by graph neural networks are concatenated with multimodal embeddings and fed into RQ-VAE. The resulting SIDs simultaneously encode semantic similarity and behavioral similarity. This approach is simple to implement, but graph embeddings need to be periodically recomputed.

**Approach 2: Building a graph in SID space.** With items as nodes (represented by SIDs) and behavioral co-occurrence as edges, GCN is run on the SID graph to produce enhanced SID representations. Collaborative signals act directly in SID space, but this requires a dual training pipeline and has high engineering complexity.

**Approach 3: Incorporating graph information during generation.** When the Transformer autoregressively generates SIDs, in addition to attending to the user's historical sequence, it also cross-attends to the user's social graph or interest graph. This approach can leverage high-order connectivity information from the graph, but requires real-time graph queries during inference, adding online latency.

---

## Part 54: Online Learning and Incremental Updates

### 54.1 Why Online Learning Is Needed

Recommendation systems face four dimensions of timeliness challenges: user interest drift (short-term driven by context, long-term driven by lifecycle), content timeliness (news and trending topics have effective windows of only hours to days), item availability changes (e-commerce sees hundreds of thousands of item status changes daily), and environmental shifts (holidays, promotions, weather, etc. causing overall behavioral pattern drift).

Offline training cannot address these challenges. T+1 updates mean today's data can only be used tomorrow, incurring at least 24 hours of delay; new items must wait until the next training cycle to be recommended (cold-start delay); trending events have already cooled off by the time the model learns about them. The goal of online learning is to enable models to learn from new data in real time, achieving minute-level responsiveness.

### 54.2 FTRL (Follow The Regularized Leader)

```python
# Google 2013: The industrial standard algorithm for online learning

# Core idea:
# Not gradient descent (SGD oscillates easily on streaming data)
# Instead: at each step, choose parameters optimal in the direction of the "cumulative sum of all historical gradients"
# Plus L1 regularization → automatically produces sparse solutions (many weights become 0)

# Why it's suitable for online recommendation:
# 1. Extremely high feature dimensionality (millions of cross features) → L1 sparsity is very valuable
# 2. Data arrives in a stream → each sample is processed once and discarded
# 3. Fast response to new features → weights for new item features can be learned immediately

# Update rule (simplified):
# w_t = argmin_w (g_{1:t} · w + λ1 * ||w||_1 + λ2/2 * ||w||_2^2)
# where g_{1:t} = cumulative sum of historical gradients

# Key hyperparameters:
# α: learning rate decay parameter
# β: learning rate stability parameter
# λ1: L1 regularization strength (sparsity)
# λ2: L2 regularization strength (prevent overfitting)

# Applications in recommendation systems:
# Online training of CTR models (LR/FM): 99% of companies use FTRL
# Online training of DeepFM: bottom FM layer uses FTRL, upper DNN uses Adam
# Real-time updates: aggregate a batch of samples every minute → FTRL update
```

### 54.3 Incremental Training vs. Full Training

Model update strategies for recommendation can be divided into three paradigms:

| Strategy | Approach | Pros | Cons | Applicable Components |
|------|------|------|------|----------|
| Full Training | Train from scratch daily using all historical data | Highest model quality, no catastrophic forgetting | Long training time (hours), cannot respond in real time | RQ-VAE codebook, Transformer backbone |
| Incremental Training | Fine-tune on new data only, starting from previous day's model | Fast training (minutes), quick response to new patterns | Potential catastrophic forgetting / error accumulation | CTR model embeddings, user tower |
| Real-time Update | Online update per sample or mini-batch | Second/minute-level response | Unstable, easily misled by anomalous data | Bidding models, exploration strategy parameters |

Recommended update frequencies for each component in an SID system: RQ-VAE uses weekly full training (the codebook must not change frequently); the Transformer uses daily incremental plus weekly full training; the SID mapping table is updated daily (new items onboarded); downstream CTR models use minute-level incremental updates (FTRL/Adam online).

### 54.4 Catastrophic Forgetting

The core risk of incremental training is that the model "forgets" old knowledge. In recommendation scenarios this manifests as: degraded understanding of old items after continuous fine-tuning, and in extreme cases the model only remembers patterns from the most recent day while forgetting long-term regularities.

Four main solutions:

**Experience Replay**: During training, mix new data with samples randomly drawn from old data, with a typical ratio of 7:3 or 8:2 new-to-old. Engineering-wise, maintaining a circular-overwrite replay buffer is sufficient.

**EWC (Elastic Weight Consolidation)**: Uses the Fisher information matrix to estimate the importance of each parameter and applies an L2 penalty on important parameters to prevent them from drifting too far. The downside is that it requires storing the Fisher matrix, making engineering complex.

**Progressive Networks**: A new parameter column is added for each time period, and old columns are completely frozen. The advantage is absolutely no forgetting; the downside is that the model keeps growing.

**Periodic Full Training**: Incremental training for 6 days plus one full training session per week. The simplest and most reliable approach, and the most commonly used hybrid scheme in industry.

---

## Part 55: A Deeper Dive into Exploration vs. Exploitation

### 55.1 The Exploration Problem in Recommendation Systems

```python
# Pure Exploitation: only recommend items the model is most confident about
# Problems:
# 1. Filter Bubble: users get trapped in known preferences, unable to discover new interests
# 2. Matthew Effect: head items get recommended more and more, long-tail items never get a chance
# 3. Model degradation: no diverse data → model becomes more biased → vicious cycle

# Pure Exploration: recommend randomly
# Problem: terrible user experience, most recommendations are irrelevant

# Goal: find a balance between the two
# Sacrifice a bit of CTR in the short term → in exchange for long-term diversity and discovery
```

### 55.2 Thompson Sampling in Recommendations

```python
# Original Thompson Sampling (Bernoulli):
# Each arm maintains Beta(alpha, beta)
# alpha = number of successes, beta = number of failures
# Sample → pick the largest

# Neural Thompson Sampling in recommendation systems:
# CTR model output: P(click | user, item) = 0.3
# But this is a point estimate with no uncertainty information
# Neural TS: estimate both mean and variance
#   mean = 0.3, var = 0.05 (high uncertainty → explore more)
#   mean = 0.3, var = 0.001 (very certain → just exploit)

# Implementation:
# 1. MC Dropout: keep dropout at inference → multiple forward passes → take variance
# 2. Ensemble: train multiple models → prediction variance = disagreement among models
# 3. Explicit uncertainty: output (mu, sigma) → sample from N(mu, sigma)

# Integration with SID:
# Beam Search score = log_prob + exploration_score
# exploration_score ∝ uncertainty (low-impression items have high uncertainty)
# → naturally explores new items / long-tail items
```

### 55.3 Exploration Strategies Unique to SID

```python
# SID's Beam Search inherently has exploration properties:

# 1. Code-word-level exploration:
# The first layer k1 determines the "general direction" (category level)
# Add randomness to the selection of k1:
# - Don't always pick top-1; instead sample from top-5 by probability
# - Equivalent to exploring at the category level
# Effect: occasionally explore categories the user hasn't seen

# 2. Layer-wise differentiated exploration:
# Shallow layers (general direction): conservative, pick top-3 by probability
# Deep layers (specific items): aggressive, uniform sampling
# Reason: wrong direction is costly, but swapping an item within the same direction is cheap

# 3. Missing path completion:
# If a certain SID prefix has never been generated (cold path)
# Proactively inject these prefixes into the Beam
# Ensure long-tail items have a chance of being generated

# 4. Temperature annealing:
# Temperature varies over time:
# New users: T=1.5 (more exploration, discover interests)
# Established users: T=0.7 (more exploitation, precise recommendations)
# Declining activity: temporarily raise temperature (attempt re-engagement)
```

---

## Part 56: Recommendation Diversity and Fairness

### 56.1 Why Diversity Matters

Consider a scenario: the model predicts the user likes sneakers best (CTR=5%). If you recommend 10 pairs of sneakers, total CTR is high but the user fatigues; if you recommend 3 pairs of sneakers + 3 sports gear items + 2 pairs of casual shoes + 2 items from new categories, total CTR is slightly lower but user satisfaction is higher—the user can discover new interests and won't get bored.

Diversity has multiple dimensions: category diversity (how many different categories the recommendation list covers), content diversity (embedding distance between recommended items), supplier diversity (how many different creators or merchants are recommended), temporal diversity (not just recent trending content but also classic older content), and price diversity (not just items at the same price point but a mix of high and low).

### 56.2 MMR (Maximal Marginal Relevance)

```python
# The most classic diversity algorithm (1998):

# Each time, select one item from the candidate pool to add to the result list:
# score(item) = λ * relevance(item) - (1-λ) * max_similarity_to_selected
# Pick the one with the highest score

# λ=1: pure relevance (greedy, no diversity)
# λ=0: pure diversity (pick the item most dissimilar to already selected ones)
# λ=0.5-0.7: balance point

# Implementation:
def mmr_select(candidates, selected, relevance_scores, embeddings, lambda_param=0.6):
    best_score = -float('inf')
    best_item = None
    for item in candidates:
        rel = relevance_scores[item]
        if selected:
            max_sim = max(cosine_sim(embeddings[item], embeddings[s]) for s in selected)
        else:
            max_sim = 0
        score = lambda_param * rel - (1 - lambda_param) * max_sim
        if score > best_score:
            best_score = score
            best_item = item
    return best_item

# Time complexity: O(K * N) (K = result size, N = candidate size)
# In industry, MMR re-rank is usually done on the ranking top-100

# Integration with SID:
# SID prefixes naturally encode category information
# Diversity constraint: items with the same prefix (same category) in the result list ≤ 3
# No need for extra embedding distance computation, just check whether SID prefixes repeat
```

### 56.3 DPP (Determinantal Point Process)

```python
# A more elegant diversity model than MMR:

# DPP definition:
# Given N items, the probability of selecting subset S:
# P(S) ∝ det(L_S)  (L_S = submatrix of kernel matrix L)
# L[i][j] = relevance_i * similarity(i,j) * relevance_j

# Intuition:
# det measures the "volume of a set of vectors"
# Large volume = vectors are mutually orthogonal = diverse
# But L also contains relevance → relevant AND diverse

# Advantages vs MMR:
# MMR: greedy selection, later choices are influenced by earlier ones (order-sensitive)
# DPP: globally optimal subset selection (order-independent)

# Industrial challenges:
# det computation is O(N^3) → infeasible when there are too many candidates
# Approximations: Fast DPP (O(N*K^2)), Greedy DPP, Sampling DPP

# In an SID system:
# Beam Search outputs top-200 candidates
# Use DPP to select 50 "relevant and diverse" final candidates from them
# Kernel matrix: L[i][j] = score_i * sim_SID(i,j) * score_j
# sim_SID: prefix overlap of two SIDs (low-cost computation)
```

### 56.4 Fairness

Recommendation fairness involves three levels:

**User-side fairness**: The quality of recommendations received by different user groups varies too much. For example, the recommendation accuracy for niche-interest users is far lower than for mainstream users. Remedies include upweighting niche groups during training and ensuring the NDCG gap across groups does not exceed a threshold.

**Supply-side fairness**: Top creators and merchants capture the vast majority of traffic, and new merchants get virtually zero impressions after onboarding. Remedies include setting impression quotas, long-tail boosting mechanisms, and newcomer protection periods.

**Content fairness**: The algorithm favors certain types of content (especially attention-grabbing content), causing clickbait and borderline content to be over-recommended due to high CTR. Remedies include quality-score weighting, content review filtering, and taste guidance.

SID makes a positive contribution to fairness: cold-start items obtain semantic representations through SID and no longer start from zero; Beam Search can generate long-tail items that have never been recommended; category quotas can be controlled via SID prefixes. However, SID may also introduce new fairness issues—RQ-VAE represents popular items more accurately (because of more training data), and the SIDs of long-tail items may have insufficient discriminability, leading to a higher collision rate. Remedies include oversampling long-tail items during training or setting up dedicated codebooks for long-tail items.

---

## Part 57: State Space Models—Challengers to the Transformer

### 57.1 The Efficiency Bottleneck of Transformers

```python
# Time complexity of Self-Attention: O(N^2)
# N = sequence length

# When N is large (e.g., user history with 500+ behaviors):
# Attention Matrix: 500×500 = 250K elements
# Computed at every layer → high inference latency

# Especially in autoregressive generation for recommendations:
# Each code word generated → full attention recomputed
# Although KV Cache helps → memory usage is large

# Seeking O(N) or O(N log N) alternatives
```

### 57.2 Mamba (S4 → Mamba)

```python
# Evolution of State Space Models:
# S4 (2022) → S5 (2022) → Mamba (2023)

# Core idea of SSM:
# Use a 1D linear recurrence (a linear version of RNN) to model sequences:
# h_t = A * h_{t-1} + B * x_t  (hidden state update)
# y_t = C * h_t + D * x_t  (output)

# Mamba's innovation: Selective SSM
# A, B, C are not fixed but depend on the input:
# A_t = f(x_t), B_t = g(x_t), C_t = h(x_t)
# → The model can decide "what to remember and what to forget" based on input
# Similar to LSTM gating, but more efficient

# Efficiency:
# Training: O(N) per layer (parallel scan algorithm)
# Inference: O(1) per step (only maintains a fixed-size hidden state h)
# vs Transformer: Training O(N^2), Inference O(N) (with KV cache)

# Mamba's application in recommendations:
# Replace the Transformer backbone in SASRec
# User behavior sequence → Mamba → predict next SID

# Mamba's application in recommendations:
# Replace the Transformer backbone in SASRec
# User behavior sequence → Mamba → predict next SID

# Advantages:
# - Inference O(1) per token: faster SID code word generation at each layer
# - No KV Cache needed: saves GPU memory
# - Long sequence handling: can look at longer user histories (500+)

# Disadvantages:
# - Parallelism not as good as Transformer (slightly slower training)
# - Weaker in-context learning capability (cannot attend to specific positions)
# - Empirical results on recommendations are not yet consistent (debated in 2024-2025)

# Current status: experimental stage, a few papers claim performance close to Transformer
```

### 57.3 Linear Attention and Other Efficient Architectures

```python
# The other path: improving Attention rather than replacing it

# 1. Linear Attention (Katharopoulos 2020):
# Standard: Attention = softmax(QK^T) @ V  → O(N^2)
# Linear: Attention = φ(Q) @ (φ(K)^T @ V)  → O(N*d)
# φ = some kernel function (e.g., elu+1, random features)
# Exploits associativity of matrix multiplication to change computation order

# 2. Flash Attention (Dao 2022):
# Does not change the math of Attention
# Changes the computation method: tiled computation, fewer HBM accesses
# Result: 3-5x speedup, exact results (no approximation)
# The mainstream approach in industry

# 3. GQA (Grouped Query Attention):
# Used in LLaMA 2: number of KV heads < number of Q heads
# 8 Q heads share 1-2 KV heads → KV Cache shrinks 4-8x
# Significant improvement in inference latency and memory

# Choices in recommendation:
# Short sequences (< 200): standard Attention + Flash Attention is sufficient
# Long sequences (500+): consider Linear Attention / GQA / Mamba
# Real-time inference: Flash Attention + GQA is the current optimal combination
```

### 57.4 Future Architecture Trends

Architecture evolution in 2024–2025 has four main directions:

**Hybrid Models**: Use Mamba for long-range modeling, switching to Attention at key positions for precise aggregation. Representative works include AI21's Jamba and Zamba. The application idea in recommendation is to use Mamba for long sequences and Attention for the most recent K steps.

**Test-Time Compute**: Dynamically deciding compute budget at inference time—simple queries take the fast path, complex queries take the slow path. Representative methods include Mix-of-Depths and Early Exit. In recommendation, popular users can take the fast path while cold-start users take the slow path.

**Sparse Attention**: Only attend to relevant positions rather than all of them. Representative works include Routing Transformer and BigBird. In recommendation, you can attend only to historical behaviors within the same category.

**Impact on SID Generation**: SID generation is autoregressive (generating codewords layer by layer). The alternative is non-autoregressive (NAR)—predicting all layers at once. Autoregressive has better quality but is slow; NAR is fast but lower quality. The compromise is a two-step approach—first coarsely predict all codewords, then refine and correct.

---

## Part 58: Mathematical Derivation Special—Deriving VQ-VAE Gradients from Scratch

### 58.1 Complete Decomposition of VQ-VAE Loss

```python
# Total Loss of VQ-VAE:
# L = L_recon + α * L_VQ + β * L_commit

# L_recon = ||x - D(z_q)||^2  (reconstruction error)
# L_VQ = ||sg(z_e) - z_q||^2  (codebook update, sg=stop gradient)
# L_commit = ||z_e - sg(z_q)||^2  (encoder commitment)

# Expanding the gradient of each term w.r.t. each parameter:

# --- W.r.t. Decoder parameters θ_D ---
# dL/dθ_D = dL_recon/dθ_D
# = d/dθ_D ||x - D(z_q)||^2
# = -2(x - D(z_q)) * dD(z_q)/dθ_D
# Standard backpropagation, no special handling

# --- W.r.t. Encoder parameters θ_E ---
# Difficulty: z_q = codebook[argmin||z_e - e_k||^2]
# argmin is not differentiable → dz_q/dz_e = 0

# STE handling:
# Forward: z_q_st = z_e + (z_q - z_e).detach()
# Backward: dL/dz_e ≈ dL/dz_q_st = dL_recon/dz_q_st + β * dL_commit/dz_e
# = dL_recon/dz_q (STE: pretend z_q = z_e) + 2β * (z_e - sg(z_q))

# Complete gradient:
# dL/dθ_E = dL_recon/dz_q * dz_e/dθ_E + 2β * (z_e - z_q) * dz_e/dθ_E
#           ^-- reconstruction gradient passed through via STE     ^-- gradient from commitment loss
```

### 58.2 Mathematical Derivation of EMA Updates

```python
# EMA (Exponential Moving Average) replaces gradient descent for L_VQ:

# Standard approach (gradient descent to update codebook entries):
# e_k -= lr * dL_VQ/de_k = lr * 2(e_k - mean(z_e assigned to k))
# Equivalent to: moving the codebook entry toward the mean of encoder outputs assigned to it

# EMA approach (more stable):
# Maintain two quantities:
# N_k = count of assignments to codebook entry k (EMA smoothed)
# m_k = sum of z_e assigned to codebook entry k (EMA smoothed)
# Update:
# N_k ← γ * N_k + (1-γ) * n_k  (n_k = assignment count in current batch)
# m_k ← γ * m_k + (1-γ) * sum(z_e assigned to k)
# e_k = m_k / N_k  (codebook entry = weighted average)

# γ = 0.999 (typical value)
# Meaning: codebook entry = exponentially weighted mean of z_e assigned to it over ~1000 recent batches

# Why it's better than gradient descent:
# 1. No need to manually tune lr_codebook
# 2. Smoother, no sudden jumps
# 3. Naturally accounts for assignment frequency (N_k)
# 4. No issue of matching codebook learning rate with encoder learning rate

# Mathematical equivalence of EMA:
# When γ→1 and data is stationary, EMA update is equivalent to:
# Gradient descent on L_VQ = ||sg(z_e) - e_k||^2
# with learning rate ≈ (1-γ) / N_k
# Automatically uses small lr for high-frequency entries (stable), large lr for low-frequency entries (catch up)
```

### 58.3 Recurrence Formulas for Residual Quantization

```python
# Layer-by-layer quantization in RQ:
# r_0 = z_e  (original encoder output)
# k_l = argmin_k ||r_{l-1} - e_k^{(l)}||^2  (find nearest codebook entry at layer l)
# z_q^{(l)} = e_{k_l}^{(l)}  (quantized value at layer l)
# r_l = r_{l-1} - z_q^{(l)}  (update residual)

# Reconstruction:
# z_hat = sum_{l=1}^{L} z_q^{(l)} = z_q^{(1)} + z_q^{(2)} + ... + z_q^{(L)}

# Reconstruction error:
# ||z_e - z_hat||^2 = ||r_L||^2  (residual norm of the last layer)

# Reconstruction error decreases across layers:
# ||r_0||^2 ≥ ||r_1||^2 ≥ ||r_2||^2 ≥ ... ≥ ||r_L||^2
# Proof: ||r_l||^2 = ||r_{l-1} - z_q^{(l)}||^2
#        ≤ ||r_{l-1} - 0||^2 = ||r_{l-1}||^2
#        (because z_q^{(l)} is the nearest codebook entry, closer than the zero vector)

# STE application in RQ:
# STE is applied independently at each layer:
# Gradient of r_0 = dL/dr_L * (dr_L/dr_{L-1} * ... * dr_1/dr_0)
# where dr_l/dr_{l-1} ≈ I (STE, ignoring argmin)
# → dL/dr_0 ≈ dL/dr_L (gradient passes directly back to the first layer!)
# Therefore RQ's gradient propagation is very direct, with no layer-by-layer decay
```

### 58.4 Convergence of Sinkhorn Iterations

```python
# The Sinkhorn algorithm solves:
# min_P <C, P> + ε * KL(P || r⊗c)
# where:
# C = distance matrix (batch × K)
# P = assignment matrix (row sums = 1/batch, column sums = 1/K)
# ε = entropy regularization coefficient

# Iteration process:
# K = exp(-C / ε)  # kernel matrix
# repeat:
#   u = 1 / (K @ v)  # row normalization
#   v = 1 / (K^T @ u)  # column normalization
# P = diag(u) @ K @ diag(v)

# Convergence theorem (Sinkhorn 1964):
# For any positive matrix K > 0:
# Sinkhorn iterations converge linearly to the unique doubly stochastic matrix scaling
# Convergence rate: ||P_t - P*|| ≤ ρ^t * ||P_0 - P*||
# where ρ = (σ_2/σ_1)^2 < 1 (ratio of singular values of K)

# In practice:
# 3-5 iterations are good enough (no need for exact convergence)
# Choosing ε:
# ε too large → fast convergence but deviates from true optimal transport → overly uniform
# ε too small → slow convergence and numerically unstable (exp overflow)
# Recommended: ε = 0.01-0.1 * avg(C)

# Relationship with temperature:
# Sinkhorn's ε ≈ temperature in VQ assignment
# temperature = ε: high temperature = soft balancing, low temperature = hard balancing
```

## Part 59: Complete Code Walkthrough—RQ-VAE Training Script

### 59.1 Data Loading

```python
import torch
from torch.utils.data import Dataset, DataLoader

class ItemEmbeddingDataset(Dataset):
    """Dataset of pre-computed item embeddings"""
    def __init__(self, embedding_path, normalize=True):
        # Load pre-computed embeddings (generated by CLIP/BERT etc.)
        self.embeddings = torch.load(embedding_path)  # [N, d]
        if normalize:
            # L2 normalization: map all embeddings onto the unit hypersphere
            norms = self.embeddings.norm(dim=1, keepdim=True)
            self.embeddings = self.embeddings / norms.clamp(min=1e-8)

    def __len__(self):
        return len(self.embeddings)

    def __getitem__(self, idx):
        return self.embeddings[idx]

# Data loading:
dataset = ItemEmbeddingDataset("item_embeddings.pt")
dataloader = DataLoader(
    dataset,
    batch_size=2048,  # Sinkhorn requires large batches
    shuffle=True,
    num_workers=4,
    pin_memory=True,
    drop_last=True,  # Ensure consistent batch size (required by Sinkhorn)
)
```

### 59.2 Model Definition

```python
import torch.nn as nn
import torch.nn.functional as F

class ResidualQuantizer(nn.Module):
    """Single-layer residual quantizer"""
    def __init__(self, d_model, n_codes, ema_decay=0.999):
        super().__init__()
        self.n_codes = n_codes
        self.d_model = d_model
        self.ema_decay = ema_decay

        # Codebook (not involved in gradients, updated via EMA)
        self.register_buffer("codebook", torch.randn(n_codes, d_model))
        self.register_buffer("ema_count", torch.zeros(n_codes))
        self.register_buffer("ema_sum", torch.zeros(n_codes, d_model))
        # Initialization: sampling from the data distribution would be better; using random here for now

    def quantize(self, z, use_sinkhorn=True, sinkhorn_iters=3, temperature=0.1):
        # z: [B, d]
        # Compute distance matrix
        distances = torch.cdist(z, self.codebook)  # [B, K]

        if use_sinkhorn and self.training:
            # Sinkhorn balanced assignment
            Q = torch.exp(-distances / temperature)  # [B, K]
            for _ in range(sinkhorn_iters):
                Q = Q / Q.sum(dim=0, keepdim=True)  # Column normalization
                Q = Q / Q.sum(dim=1, keepdim=True)  # Row normalization
            indices = Q.argmax(dim=1)  # [B]
        else:
            # Standard argmin
            indices = distances.argmin(dim=1)  # [B]

        z_q = self.codebook[indices]  # [B, d]
        return z_q, indices

    def ema_update(self, z, indices):
        """EMA update of the codebook (training only)"""
        if not self.training:
            return

        # Count how many vectors are assigned to each codeword
        one_hot = F.one_hot(indices, self.n_codes).float()  # [B, K]
        count = one_hot.sum(dim=0)  # [K]
        sum_z = one_hot.T @ z  # [K, d]

        # EMA smoothing
        self.ema_count = self.ema_decay * self.ema_count + (1 - self.ema_decay) * count
        self.ema_sum = self.ema_decay * self.ema_sum + (1 - self.ema_decay) * sum_z

        # Update codebook = weighted mean
        n = self.ema_count.clamp(min=1e-5)
        self.codebook.data = self.ema_sum / n.unsqueeze(1)

        # Dead codeword detection and reset
        dead_mask = self.ema_count < 1.0  # Not assigned for a long time
        if dead_mask.any():
            # Replace dead codewords by randomly sampling from the current batch
            alive_indices = torch.where(~dead_mask)[0]
            dead_indices = torch.where(dead_mask)[0]
            n_dead = dead_indices.shape[0]
            random_z = z[torch.randint(0, z.shape[0], (n_dead,))]
            self.codebook.data[dead_indices] = random_z
            self.ema_count[dead_indices] = 1.0
            self.ema_sum[dead_indices] = random_z

    def forward(self, z):
        z_q, indices = self.quantize(z)
        self.ema_update(z, indices)

        # STE: forward uses z_q, backward passes gradients to z
        z_q_st = z + (z_q - z).detach()

        # Commitment loss
        commit_loss = F.mse_loss(z, z_q.detach())

        return z_q_st, indices, commit_loss


class RQVAE(nn.Module):
    """Complete RQ-VAE"""
    def __init__(self, input_dim, hidden_dim, code_dim, n_codes, n_layers):
        super().__init__()
        self.n_layers = n_layers

        # Encoder: input features → latent space
        self.encoder = nn.Sequential(
            nn.Linear(input_dim, hidden_dim),
            nn.ReLU(),
            nn.Linear(hidden_dim, hidden_dim),
            nn.ReLU(),
            nn.Linear(hidden_dim, code_dim),
        )

        # Decoder: latent space → reconstructed input
        self.decoder = nn.Sequential(
            nn.Linear(code_dim, hidden_dim),
            nn.ReLU(),
            nn.Linear(hidden_dim, hidden_dim),
            nn.ReLU(),
            nn.Linear(hidden_dim, input_dim),
        )

        # Multi-layer quantizers
        self.quantizers = nn.ModuleList([
            ResidualQuantizer(code_dim, n_codes)
            for _ in range(n_layers)
        ])

    def encode(self, x):
        return self.encoder(x)

    def decode(self, z_q):
        return self.decoder(z_q)

    def forward(self, x):
        z_e = self.encode(x)  # [B, code_dim]

        # Layer-by-layer residual quantization
        residual = z_e
        z_q_sum = torch.zeros_like(z_e)
        all_indices = []
        total_commit_loss = 0.0

        for i, quantizer in enumerate(self.quantizers):
            z_q_layer, indices, commit_loss = quantizer(residual)
            z_q_sum = z_q_sum + z_q_layer
            residual = residual - z_q_layer.detach()  # Next layer quantizes the residual
            all_indices.append(indices)
            total_commit_loss += commit_loss

        # Reconstruction
        x_recon = self.decode(z_q_sum)

        # Reconstruction loss
        recon_loss = F.mse_loss(x_recon, x)

        # Total loss
        loss = recon_loss + 0.25 * total_commit_loss

        return {
            "loss": loss,
            "recon_loss": recon_loss,
            "commit_loss": total_commit_loss,
            "indices": torch.stack(all_indices, dim=1),  # [B, L]
            "x_recon": x_recon,
        }
```

### 59.3 Training Loop

```python
def train_rqvae(model, dataloader, n_epochs=50, lr=1e-3, device="cuda"):
    model = model.to(device)
    optimizer = torch.optim.Adam(model.parameters(), lr=lr)
    scheduler = torch.optim.lr_scheduler.CosineAnnealingLR(optimizer, T_max=n_epochs)

    for epoch in range(n_epochs):
        model.train()
        total_loss = 0
        total_recon = 0
        total_commit = 0

        for batch in dataloader:
            batch = batch.to(device)
            output = model(batch)

            optimizer.zero_grad()
            output["loss"].backward()
            torch.nn.utils.clip_grad_norm_(model.parameters(), 1.0)
            optimizer.step()

            total_loss += output["loss"].item()
            total_recon += output["recon_loss"].item()
            total_commit += output["commit_loss"].item()

        scheduler.step()
        n_batches = len(dataloader)

        # Evaluate every epoch
        if (epoch + 1) % 5 == 0:
            metrics = evaluate_rqvae(model, dataloader, device)
            print(f"Epoch {epoch+1}/{n_epochs}")
            print(f"  Loss: {total_loss/n_batches:.4f}")
            print(f"  Recon: {total_recon/n_batches:.4f}")
            print(f"  Commit: {total_commit/n_batches:.4f}")
            print(f"  Utilization: {metrics['utilization']:.2%}")
            print(f"  Collision Rate: {metrics['collision_rate']:.2%}")


@torch.no_grad()
def evaluate_rqvae(model, dataloader, device):
    model.eval()
    all_indices = []

    for batch in dataloader:
        batch = batch.to(device)
        output = model(batch)
        all_indices.append(output["indices"].cpu())

    all_indices = torch.cat(all_indices, dim=0)  # [N, L]

    # Codebook utilization: how many distinct codewords are used per layer
    utilizations = []
    for layer in range(all_indices.shape[1]):
        unique = all_indices[:, layer].unique().shape[0]
        total = model.quantizers[layer].n_codes
        utilizations.append(unique / total)

    # Collision rate: how many different items map to the same SID
    sids = [tuple(row.tolist()) for row in all_indices]
    n_unique_sids = len(set(sids))
    collision_rate = 1 - n_unique_sids / len(sids)

    return {
        "utilization": sum(utilizations) / len(utilizations),
        "collision_rate": collision_rate,
        "per_layer_util": utilizations,
    }
```

### 59.4 SID Assignment and Export

```python
@torch.no_grad()
def assign_sids(model, all_embeddings, device, batch_size=4096):
    """Assign SIDs to all items"""
    model.eval()
    all_sids = []

    for i in range(0, len(all_embeddings), batch_size):
        batch = all_embeddings[i:i+batch_size].to(device)
        z_e = model.encode(batch)

        # Layer-by-layer quantization (at inference time use argmin, not Sinkhorn)
        residual = z_e
        layer_indices = []
        for quantizer in model.quantizers:
            z_q, indices = quantizer.quantize(residual, use_sinkhorn=False)
            residual = residual - z_q
            layer_indices.append(indices)

        sids = torch.stack(layer_indices, dim=1)  # [B, L]
        all_sids.append(sids.cpu())

    return torch.cat(all_sids, dim=0)  # [N, L]


def export_sid_table(item_ids, sids, output_path):
    """Export the SID mapping table"""
    sid_table = {}
    reverse_index = {}  # SID → item_ids

    for item_id, sid in zip(item_ids, sids.tolist()):
        sid_tuple = tuple(sid)
        sid_table[item_id] = sid_tuple

        if sid_tuple not in reverse_index:
            reverse_index[sid_tuple] = []
        reverse_index[sid_tuple].append(item_id)

    # Check collisions
    collisions = {k: v for k, v in reverse_index.items() if len(v) > 1}
    print(f"Total items: {len(item_ids)}")
    print(f"Unique SIDs: {len(reverse_index)}")
    print(f"Collisions: {len(collisions)} SIDs have multiple items")

    torch.save({"sid_table": sid_table, "reverse_index": reverse_index}, output_path)
    return sid_table, reverse_index
```

## Part 60: Numerical Example — Manual Step-by-Step RQ-VAE Computation

### 60.1 Setup

```python
# Minimal example: d=4, K=4 (4 codewords per layer), L=2 (two layers)

# Original item embedding (encoder output):
z_e = [0.5, -0.3, 0.8, 0.1]

# First-layer codebook (4 codewords, each 4-dimensional):
codebook_1 = [
    [0.4, -0.2, 0.9, 0.0],  # codeword 0
    [-0.5, 0.6, -0.1, 0.3], # codeword 1
    [0.8, 0.1, 0.2, -0.4],  # codeword 2
    [0.1, -0.7, 0.5, 0.6],  # codeword 3
]

# Second-layer codebook (4 codewords, each 4-dimensional):
codebook_2 = [
    [0.1, -0.1, 0.0, 0.1],   # codeword 0
    [-0.1, 0.0, -0.1, 0.0],  # codeword 1
    [0.0, -0.1, -0.1, 0.1],  # codeword 2
    [0.1, 0.1, 0.0, -0.1],   # codeword 3
]
```

### 60.2 First-Layer Quantization

```python
# Compute the distance from z_e to each codeword:
# dist(z_e, codebook_1[0]) = sqrt((0.5-0.4)^2 + (-0.3-(-0.2))^2 + (0.8-0.9)^2 + (0.1-0)^2)
#                           = sqrt(0.01 + 0.01 + 0.01 + 0.01) = sqrt(0.04) = 0.2

# dist(z_e, codebook_1[1]) = sqrt((0.5-(-0.5))^2 + (-0.3-0.6)^2 + (0.8-(-0.1))^2 + (0.1-0.3)^2)
#                           = sqrt(1.0 + 0.81 + 0.81 + 0.04) = sqrt(2.66) ≈ 1.63

# dist(z_e, codebook_1[2]) = sqrt((0.5-0.8)^2 + (-0.3-0.1)^2 + (0.8-0.2)^2 + (0.1-(-0.4))^2)
#                           = sqrt(0.09 + 0.16 + 0.36 + 0.25) = sqrt(0.86) ≈ 0.93

# dist(z_e, codebook_1[3]) = sqrt((0.5-0.1)^2 + (-0.3-(-0.7))^2 + (0.8-0.5)^2 + (0.1-0.6)^2)
#                           = sqrt(0.16 + 0.16 + 0.09 + 0.25) = sqrt(0.66) ≈ 0.81

# The nearest is codeword 0 (distance 0.2)
# k1 = 0
# z_q1 = [0.4, -0.2, 0.9, 0.0]
```

### 60.3 Residual Computation and Second-Layer Quantization

```python
# Residual = z_e - z_q1:
# r1 = [0.5-0.4, -0.3-(-0.2), 0.8-0.9, 0.1-0.0]
#    = [0.1, -0.1, -0.1, 0.1]

# Layer 2: find the nearest codeword to r1 in codebook_2:
# dist(r1, codebook_2[0]) = sqrt((0.1-0.1)^2 + (-0.1-(-0.1))^2 + (-0.1-0)^2 + (0.1-0.1)^2)
#                         = sqrt(0 + 0 + 0.01 + 0) = 0.1

# dist(r1, codebook_2[1]) = sqrt((0.1-(-0.1))^2 + (-0.1-0)^2 + (-0.1-(-0.1))^2 + (0.1-0)^2)
#                         = sqrt(0.04 + 0.01 + 0 + 0.01) = sqrt(0.06) ≈ 0.245

# dist(r1, codebook_2[2]) = sqrt((0.1-0)^2 + (-0.1-(-0.1))^2 + (-0.1-(-0.1))^2 + (0.1-0.1)^2)
#                         = sqrt(0.01 + 0 + 0 + 0) = 0.1

# dist(r1, codebook_2[3]) = sqrt((0.1-0.1)^2 + (-0.1-0.1)^2 + (-0.1-0)^2 + (0.1-(-0.1))^2)
#                         = sqrt(0 + 0.04 + 0.01 + 0.04) = sqrt(0.09) = 0.3

# Codewords 0 and 2 have the same distance (0.1); pick the first one: k2 = 0
# z_q2 = [0.1, -0.1, 0.0, 0.1]
```

### 60.4 Final Result

```python
# Final SID = [k1, k2] = [0, 0]

# Reconstructed vector = z_q1 + z_q2:
# z_hat = [0.4+0.1, -0.2+(-0.1), 0.9+0.0, 0.0+0.1]
#       = [0.5, -0.3, 0.9, 0.1]

# Reconstruction error = ||z_e - z_hat||^2:
# = (0.5-0.5)^2 + (-0.3-(-0.3))^2 + (0.8-0.9)^2 + (0.1-0.1)^2
# = 0 + 0 + 0.01 + 0
# = 0.01  (very small!)

# Residual r2 = z_e - z_hat = [0, 0, -0.1, 0]
# ||r2||^2 = 0.01

# If we add a third layer, we can further reduce this to ~0

# Information usage:
# Original information: 4 float32 values = 128 bits
# SID compression: 2 two-bit indices = 4 bits (K=4 → log2(4)=2)
# Compression ratio: 128/4 = 32x
# But the capacity in this example is too small (4^2=16 possible SIDs);
# in practice we use 1024^4 = trillions of possibilities
```

## Part 61: Glossary

### Machine Learning Basics

| Term | English | Definition |
|------|---------|------------|
| Gradient Descent | Gradient Descent | Optimization method that updates parameters along the steepest descent direction of the loss |
| Learning Rate | Learning Rate | Step size for each parameter update |
| Overfitting | Overfitting | The model memorizes training data but generalizes poorly |
| Regularization | Regularization | Techniques that constrain model complexity to prevent overfitting |
| Loss Function | Loss Function | A function measuring the gap between model predictions and ground-truth values |
| Backpropagation | Backpropagation | Algorithm that computes gradients for each parameter via the chain rule |
| Batch Size | — | Number of samples used per parameter update |
| Epoch | — | One complete pass through the entire training set |

### Deep Learning

| Term | English | Definition |
|------|---------|------------|
| Embedding | Embedding | Dense vector representation of discrete objects (e.g., words/items) |
| Attention | Attention | Mechanism that dynamically weights and aggregates sequence elements |
| Transformer | — | Neural network architecture based on Self-Attention |
| Autoregressive | Autoregressive | Predicts the next step based on all previous outputs at each step |
| KV Cache | — | Caches previously computed Keys/Values to avoid redundant computation |
| Positional Encoding | Positional Encoding | Method for injecting positional information into Transformers |

### Vector Quantization

| Term | English | Definition |
|------|---------|------------|
| Codebook | Codebook | A set of discrete, learnable vectors (collection of codewords) |
| Codeword | Codeword | A single vector within the codebook |
| VQ-VAE | Vector Quantized VAE | Replaces the continuous latent space of a VAE with a discrete codebook |
| RQ-VAE | Residual Quantized VAE | Multi-layer residual quantization with exponentially growing capacity |
| STE | Straight-Through Estimator | Gradient trick: discrete in the forward pass, continuous in the backward pass |
| EMA | Exponential Moving Average | Method for updating the codebook via exponential moving averages |
| Commitment Loss | — | Loss that constrains encoder outputs to stay close to codewords |
| Codebook Utilization | Codebook Utilization | Proportion of codewords actually used out of the total |
| Collision Rate | Collision Rate | Proportion of different items mapped to the same SID |
| Sinkhorn | — | Optimal-transport iterative algorithm that enforces uniform assignment |
| FSQ | Finite Scalar Quantization | Codebook-free approach; each dimension is independently discretized |

### Recommendation Systems

| Term | English | Definition |
|------|---------|------------|
| CTR | Click-Through Rate | Click rate = clicks / impressions |
| CVR | Conversion Rate | Conversion rate = conversions / clicks |
| Retrieval | Retrieval | Coarse filtering of a relevant subset from the full candidate pool |
| Ranking | Ranking | Fine-grained scoring and sorting of the retrieval results |
| Cold Start | Cold Start | The dilemma of having no historical data for new users/items |
| A/B Testing | A/B Test | Controlled experiment to evaluate the effect of a strategy |
| Beam Search | Beam Search | Sequential search algorithm that retains the top-K candidates |
| ANN | Approximate Nearest Neighbor | Approximate nearest neighbor search |

### Semantic ID Specific

| Term | English | Definition |
|------|---------|------------|
| SID | Semantic ID | Semantic item identifier generated by RQ-VAE |
| Generative Retrieval | Generative Retrieval | Directly generates candidate items using an autoregressive model |
| Prefix Sharing | Prefix Sharing | Items in the same category share an SID prefix |
| Codebook Drift | Codebook Drift | Changes in SID mappings caused by codebook updates |
| Multimodal Encoding | Multimodal Encoding | Fusing multiple feature types such as image/text/behavioral signals |
